DAVERNE, JARDINIEES-MARAICUFRS A PARIZ. Travaillez, prenez de la peine, C'est le fondz qui manque le moins. LA FORTAIRE. WORK AWARDED THE GRAND GOLD MEDAL OF 1,O0OFR. BY THE ROYAL AND CENTRAL AGRICULTURAL SOCIETY. PARIS, CHEZ Mme Ve BOUCHARD-HUZARD, IMPRIMEUR, LIBRAIRE DE LA SOCIETE, rue de l'Éperon-Saint-André, n° 7. 1848. ~. DS6 COO --- --: 2~~-- ---- MAIN LIB.-AGRI. DOUEeG, GOOgle The Société royale et centrale d'agriculture de la Seine has always taken a great interest in market gardening in Paris: as early as 1810, it had opened a competition for the composition of a practical manual of this crop, in order to spread knowledge of it outside the capital, within which it is more or less concentrated; and, although this competition had remained open for ten years, no competitor presentedó. M749438 8942800G In 1842, the Royal Society renewed its appeal, through Mr. Viscount Héricart de Thury, at a general meeting presided over by the Minister of Agriculture and Trade. the Minister has now founded a prize to be awarded in 1843 to the author of the best treatise on market gardening in Paris, in accordance with the program to be drawn up by the Agricultural Society. Encouraged by a few friends, we joined the ranks. Our first entry didn't quite meet the requirements of the program, but we did win the silver medal, and were urged to enter the same competition again the following year. Flattered by this initial distinction, we reviewed our manuscript, re-submitted it to the competition, and had the honor of seeing it crowned with the grand gold medal of 1,00o francs, in a formal session on April 14, 1844. The Société royale et centrale d'agriculture did not stop at this mark, which was already very flattering for us, but decided that our work would be printed at its own expense in its Mémoires, and authorized us to print as many copies as we saw fit; these are the copies we are offering to the public today. We will now outline the difficulties we encountered in composing this work, the plan we followed and the goal we set ourselves. First of all, we would point out that market gardening has never been learned or perfected except by practice, and that no market gardener had ever written about it before us. There can be no objection to our first assertion; the second can be explained by two reasons, which we shall try to highlight. Market gardening, as it is practised in Paris, never leaves the grower any spare time if he wants to make an honourable living from it; and, when the idea comes to him to pass on his knowledge to others by means of the press, his family and his crops show him that he doesn't have enough time to sacrifice to this object. But, assuming that this first difficulty is not insurmountable, here is a second no less great. Market gardeners, generally receiving only the first elements of instruction, are frightened by the distance they suppose exists between their knowledge and that of the literate man who knows how to communicate his thoughts through the press, and they believe that it is absolutely necessary to cross this distance in order to dare to be printed. We have long been held back by this idea, and if friends had not often repeated to us: "Work, take the trouble; learn to exercise your intelligence as you know how to exercise your arms, and in a short time you will be able to explain your thoughts yourself;" if, we say, friends had not encouraged us in this way, we would have continued to keep silent as our colleagues keep silent. IX We don't claim to have achieved remarkable purity or impeccable style, but we do believe we can be read and understood. Moreover, the Royal and Central Agricultural Society having kindly informed us that it was more concerned with content than with form, we hope that the public will show the same indulgence. As for the plan of our work, it was traced out for us by the program of the Royal Agricultural Society, and we have followed it as closely as possible. This program positively called for a practical manual of market gardening as we practise it in Paris, because its procedures, aims and results, very different from those of vegetable growing, had never been published. As for the aim of our work, it was based on? several reasons, which we shall now explain. Firstly, we shared with the Royal Agricultural Society the desire that a practical manual of vegetable growing in Paris should be produced and published; for, although those who have written on vegetable growing have borrowed some of our procedures, they have never rendered them with the precision and detail that only vegetable growers know; And when it is generally agreed that this type of cultivation can only be adequately described by practising market gardeners, we may be forgiven for daring to try and put this important part of horticulture on a par with its other branches. In view of the railroads being built on all sides, in view of industry's efforts to obtain heat at the lowest possible cost, it is easy to foresee that market gardening in Paris is about to undergo changes, and we have thought it useful to describe this culture as it was practiced in Paris in 1844, so that later we can better judge and appreciate the changes it may undergo. Remembering also how long experience takes, and how many years a market gardener needs to acquire solid knowledge in his profession, we thought we'd shorten the study time for our children and young market gardeners, by leaving them the fruit of our practice and repeated experiments over many years. Finally, if our work does not live up to our expectations, if it is not judged with the indulgence that simple market gardeners are entitled to expect, we shall always have the satisfaction of having been the first to respond to the call of the Royal and Central Agricultural Society, and the honor of having earned its approval. A brief history of market gardening in Paris over the past fifty or sixty years. It would undoubtedly be desirable for us to begin this work by researching the origins of market gardening in Paris, and to follow and explain its successive progresses from its beginnings to the present day; but such research is not within our power; we have neither the time nor the means to go beyond human memory, and we cannot and do not wish to say anything other than what we have seen and done ourselves. It is generally known that, whenever the walls of Paris have been moved back, the market gardeners have been obliged to move back too, to make way for new buildings, and that this move was always onerous for them, in that they left ground that had long been improved, to establish themselves on new soil, often rebellious to their cultivation, and which could only be improved with time and great expense. Many of us will remember that, towards the end of the last century, when the perimeter wall was moved to where the octroi gates now stand, many market gardeners went to establish themselves beyond the new gates, because the price of the circumscribed land immediately increased considerably. Later, when the Saint-Martin canal was built, a fair number of market gardeners were forced to move further out to make way for the new constructions. Until 1780, market gardens could be found along the boulevard, from the Porte Saint-Antoine, now the Place de la colonne de Juillet, to near the Madeleine. The thirtieth district of Paris was then called the Pont-aux-Choux, so numerous were the marshes. Finally, until the revolution of '89, market gardeners kept the custom of calling their gardens marais; but since then, most say their garden, because these gardens no longer resemble the marshes in which the first market gardeners had established themselves, hence the name maraichers that their successors still bear. We should now look back to see who our forefathers were and what state the profession of market gardener was in their hands; but market gardeners know no chronicle other than the tradition passed down orally among them, and their memories go back no further than two or three generations. So we ourselves can't go back more than sixty years into the time that preceded the one in which we live, unless we echo what others have written, something we'll always avoid. From the testimony of our oldest colleagues, it is clear that, 60 or 80 years ago, market gardening was much less advanced than it is today; that there were fewer seasons in a year; that the art of early produce was still in its infancy; that the most skilful market gardeners still only had bells, and in small numbers, to advance their vegetables and especially to raise a type of embroidered melon, the only one they knew at the time, and which today still bears the name of market garden melon. It's not that the art of early produce and the use of stretchers were unknown in Paris at that time; both had long been making headway in royal gardens and among several great lords; But they had not yet penetrated market gardening, when in 1780, a market gardener named Fournier made the first use of frames in his cultivation with such pronounced success in obtaining early fruit much earlier than before, that a large number of his colleagues imitated him and immediately obtained great benefits. The use and maneuvering of frames became an important branch of market gardening, and it is from this period that the term forced cultivation has become familiar to us. The same Fournier who introduced chassis to market gardening in 1780 also introduced cantaloupe a few years later; he was also the first market gardener to grow potatoes. The first to introduce white asparagus was a man named Quentin, around 1792. Green asparagus began to be forced by the same man and his brother-in-law, named Marie, around 1800. The first to force cauliflower was Besnard, around 1811. The first Romaines to be forced were by Messrs Dulac and Chemin, around 1812. Fine Italian chicory began to be forced at the same time, by Baptiste Quentin. Among today's market gardeners, the Quentin brothers, Fanfan and Baptiste, along with Mr. Dulac, were the first to force-crop beans, in 1814. Forced cultivation of carrots was carried out for Gros. In 1836, M. Gontier was the first to use the thermosiphon in forced cultivation under chassis. We have just named five market gardeners of our era, because they have enriched market gardening with new processes, and recognition demands that their names be no more forgotten than those we have named before. We would also like to name those of our colleagues who have distinguished themselves by the perfection of their cultivation, the extent of their operations, their activity, their skill and their good administration, but the number would be too great; But the number would be too great; moreover, by highlighting the merit of some of our colleagues and passing over the others in silence, it would appear on our part a partiality of which we do not wish to be accused; but we will say with pleasure, with pride even, that there is in the market gardening class more ability, more intelligence than is generally believed in the other classes. This is all we can say about market gardening in earlier times, and its progress up to 1844. These are certain facts, many of which have been accomplished before our very eyes, and from which we are benefiting today. 0 CHAPTER HI. ART. 1. - Horticultural statistics. As our work is particularly intended for our children, the young market gardeners, we must give them the meaning of words that are not learned in our practice, and the word statistics is one of these. Thus, we tell them that the word statistics means the extent of a country, of a place, its climate, its various products, its trade, its flourishing or suffering population, its wealth, the money it spends and the money it earns, etc. It is therefore by enumerating these different relationships that we will give an idea of the statistics of the gardener-gardener class, currently practicing their profession in the new precinct of Paris. 1° We have found, by a very approximate calculation, that the total amount of land used for market gardening, within the new Paris enclosure, is now about 1,578 hectares. 2° This land is divided into 1,800 marshes or gardens; the largest contain about 1 hectare, and the smallest about half a hectare; but the largest number of market gardens contain three quarters of a hectare, because this amount of land is most in keeping with the good administration and supervision of a single leader. 3° From the fact that there are 1,800 market gardens within the new Paris city limits, it follows that there are also 1,800 master market gardeners to manage them. Of these masters, some are owners and others tenants; but as this difference has nothing to do with capacity or morality, we need not concern ourselves with it. 4° As for the staff of each establishment, it is rather difficult to fix it rigorously: firstly, because all gardens do not have the same size; secondly, because the operation of some offers difficulties that are not encountered in others; thirdly, because some market gardeners have a way of working, calculating and administering that the others do not, which can increase or decrease the number of hands needed for exploltation. We have to calculate the strength of the master's children, and see if the work of one, two, three or four children can be equivalent to the work of one or two men; finally, the seasons, whether contrary or favorable, decrease or increase the number of supernumerary workers. However, based on our own long experience, we can say with a fair degree of certainty that, to cultivate a 1 hectare garden, in which early and late crops are grown, a staff of five or six people is needed at all times, made up of the master and mistress, a hired girl, a hired boy if there are children fit to work, or, failing them, two hired boys and often one or two men by the day. Hired hands sleep at home and are fed at the master's table. Hired girls are paid according to their strength, intelligence and knowledge; the average wage can be set at 240 fr. per year. Boy gardeners are paid by the month, and the price is not the same in summer as in winter. From October to March, a boy earns 18 to 20 fr. per month and 2 fr. per Sunday; from April to September, he earns 30 to 32 fr. per month and 2 fr. to 2 fr. 50c. per Sunday. This staff is not always sufficient: in the long, long droughts of summer, for example, extraordinary wetting is necessary to keep the merchandise soft, prevent it from hardening or rising; in this case, the master market gardener takes on one or more men by the day, whom he feeds and to each of whom he gives 2 fr. per day. If they are women, he gives them 1 fr. plus food. There are 9,000 people employed in market gardening within the new Paris city limits.  3,600 To be carried forward ... ... . 3,600 Children, whose work is estimated at that of 1,500 men, ci. . . . . . . 1,500 Hired men and women... 3,900 TOTAL... . 9,000 It goes without saying that not all the market gardeners, not all the day laborers, know the trade equally well, and are not all endowed with the same intelligence or activity. The master is quick to recognize what each one is capable of, and employs it according to his or her faculties. Many of these market gardeners are young men from the provinces, who come to Paris to learn or improve their skills; and, whether this is true or not, it is certainly true among the market gardeners that some provinces provide better workers than others; so when a market gardener hires a boy, he makes a point of finding out which country he comes from. Up to now, the police have not obliged market gardeners to provide themselves with a livret, but their masters require them to provide proof of good conduct in lieu of a livret. In general, this class of workers is very peaceful, and very rarely gives rise to complaints. 5° What proves that market gardeners have followed the progress of well-being that has developed in the working classes is that they ocupy many more horses than in the past? the 1,800 market gardeners existing within the new Paris city limits own at least 1,700 horses, which spend, on average, each 2 fr. 50 per day on their food. Of these 1,700 horses, 1,300 are kept all year round and 400 are sold at the start of winter, when they are no longer needed to draw water. The 1,300 horses that market gardeners can't do without cost from 500 to 800 fr. to buy, or an average of 650 fr. each. These horses are used to take the vegetables to the market early in the morning, and on their way back bring back a wagonload of manure; sometimes they return to fetch several wagons in the same day; and in summer, they are kept busy for several hours a day drawing water from the well, by means of a manege or gear pump, descriptions of which we'll give later; but we'll say straight away that a manege and its accessories cost from 4 to 500 fr. As for gear pumps, there are still only a small number established in the gardens of Paris market gardeners, and their price varies according to their perfection, their complication and the depth of the wells. For a well 10 meters deep, a gear pump costs from 1,500 to 1,600 fr. but there are some that cost up to 2,600 fr. The hoo horses mentioned above are of lesser value than the others, O8 because the market-gardeners who buy them only use them for about six months in summer, and then sell them on; their price is 150 to 200 fr. or, on average, 175 fr. each. They are also used to bring vegetables to the market, collect manure and draw water. If we now calculate the cost of the 1,700 horses employed in market gardening in the new Paris precinct, we find that 1,300 horses, at 650 fr. each, add up to........................................................................... 845,000 fr. That 400 horses, at 175 fr. each, make.......... . . . ..... 70,000 fr. TOTAL. ... . 915,000 fr. If we then calculate the cost of feeding these horses at 2 fr. 50 c. per day, we find that 1,300 horses cost per year... 1,166,250 fr. That 400 horses cost, for six months of feed, more than... . 182,000 fr. TOTAL... ... . 1,368,250 fr. 6° Many circumstances give different values to land suitable for market gardening in Paris. Every day, some market gardeners move away from the center of the capital, because new buildings, factories and warehouses are constantly springing up, taking over the land and making it more expensive than the farmer can afford: it also sometimes happens that the farmer finds it profitable to sell his land and move further away, and sometimes he is obliged to move away for public utility reasons. A marsh on a street or paved road is more valuable than one at the end of an unpaved lane or subject to some kind of servitude; one that is well enclosed by walls is also more valuable than one that is poorly or not at all enclosed. However, we'd like to give you an idea of the current value of land suitable for market gardening in Paris. Twenty years ago, the land between the boulevard and the octroi wall was worth between 20 and 22,000 fr. per hectare; today, it's worthless. On the banks of the Canal Saint-Martin, and other areas where trade has expanded, a hectare sells for 80 or 100,000 fr. Those situated between the octroi wall and the new enclosure can be divided into two categories: the first are those which, long since cultivated as marshland, are reputed to be of good soil quality, enclosed by walls and close to a paved street; these are now worth 28 to 30,000 fr. The second are those located on the back, with no easy access to a paved street, which sell for 16 to 20,000 fr. per hectare enclosed by walls, including housing. There are two types of rent for a hectare of land devoted to market gardening. When the land is of the first quality, it rents for 12 to 1,300 fr. per hectare, including the gardener's lodging; when it is of the second quality, the hectare rents for 8 to 900 fr. In addition to the rent, the market gardener tenant also pays the personal tax and the doors and windows. 8° The number of frame panels used in market gardening within the new Paris city limits is estimated at... ... . The number of bells is estimated at 360,000 ... ... ... ... ... ... ... . . . ... 2,160,000 9° In a marsh that has been used for market gardening for some time, fertilizers and fertilizing materials are found in the debris of the manure used to make the beds and mulch: so, calculating the amount of manure used by our sashes and bells, the price of the fertilizer will be part of the price of the manure. On average, one panel of chassis uses up 4 fr. of manure per year; that's four times 360,000 or.... 1,440,000 fr. To keep 2,160,000 bells busy, you need manure for... ... ... . 270,000 fr. TOTAL expenditure on manure and fertilizers..... . . . . . . . . . . . . . 1,810,000 fr. 10° We are unable to give a plausible approximation of the annual amount spent on market gardening within the new Paris city limits. 11° While for several reasons it is not possible for us to give a figure for the cost of market gardening in Paris, we can give a very approximate figure for its revenue. For a long time we have been dealing with this subject for the various seasons of the year, good and bad, as well as with the products of open-ground and forced cultivation, and we can affirm that the total revenue from the sale of vegetables by market gardeners within the new Paris city limits amounts to around 13,500,000 fr. per year. ART. 2 - Horticultural economics. By the term "economy", we mean the relationship between expenditure and income, so that the latter is always greater than the former. But if it's easy to establish this ratio for a few market-gardening establishments, it's extremely difficult to establish it for all the 1,800 market-gardeners in Paris; for, although each of us believes we're doing our best, an unforeseen event can overturn all our calculations and make the expenditure greater than the revenue; and this unfortunately happens from time to time, and sometimes several years in a row; However, our position among our colleagues puts us in a position to approach the truth more closely than any other, and this is what we shall try to do in the following paragraphs. 1° To cultivate 1 hectare of land that has been converted to marshland for ten years or more, and where only field vegetables are grown, four people are enough for eight months of the year; but from May to August, two more are needed. It's clear that if you were to set up in new, uncultivated, stony ground, you'd need more people until the soil was loosened up and easy to work. As for the number of hands needed to cultivate 1 hectare of land in full-ground and early crops, we've already mentioned this, on page 7, paragraph 4. 2° Here, according to the order of the program, we need to establish how much fertilizer it costs each year to cultivate 1 hectare of land in full-ground vegetables, and 1 hectare in the most complex way, i.e. in early crops and full-ground cultivation. Since the question is twofold, let's divide it up first. As we have just said, when a market gardener sets up on new land that has not yet been cultivated as marshland, he needs a few O8 years to make the soil loose and easy to cultivate. During these first few years, the fertilizers needed to make the soil fertile can be considerable. If the soil is too sandy, it needs a fatty fertilizer, such as cow manure; if it is too strong, it needs a lighter fertilizer, such as horse manure: but it is impossible for us to say how much of either fertilizer is needed to bring the land to the state of fertility and divisibility most suitable for market gardening, or even to specify how many years it will take to reach this state. But once it has become suitable for market gardening, we can say how much fertilizer is needed per year to maintain it in its fertile state, even though some vegetables are more exhausting than others. Of course, we're talking here about a marsh where there are no early vegetables, and therefore no layers; but we can't conceive of market gardening without mulch, without soil to cover the seedlings; and, as in a marsh the same area can produce three or four harvests a year, this area must also be mulched or top-dressed three or four times in one year, which already produces a fertilizer, but insufficient to maintain fertility; it is therefore necessary for the market-gardener who does not make beds and, consequently, no early vegetables to buy, each year, from market-gardeners who make beds and early vegetables, for around O8,200 fr. per hectare of old layer manure, part of which is buried as fertilizer, and the rest used as mulch and to be converted into potting soil. However, when their soil is too light or too sandy, they buy only 100 fr. per hectare of old layer manure, which is enough to mulch their plants and seedlings, and they use the other 100 fr. to buy fat horse or cow manure, which they bury as fertilizer. In a market-gardening establishment of 1 hectare where a lot of early produce is grown, the expense is much greater; in one, for example, where 40o panels of frames and 3,000 bells are used, more than 3,000 fr. of manure is spent each year. There's an observation to be made here. It is generally believed that, if we obtain such beautiful vegetables, it is because we use a lot of fertilizer; this is a mistake. When our land is ready for market gardening, we don't use fertilizer as such: the fertility of the soil is maintained firstly by mulching and topdressing, which we renew and bury at least three times a year; secondly, because we change the position of our melon patches 2 times a year as much as we can, and the water with which we water our cucumbers passes through the manure and carries its fertilizing parts into the soil; finally, because our trench melons are grown in pits filled with manure, which imparts part of its fertility to the soil, and when we remove this manure from the pits or trenches, a more or less decomposed part always remains, which naturally fertilizes the soil and puts it in the most fertile state for market gardening: those of us who grow a lot of early vegetables sell part of our potting soil each year, and never buy fertilizers of any kind. We would perhaps like to see you discuss the cost of maintaining 1 hectare of land under open cultivation, under forced cultivation, and the profit to be made from this cultivation in one year; that we indicate the cost of producing each vegetable and the price at which it is sold, and that we draw up a table of the cost and profit of cultivating all these items. All these details would undoubtedly be necessary for good statistics, but to obtain them we would first have to ensure that expenditure and profit were always invariable, which is not the case; then we would have to delve into the secrets of our colleagues, which we will never allow ourselves to do. Land and everything related to it. 18 We know of, and in fact there are, only three types of soil used for market gardening in Paris, distinguished by the names of hard soil, soft soil and sandy soil: the first is dominated by clay, which is the most difficult to work; the second is where clay and sand, whether calcareous, selenite or siliceous, are in the most suitable ratios to form an easily divisible soil; the third is where siliceous sand, in particular, is excessively entrained and destroys the necessary cohesion. Each of these soils has its advantages and disadvantages for market gardening. However, in summer and autumn, large vegetables such as cabbage-apple, cauliflower, artichoke, cardoon, etc., grow vigorously if the surface is hoeed often to prevent splitting and cracking, or covered with a layer of mulch. This type of soil, which retains its freshness easily, does not need to be watered as frequently as the others, and the fertilizers given to it should be horse manure, no more than half consumed, and potting soil that has already been used. The second type, loose soil, is the most favorable for all kinds of vegetables; it can withstand neither drought nor humidity; moreover, it is protected from the first type by mulch, which is widely used in market gardening, and the fertilizer it needs is a base layer or loam. On the subject of fatty loam, we need to make an observation here: by the way we work our beds and water them to obtain several seasons, the manure is consumed entirely within the year and is converted into a truly fatty loam which, mixed with that with which the beds were loaded, revives the latter and restores the fertility it may have lost by feeding up to four seasons of vegetables. In middle-class gardens, where we don't work like we do, the bottom layer is never greasy; it's more or less dry, more or less burnt manure, which would only be good for mulching, if we mulched in middle-class gardens. The third type, sandy soil or soil that lacks cohesion, heats up easily in the first heat of spring; vegetation appears earlier than in other soils, and spring harvests are earlier and of good quality; but, in summer, vegetation languishes, because this type of soil heats up and dries out too much; its produce becomes tough and rises before its time. Although this type of soil is one of the easiest to plow and till, it is also one of the most expensive to cultivate for market gardening, because after spring it needs to be covered with a thicker mulch and watered much more often, and no matter what you do, the vegetables never come out as strong or as beautiful as in a more consistent soil. The most suitable fertilizer is cow manure. It is in years that are too wet for other soils that it produces its maximum yield. CHAPTER IV. Exposures and local situations. Although the new market gardener can very easily choose the best-placed and best-exposed land, we must nevertheless give you an idea of the advantages and disadvantages of the various exposures and local situations. By local situations we mean 1° A marsh with easy or difficult access, more or less far from the market hall; 2° A marsh on a high spot, exposed to all winds, with very deep wells, or a marsh in a low spot with shallow wells; 3° A manais placed on horizontal ground or placed on sloping ground. We'll explain how we feel about these different situations. 4° A marsh with easy access, with a cart and as close as possible to the market hall, will always be the most sought-after, if the land is good; it will save a lot of time, but will be sold or rented at the highest price. Marshes that are difficult to access with a cart, located on the backside or at the end of an unpaved alleyway, are less valuable, but the transport of goods is more laborious and time-consuming. 2° We would never advise a market gardener to set up in a high spot exposed to all winds, where wells are very expensive. Moderate air is useful for growing vegetables, but strong winds are harmful to them in an infinite number of ways, especially in terms of their appearance and, consequently, sales. Secondly, water drawn from a very hollow well is too expensive, whatever the means employed to raise it. 3° A marsh on level ground is the most advantageous, firstly because it's less tiring to cultivate, and secondly because you can direct the water from the sprinklers through 08 pipes to all parts of the marsh without extraordinary expense. A sloping marsh has several disadvantages: during the summer, at watering time, active supervision is needed to ensure that the water from the lowest toners is not lost, and that the water thrown on the ground soaks into the place where it is spread. In spite of these two disadvantages, if a market gardener were forced to set up on a slope, we would advise him to choose the eastern or southern slope in preference to the southern one, unless he grows absolutely nothing but early vegetables, or if the soil is clayey enough not to fear the summer heat and drought. As for the northern slope, don't think about growing early vegetables there; but summer and part-autumn vegetables will do very well. Now let's look at exposure. In gardening, the word "exposure" has a rather restricted meaning. A wall, for example, facing east to west forms two exposures, one to the south and the other to the north: to the south, the temperature is warmer; to the north, it is colder; but the effect of these exposures is no longer felt 5 or 6 meters from the wall. A roughly square marsh can have four exposures if it is enclosed by walls; one faces east, one faces south, one faces west and one faces north, and each of these has its own specific properties, which we will explain, starting with the south exposure, which is the best, after explaining the height of the walls that must or can enclose a marsh. A height of 1 metre 94 centimetres to a metre 27 centimetres is sufficient for the east, south and west exposures; however, if the south-facing wall is higher, if it's a building, for example, the exposure would be too hot in summer: as for the north-facing exposure, it's just the opposite; the lower the wall, the worse it is. On the south-facing side, we set up a cótière or flower bed, a metre 27 centimetres wide, and as long as the wall, where, with the help of this same wall, we plant romaine grown under cloche from February, and at the same time we sow carrots, or radishes, or spinach, or parsley among the romaine; and all these plants become ready for sale three weeks or a month before those planted in the open marshes; and in March we counter-plant our first open-ground cauliflowers grown in trays. Both sunrise and sunset exposures can have their own coastline, but not as wide as the one at noon, and receive the same plants or seedlings a fortnight or three weeks later; and, as the sun only favors them half the day, one from morning to noon, the other from noon to evening, the plants do not grow as quickly or are not as early as those at noon. In passing, we should point out that, if it freezes at night and the sun is shining when it rises, plants in the east-facing position are exposed to being burnt by its rays. Northern exposure, because of its coolness, can only be of some use in a marsh in summer, to receive seedlings or plants that like coolness at this time of year, such as spinach, chervil, burnet, pear, cabbage, etc. It would be better if a marsh could be planted in a northern exposure. It would be better if a marsh had no walls on this side, or only a very low one; but, when there is a wall, it's against it that sheds are erected to hold sliding boxes, sash panels and doormats when they're no longer needed. In addition to the warmer exposures that walls provide, they have the added advantage of making it possible to establish espaliers for vines or fruit trees, the produce of which is not to be sneezed at. Many marshes in Paris do not have walls, but market gardeners replace them, albeit incompletely, with windbreaks made of rye straw, about 1 meter 50 centimeters high. These windbreaks form shelters that produce only part of the good effects of a wall, and cannot hasten vegetation with the same rapidity; but their protection is not to be scorned, and they are well worth using, even making lower ones in different parts of the marsh to encourage certain seedlings or the growth of certain vegetables. We'll talk more about windbreaks in Chapter VII. CHAPTERV. Improving substances used as enyraie, amendemente stimulants. Today's home gardeners, like those of yesteryear, know only manure, water and heat as fertilizers and stimulants; nothing more. Many of us remember, however, seeing street sweepings used to improve the soil of certain marshes in Paris; but this practice has long since been abandoned by market gardeners within the walls of the capital, although it is still followed, and with good reason, in the small-scale cultivation of the surrounding area. As we've already said, it's only those market gardeners who don't grow early vegetables, and therefore don't use beds, who are obliged to buy manure expressly to fertilize their land; but the vast majority of us never buy any for this purpose; Our melon beds in the trenches provide us with mulch, and our melon beds on the ground provide us with the soil necessary for our crops; and, as we bury by ploughing three or four mulches and as many topsoils each year, this is enough to maintain the fertility of the soil of our marshes, which, as we know, produce larger and more beautiful vegetables than any other crop. Our abundant and frequent watering, our mulches that keep the soil fresh and prevent weeds from growing, the care we take in weeding and hoeing at the right time, also contribute greatly to the beauty of our vegetables. After this general overview, we'll explain how we obtain the mulch and topsoil we use, how we use it and what effects we expect from it. Mulch. - Mulch is made from very short, half-consumed horse manure. We can obtain this in three ways: 1° manure from old melon beds, which have been made in trenches; 2° old mushroom millstones, which are no longer profitable; 3° debris from our new manure piles, when we demolish them to make our beds. All these short, 10 O half-consumed fomiers are shaken out with a fork, so that what is too consumed falls off, and what is not too consumed forms mulch. Use of mulch - Every time we plough a bed, and once it has been straightened and raked, before planting it, we spread a layer of mulch 6 to 8 millimeters thick over it, so that the soil is perfectly covered, after which we plant it. Good effects of mulch. - Mulch prevents the surface of the soil from drying out, cracking or splitting; it keeps it cool; it ensures that water from watering does not run off to the right or to the left, that it penetrates where it is poured in, and prevents it from evaporating. Without mulch, we'd have to triple our watering, and the vegetables wouldn't grow as well. However, in our opinion, mulch has one small drawback, which is that it should not be spread on the beds before the end of April or May; that, if tender vegetables are planted early, lettuces for example, in a bed covered with mulch, the late spring frosts will do them more harm than if they were in a bed covered with potting soil; that's why we use potting soil, but don't mulch the beds we plant before the end of April. Potting soil. - The potting soil used in market gardening comes from the manure consumed in the beds we make on the ground. These layers, which produce several seasons, are also ploughed several times, watered heavily, and, in autumn, their manure is completely decomposed, changed into greasy loam; then we break it up with a fork, mixing it with that which was on the layer; we put it in a heap, and we then use it to put on new layers and to make our topdressings. The layers we make in trenches are loaded or covered with soil; we'll explain why later: but those we make on the ground are loaded with compost, and we put on the manure a thickness of about 15 or 14 centimetres. It's on this potting soil, using frames, that we grow our early crops, sow radishes, carrots and wild chicory, and plant romaine, lettuce, curly endive, cauliflower and so on. We also use potting soil to cover the beds we plant before May; we spread it a or 5 millimeters thick over the soil, so that it is evenly covered. In other seasons, as we'll see later, we still use potting soil, but in early spring, we prefer potting soil to mulch to cover our beds, because it doesn't attract moisture like mulch, its surface dries easily, and the plants in the bed are less likely to be tired by late frosts. Through watering, the food parts of the potting soil and mulch dissolve, are carried by the water into the soil, and nourish the plants, until they themselves are buried and benefit from a new season. CHAPTER VI. Water for watering. Up to now, all vegetable gardeners in Paris have used well water for watering. This water is clear, but hard and cold. If it were drawn from the well and kept in the air in a basin or reservoir, it would be better for watering; but the use of basins is not yet accepted by Paris market gardeners. They have half- or three-quarter-buried barrels in the most suitable places in their marshes, to receive water from a well via an underground pipe, with as many branches as there are barrels to fill. Marshes where water is channeled into the barrels are very rare today. There are districts in Paris where well water is a little selenite; some florists complain about this; but we know of no market-gardener who says that the water from his well is not as good as that from other wells. We know that our well water is too cold and that, if it were at the temperature of river water, it would be much better for our watering; but we are obliged to use it as we have it: only experience has taught us that some vegetables, at a certain period of their growth, fear it when the sun shines warmly. It is generally accepted that morning and evening watering are best. As the sun warms the soil watered in the morning, the plants should undoubtedly do well. Evening watering is good because plants are thirsty after a hot day, and because the water has time to penetrate during the night and refresh the plant roots before being excited to evaporate in the next day's sun. But when the nights become cold and long, evening watering is no longer as effective; on the contrary, it can slow down or suspend vegetation, due to the freshness of its water combined with that of the night. Morning and evening watering are not enough for market gardeners in Paris; they are often obliged to water all day long, even under a very hot sun, with their very cold water, and it is in this case that they have recognized that the contact of cold and heat is detrimental to some vegetables in a certain state of growth. Thus, although it is the rule among us to water all kinds of vegetables that have not yet reached three quarters of their growth in the morning, there are others that, having reached the same point, can no longer be watered, without damage, with cold water such as that from our wells, when the sun is shining brightly; such are melons, gherkins, romaine, &carole and chicory. Cold water, thrown on a romaine heated by the sun, makes it speckle, in market gardener's terms. CHAPTER VII. Tools, instruments and machines. OBSERVATION. - However, since walls replace windbreaks and are much more advantageous, and since wells are an absolute necessity in our marshes, we felt it would be better to discuss them in the chapter on instruments than elsewhere. Watering cans. - Although watering cans are made of zinc, tin and yellow copper, market gardeners only use red copper watering cans, which are heavier and more expensive, but stronger and longer-lasting. Market gardeners only use watering cans with knobs, with which they carry out very light basins, full sprinklings and mouth sprinklings, i.e. in some cases, it is more advantageous to pour the water through the mouth of the watering can than through the knob. An ordinary watering can weighs a kilogram when empty, and holds 10 liters of water. When watering, a man carries two full watering cans, one in each hand, and, by a certain movement, empties them both at once to shorten time, which is always precious in our state. The perfection of a watering can consists above all in the inclination of its head, and in the number and width of the holes through which it is pierced. The number of watering cans needed in a farm depends on the number of men who can water at the same time during the summer: each pair of red copper watering cans (also called jugs in some marshes) costs from 28 to 32 fr. the average price. ... ... .... . . . .. 30 fr. Spade. - The spade is the gardener's plow. It's a long square iron blade, reinforced with a ridge underneath, fitted with a socket at the top to receive a wooden handle about a meter long, and sharp and cutting at the bottom. The spade is used to plow, re6 O turn and divide soil to a depth of 8 to 10 inches (22 to 28 centimeters). Spades come in different sizes and qualities, depending on the strength of the user. A spade costs around 5 fr. 5 fr. Binette. - This is a small, sharp blade with a quarter- or half-circle bent socket, into which a wooden handle 1 to a meter long is inserted. The hoe is used to stir up the soil in plantations where grass is beginning to grow, in order to kill it; it costs 75 c.; ci. 75c. Bordoir. - This is the name given to a piece of wooden planking, about 1 meter long and 20 centimeters wide, with a small round wooden handle 12 centimeters long in the middle on one side: this tool is used to edge potting soil in layers with bells or without a [box; to use it, place it in the field on the edge of the manure in the layer, and draw and press the potting soil against the bordoir, to give the potting soil consistency and solidity. When the potting soil has made a good wall, the kerbstone is slid in a little more, and the whole layer is edged. With this tool, a single man can edge a layer; by means of his handle, he can also beat and slump the potting soil of the layer when it is lined, so that it is less hollow and sags less. Market gardeners sometimes make their own bordoir; if they do, it costs 1 fr. 50 c.; ci. . . . . . . . . 1 fr. 50c. Windbreaks. - Windbreaks are strong straw mats which, instead of being sewn with string, are held together by three rows of laths tied with wicker; they are used to enclose marshes which have neither walls nor hedges, and to provide shelter for certain seedlings and plantations: they are planted upright, supported by strong stakes. Straw, laths and stakes included, a meter of windbreaks cost about a fr. per double meter; ci... Ef. Stretcher wheelbarrow. - This wheelbarrow is made up of a wooden wheel and two long handlebars joined by several crossbars to form an open bottom; it has nothing on the sides, but only a sideboard at the front; it is used to transport 4 large-volume, light-weight items, such as new manure. Its price is about 11 fr; ci... ... ... ... ... ... ... .... 11fr. box wheelbarrow - This one is shorter than the other. The bottom, front and two sides are made of thin planks; it is used to transport consumed manure, loam, earth and rubbish; there are larger and smaller ones: the ones we use cost o fr. each; ci... . .. . . . . . . . . . . . . .. 9 fr. Calais - This is what we call a small hollow dummy, made of small strips of burgundy wood, into which we put sorrel, lettuce, etc.: they cost 1 fr. a0 c. a dozen; ci. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .. .. 1 fr. 20c. Wedge or cleat - This is a piece of wood 3' centimeters thick, 10 centimeters wide and 18 centimeters long: it is used to lift the frames from behind when you want to give air to the plants underneath; it is placed on the edge of the box flat, in the field or upright, depending on the amount of air you want to give them. Cinnamon. - A large copper one is needed at the bottom of the trough or tank, costing... 35 fr. A smaller one is required for each barrel, at a price of 8 to 10 fr. 9 fr. Carts. - The carts used by market gardeners to bring their vegetables to the market and cart their manure are one-horse-drawn and cost from 500 to 550 fr. on average. 525 fr. Chargeoir - This is a sort of crudely made tripod, with two arms or sticks at the top to act as backrests, on which the hottriau is placed to load it with manure, earth or potting soil, etc.: this chargeoir costs 5 fr. This loader costs 5 fr; ci.... 5 fr. Chassis. - Châssis and panneau de châssis are synonymous. This is a square piece of oak wood, painted with two coats of oil, measuring 1 meter 33 centimeters on each side and divided into four by three small timbers to support the glass; Frames are indispensable in forced cultivation, in that they maintain the heat and humidity of the bed, and the daylight, the heat of the sun penetrating through the glass, keeps the plants underneath at a temperature favorable to their growth; when the heat is deemed too great, or we wish to harden the plants a little, we give them air by raising the frame more or less from behind. It goes without saying that, since we only use trunks and frames to grow early plants, they should always be tilted towards the south as far as possible. A panel of oak wood, painted and glazed, costs from 10 to 12 fr; average term. 11 fr. Stretcher. - The stretcher is made up of two 2-meter 66-centimeter long arms, joined by four 70- to 80-centimeter long bars, placed in the center, 16 centimeters apart. In our marshes, the stretcher is particularly useful for transporting frames from the shed to the trunks and from the trunks to the shed. Two men can carry five or six frames on a stretcher. Bells. - Market garden bells are lo centimeters in diameter at the base, about a0 centimeters at the top, and 36 to 37 centimeters high. In the past, bells were made of a greener glass than they are today; market gardeners complained about this, and now they are made of a whiter, but more fragile glass. The usefulness of cloches in the cultivation of early crops is less effective than that of frames; but their use is more general in that it is less expensive. The price of cloches varies a little: today, they are sold for 80 to 85 fr. per cent; the average ternre... 82 fr. 50c. 08 Chests. - There are chests with one or two frames, but in our marshes they are almost all with three frames. A chest with three frame panels is a square 4 meters long, 1 meter 33 centimeters wide, built of fir planks and laid on a layer; the back is a7 to 30 centimeters high, and the front 19 to 22 centimeters: we prefer fir, because it's less heavy and less expensive than oak; we don't paint it, because oil paint can harm plant roots. The front of the box is fitted with small cleats to prevent the frames from sinking, and the top is trimmed with three dovetailed crosspieces to maintain the gauge and support the edges of the frames. Our chests have neither the depth nor the inclination of those in middle-class gardens, because we want the plants to be close to the glass; but, if we wanted to force plants that grow taller, such as tomatoes, eggplants and cauliflowers, we would put two chests on top of each other. A box with three frame panels costs do q fr. to 7 fr. 50 c.; average term... ... . 7 fr. 25e. Cordeaw - A cord is essential, along with a double meter, for laying out planks and paths properly. The size of the cord is arbitrary. It is sold by weight, at the rate of 1fr. per half kilogram; here... 1 fr. Cotière - In market gardening terms, a more or less wide bed, sheltered or protected by a wall, a windbreak or a hedge against cold winds, where vegetables are sown or planted earlier than in a plein carré. Rack. - This is a piece of oak lath, 20 to 24 centimeters long, on one side of which are three notches or elongated notches, 5 centimeters apart, to support the more or less high bells, when you want to give air to the plants they cover. Double metre ferré. - We have replaced the toise with the double metre, which is roughly the same length; we use it to measure the width of our boards, so that they are uniform. We don't use the end of the double meter to mark out the radii of a board for planting lettuces, onions, etc. We use a different method, as we'll see later. The double meter, shod at both ends, costs from I fr. to1 fr. 50c.; average term... ... ... ... ... .... 1 fr. 25c. Flail. - A flail consists of a handle 1 meter 5o centimeters long, to the end of which is attached by straps a batten half as short and much larger than the handle. The flail is used to beat ripe vegetables whose seeds do not fall easily, such as chicory: it costs 1 fr. 50 c.; ci. . . 1 fr. 50c. Fork. - Indispensable tool for loading and unloading manure, shaping layers and breaking up clods on ploughed beds. The fork is made of iron, with three large pointed tines, slightly angled near their origin, to give them the right direction for their purpose. The side opposite the tines has a socket for a strong wooden handle 1 meter 50 centimeters long. The price of a good fork is 5 fr. to 5 fr. 50 c.; average term. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 5fr.25c. Gibet. - In the marshes, this is the name given to three pieces of wood planted in a triangle around a well, 3 meters high, joined at the top by a pulley over which runs a rope with a bucket at each end, which a man raises and lowers with his arms. This device can only be used in shallow wells; its use is diminishing daily in marshes, and it is being replaced by maneges and gear pumps: it costs, all assembled, from 28 to 30 fr.; ci... ... ... ... .... . . . . 29 fr. Hoods - Market gardeners' hoods are louvered and smaller than ordinary hoods; most market gardeners need at least two dozen. These hoods are used to artfully arrange the vegetables that are to go to the market, and when a hood is loaded with vegetables in this way, the whole thing is called a lane: a cauliflower lane, a melon lane. A market gardener will say: "Today I sent twenty lanes of merchandise to the hallé. 08 Women are more skilful than men at tastefully mounting merchandise on a hood, so it's almost always they who do the work. A hood costs from 2 fr. to 2 fr. 50 c.; average term... ... ... ... .... 2 fr.25c. Hottriau. - This word, which seems to be a diminutive of hotte and should mean a small hood, refers, on the contrary, to a hood two or three times larger than the others. The hottriau is made of small wood and wicker, like ordinary hoods, and is likewise carried on the back by means of two straps; by means of it, a man carries a considerable volume of manure at the time of making the layers, and passes where one could not pass with a wheelbarrow: it is used to carry compost on the layers, on the boards; it is used to carry away the old manure from the trenches; finally the hottriau is a very-useful piece of furniture in a marsh: it costs 5 or 6 fr.average term ... ... ... ... . 5 fr. 50c. Manege. - In gardens, this is the name given to a heavy frame device used to draw water from a well by means of a horse. There are more or less complicated maneges, depending on fortunes and needs. Here is an abbreviated description of those of intermediate cost. On two opposite sides of a well's curbstone, two strong pieces of squared timber, about 3 meters high and 22 centimeters square, are planted and sealed upright and at least a meter apart: These are joined to the som08 met by a crossbeam of the same squareness; then a second crossbeam of the same diameter is placed about 9o centimetres below the first, and the result is a frame or chassis divided into three equal parts by two strong pieces of squared timber, mortised into the two crossbeams; these two pieces of timber are drilled and hollowed out in the middle, to each receive a pulley. Directly opposite, and 5 or 6 meters from the well, another piece of wood of the same height and diameter is planted, and joined to the first frame by a cross-beam to give strength to the whole. At the center, or 3 meters from the well, we place a shaft rotating vertically on two iron pivots, one at the bottom on a stone die, and the other in the upper crosspiece; this rotating shaft has in its upper part a large drum, and a tail or lever attached to the shaft, 2 or 3 meters long, which descends obliquely to 84 centimeters from the ground, and at the end of which we harness a horse to turn the shaft and drum. Two strong ropes are then provided, long enough that, after being attached to the drum by one end, one at the bottom, the other at the top, and having passed over the pulleys, they can descend into the well water, and still be 6 to 7 meters long, Then a large bucket (80 to 100 liters) is attached to each end, and as the horse turns the drum, one empty bucket descends into the well and the other rises full of water. In many marshes, a person is present to pour the water into a trough or reservoir; but in a few others, a hook stops the bucket and forces it to empty itself. A fully assembled manege costs from 500 to 600 francs; average term:.... ... ..... . .. 550 fr. mannes - Mannes are baskets without handles; they are more or less large and deep, and made of wicker. There must be a certain number of them in a market-gardening establishment. They are used to store various vegetables, particularly herbs, to take to the market. A manne costs 9o cent; ci. 90c. Mannettes -There are two types, both in the shape of wicker baskets, used to hold melons and cauliflowers for delivery to the market: they cost 59 to 75 centimes each; average price... ... ... .... 65c. Maniveau. - This is a sort of small wicker chaseret, used only to put strawberries and mushrooms for retail sale: these little utensils sell for 1 fr. 25 c. per cent; ci... ..... ..... ... ... 1 fr.25c. Wall. - The most suitable wall for enclosing a marsh, on the east, north and west sides, should be 6 to 7 feet (a meters to a meters 33 centimeters) high, not including the coping above it, and 15 inches (40 centimeters) thick. This wall, built of rubble stone and plaster, costs 10 to II fr. per running meter in Paris; average term... ... ... ... ... . 10 fr.50c. Doormats. - Gardeners everywhere make their own doormats: they come in many sizes and thicknesses; ours are neither very large nor very thick, so that they are not too heavy and dry quickly after getting wet in the rain; they are a meter long and 1 meter 35 centimeters wide. We use them to cover our diapers, bells and frames against cold and hail, and sometimes against the sun; we always use a very large number of them. Some market-gardening establishments have up to a thousand of them. Here's how to make a gardener's doormat: on even, earthen ground, fix two boards 2 meters long and 10 centimeters wide, parallel and 1 meter 33 centimeters apart; these two boards are called doormat looms, and serve to fix the width. The gap between these two boards is divided into four equal parts, by three lines placed 33 centimeters apart and as long as the boards, and at the two ends of each line a small finger-sized wooden stake is driven in firmly, projecting 4 centi08 meters above the ground: the 6 posts determine the length of the doormat, just as the side boards determine its width; then you take some doormat twine, stretch it tightly from post to post in the longitudinal direction, securing it to the posts with a tab or loop. This gives us three lines of string, each 2 meters long; but we don't have to cut the string at the loop of the pegs at the bottom of the loom, or on the side where we want to start the doormat, because experience has taught us that just twice as much string is needed to sew the doormat to what is stretched underneath: so, after the loop is made, you'll need to measure twice the length of the stretched string, and spare this double length, or 4 meters before cutting the string; this teaches immediately, moreover, that you need 18 meters of string to make a doormat 2 meters long. The ends of the string are each wound onto a small 12-centimetre-long piece of wood, the use of which we'll see shortly. With the strings thus stretched out, we take some well-cleaned, well-trimmed rye straw; we lay a bed of it on the strings, 4 mêtre So centimetres thick, pressing the foot of the straw against the board on this side; we lay as much on the other side so that it lies head to tail on the first; we even out the whole thickness as much as possible; then we proceed with the sewing. One or two men can sew a doormat at 0 the same time; you kneel at the bottom of the loom where the bobbins are; with your left hand you take a pinch of straw the size of your finger and lift the string underneath at the same time; Then, with the right hand, you pass the spool to the right under the taut string, and withdraw it to the left, engaging it or passing it between the pinch of straw and the string above to form a stitch or slipknot; you take another pinch of straw, and make another knot, and all this in less time than it takes to say it. All in all, a doormat 2 meters long and 1 meter 40 centimeters high costs 50 c.; ci... ... ... ... ... ... ... .... 50c. Wooden shovel. - Everyone knows the shape of a wooden shovel; there are larger and smaller ones: the average one is suitable for market gardeners; it is particularly useful for loading soil into the layers they make in trenches, sometimes also for loading some of those they make on the ground, for loading a hottriau, a wheelbarrow of potting soil, of consumed manure, finally for collecting all kinds of debris in heaps. A wooden shovel costs from 1 fr. to r fr. 50C.; average term ... ... ... ... ... ... . 1fr.25c. English shovel. - This can, to a certain extent, replace the wooden shovel in all its uses; but, having its blade in beaten and fairly polished iron, it is more convenient than the other for mucking out earth and potting soil. An English shovel costs from 6 to 8 francs; the average price... ... ..... 7fr. Wooden planters. - These are round pieces of wood, 4 centimeters thick, about 26 centimeters long, with a blunt point at the bottom, curved like a corbin beak at the top, with which we plant our lettuces, onions, and anything else that is not too delicate to pick up. After making the hole with the planter and getting the plant's roots in, we fix them properly by pressing soil against them with another stroke of the planter. We make our own wooden planters. Iron planters. - These differ from the previous ones only in that they have an iron bottom and last longer: they are used for the same purposes and are perhaps not as good for plants, because of their hardness and the oxide they can deposit near the roots. In any case, these iron planters cost from 1 franc to 1 franc 50 centimes a piece; average term..... . .... . .. . ..1fr.25c. Gear pumps. - These devices are still new to the market gardens of Paris, and there are as yet few of them established, no doubt because of their high price; but we have no doubt that they will multiply rapidly, for their advantages are indisputable: they are more or less complicated, no doubt because of the depth of the well and the height to which they push the water above ground level; and, although one of us has had one established at his well, we shall refrain from describing it, for fear of giving only an inaccurate idea of it; but we can say that the simplest cost from 1,500 to 1,600 francs, and that there are some that cost up to 2,600 francs; average term.......... . . . . . . 2,200 fr. Well - A well can be more or less deep, and the excavation may encounter unforeseen difficulties. The soil may require a thicker or thinner foundation, so it's not easy to say in advance what a well will cost. The Paris subsoil being fairly well known, there are contractors who make wells at so much per meter, because of the perfection of the work; but a market gardener's well needing only solidity, it can cost from 90 to 100 fr. per meter; average term. ... ... . . . . .. . . . . . .. 95 fr. Rake. - A rake consists of a piece of wood, called a head, about 32 to 45 centimeters long, into which are set twelve or sixteen iron teeth. The rake head is pierced in the middle by a hole into which the end of a long handle is inserted. The rake is used to scrape, divide and smooth the top of planks where seedlings are to be planted; it is also used to clean and remove weeds and garbage from paths and walkways: it costs from 2 fr. to 2 fr. 50 c.; ci.. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 2 fr. 50c. Rake. - There are push rakes and pull rakes; they are made of wrought iron and scythe iron: push rakes have a 7-centimeter-wide, 27-centimeter-long blade, with a socket in the middle to accommodate a long handle. The push rake is used for rapid weeding in large plantations, as in a cabbage patch; the pull rake differs only in that it is shorter and its socket is curved in a semicircle, so that it can be pulled along while working; it is used for raking paths and hard spots. Both cost 2 f. 25 c.: here 2 f. 25c. Serfouette. - There are double and single serfouettes. Double serfouettes have the eye in the middle to accommodate a boïs handle; on one side is a small, sharp blade, and on the other are two teeth: they are little used in our marshes, though convenient. The simple ones have the eye at one end to receive the handle, and the other has a blade or two teeth which are used to crutch the earth in plantations and better prepare it to receive watering. A serfouette costs 75 c. : ci. . . . . . . . ... .. .. 75c. Barrels. - We have already mentioned that market gardeners place barrels in their marshes, and at suitable distances, to receive the ean that arrives from the well by means of buried pipes or channels on the ground. These barrels, which are buried three-quarters or more of the way down, are of two kinds4 : some are wine barrels, hooped in wood and bound with wicker, and cost only 5 or 6 fr. each; but, apart from the fact that they can only hold a small quantity of water, they also have the disadvantage of lasting only three or four years, and their use ends up being more expensive than the next. Oil pipes or barrels are preferred today, firstly because they are larger, and secondly because they are hooped with eight or ten iron hoops, and their wood is soaked in oil which makes them resistant to rotting for twelve or fifteen years. These large oil barrels contain around 540 liters of water, which loses its crudeness as in a small basin; and, although they cost us 14 and 15 fr. each, there is still profit in preferring them to wine barrels: ci... 14 fr. 50c. Pipes - The water drawn from the well by a manege or gear pump is poured into a trough or small reservoir higher than the marsh floor. The bottom of this reservoir, usually lined with lead or zinc, is pierced with a hole to which is fitted a piece of lead pipe, divided in two by a large copper cinnamon, which is opened and closed as required. This lead pipe should go down roughly perpendicularly to 6 or 8 inches into the earth, and there run horizontally and in such a way that the head of the underground pipe that is to carry the water to all the barrels in the marsh can be soldered to it. O8 This pipe could be made of lead or cast iron: but market gardeners prefer sandstone, i.e. made of pieces of sandstone pipe fitted end-to-end and puttied with fountain putty. As these pipes are curved in the shape of a T, as many branches as you like can be established on the main pipe. Water usually enters barrels through the bung hole, and would often overflow and be lost, if this were not remedied in the following way: instead of the end of the pipe entering the barrel being made of stoneware, it is replaced by an end of lead pipe, fitted with a copper cinnamon, which is opened to fill the barrel and closed when it is full. There are stoneware pipes with two internal diameters; those with a diameter of 5 and a half centimeters cost 68 c. per meter, all laid: ci. 68 c. Those with a diameter of 8 centimeters cost 75 c. per meter, also fully laid: ci.. 75c. Van. - Basketwork in the shape of a skimmer shell, with two handles, used for winnowing and cleaning seeds. There are vans of various sizes; those we use cost from 3 to 4 fr. Average term, 3 fr. 50 c. ci. . . . . . .. 3 fr. 50c. CHAPTER VIII. Market gardening operations. OBSERVATION. - To avoid repetition and length in chapter X, where we shall explain market gardening in all its details, we have felt it necessary to give here the nomenclature and explanation of all the cultivation operations that are carried out more or less often in the course of a year. So, for example, when we find in chapter X: one plows, one topsoils, one mulches, etc., if the reader does not know what these operations are, he will find the explanation in this chapter VIII. ~i, Accot, accoter. - An accot is a belt of short manure, 40 to 50 centimetres wide and the same height as the layer, placed around it and pressed down to prevent the cold from penetrating the layer from the sides. The accot 20.- brst, differs from the rechaud in that it is made with old manure, while the rechaud is made with new manure, hot or which can heat up and communicate its heat to the layer it surrounds. Th pan batrddos. - The use of ados is of great im~ portance in market gardening in Paris: it is. ~ by means of ados that we supply ptd consumption with head lettuce in November and December; it is on ados that, from October and November, we raise romaine, lettuce and cauliflower, which you deliver for consumption in the spring. So we need to explain here, in some detail, how we train the ados in our marshes. It's rare that we can make an ados against a wall with a southern exposure, as the academy indicates; but, if we have a wall that shelters us from the north wind, we make our ados preferably, pon against this wall, because the service would be difficult, but at a certain distance in front of this wall. If we don't have a wall, we build our ados in a square; the only essential condition is that these ados slope towards the south. Our custom is to make our ados 1 meter 28 centimeters (4 feet) wide, so that we can place three rows of bells on top: this width is the most convenient for perfect work. When we want to make an ados, we take a strip of land running from east to west and of the width indicated above, and, while ploughing it, we lower it by 16 centimetres (6 inches) on the south side, and raise it by 16 centimetres (6 inches) on the north side; this gives the ados 32 centimetres (1 foot) of slope to the south, and this slope is the one we find most advantageous. Once the ploughing is done and the earth well divided, we take a string, stretch it over the crest of the ados, cut its edge with a spade and beat it with the back of the spade, to make it solid and so as to form an almost vertical slope to the north of the ados, and re-slope the earth that has been cut on the same ados. C Once this operation is complete, we use a three-pronged fork to level out the soil and break up the clods in the soil bed; we rake the soil to give it a smooth surface with the right slope to the south; then we spread a 5-centimeter-thick bed of fine potting soil over the entire surface; we press this soil down with a boarder or a shovel, and the soil bed is finished; all that's left to do is plant it. But we almost always plant several teas in front of each other, and if they were close together, they would harm each other in that the high side of one would cast its shadow on the low side of the one behind it, and the plants would suffer. It is therefore necessary to leave a path 96 centimetres (3 feet) wide between each teenager. Amender (to make better)-While this word does not exclude any of the means that can make things better, farmers use it to make the soil better without using fertilizers. Thus, sand is improved by mixing it with clay; clay is improved by mixing it with sand; dry soil is made fertile by moistening it properly, and over-moist soil by drying it out to a certain degree. Finally, some soils are sterile only because of their impermeability; they can be improved by dividing them by ploughing and exposing all their parts to atmospheric influences. Watering. - In market gardening, no plant can do without watering, because 98 it needs to grow fast and well; a properly watered vegetable retains its tenderness, develops fully and looks healthy. Market gardeners water more than other gardeners, so they generally have more beautiful vegetables; we use only large apple watering cans in our marshes, and with them we perform the lightest basins as well as the most copious waterings, with apple and mouth; in the marshes, we use the word wet more often than the word water. Although some vegetables require more watering than others, it is not easy to establish rules for the quantity of water to be given to each species of plant; all we know is that you can't give them too much in hot weather accompanied by severe drought; but in other cases, practice and observation should teach you to moderate or increase watering. Thus, in spring, as long as late frosts are to be feared, watering should be avoided after two o'clock in the afternoon, so that the moisture on the surface of the soil is sufficiently dissipated not to contribute to increasing frost the following morning. In summer, you can water all day long, especially in the evening, because water poured on in the evening does not vaporize, is used to the benefit of the plants, and many of them 98 grow more at night than during the day, when they do not lack humidity. In autumn, you should only water from morning to two o'clock in the afternoon; later, the coolness of the water, combined with that of the night, could slow or stop vegetation. There is an effect caused by watering in the middle of the day, which we'll explain when we talk about growing romaine. We believe that if we didn't water with cold, raw water from the well, that if we had large basins in our marshes where the water would take on a higher temperature, it would be better for watering; But the use of basins has not yet been introduced into market gardening in Paris, and several difficulties stand in the way of its introduction; moreover, we are convinced that our well water loses its rawness in the journey it takes from the well to our barrels. Although the way to carry out an ordinary apple watering is quite simple, it can only be learned by practice, so we won't try to describe it; we'll just say that the water must fall from quite high up and not beat the earth, -4 Watering with the mouth.-This is how you water 2 when, instead of pouring water through the head of the watering can, you pour it through its opening, called the mouth. This is how you water certain large vegetables98 whose roots don't run and which need a lot of water, such as cauliflower and cardoons. Bassiner. - This is watering with an apple, 7-t, but is done very lightly, so that the water falls as a fine rain: to succeed, the watering can must be raised a lot and moved briskly. Drenching only darkens the soil, and only wets it to a depth of 1 or z centimeters: you should particularly drench seedlings whose seeds are not deeply buried. Hoeing. - This means stirring the soil to a depth of 6 to 10 centimetres, between plants, with a hoe, to kill any weeds that grow there and take the food intended for the plants. Hoeing also lifts and loosens the soil, making it better able to absorb atmospheric influences and water from watering. Border. - When you're not putting a box or frame on a bed, you need to border the soil or potting mix on top, i.e. raise the soil or potting mix vertically in the shape of a small wall, 12 to 16 centimetres (5 to 6 inches) high all around the surface of the bed. To do this, use a border (see this word), which is placed on the edge of the bed and against which you press the soil or potting mix, so as to make it solid. When the 0 length of the kerb is solidified, it is slid a little further out, and so on, until the whole length of the bed has been solidified. ~~ ~-~~ti 58 Borner. - Market gardener's term expressing the action of pressing the soil with a planter ~ b against the root of a plant, when this root is placed in the hole prepared for it with the same planter. In more general terms, it means firming up the soil around the roots of a plant that has just been planted. ~ Bouchonner. - Melons are said to bouchonf- 7 tist nent when, planted under cloches, their 4hfy ~~hne branches can emerge from the cloche to elongate, and the leaves at their ends remain close to like a sort of buffer; this happens when the cold doesn't allow them to emerge, or when you forget to lift the cloches when necessary. The corking upsets vegetation. 4 Our Brouiller. - If a square in which you've spent the ra~~ tissoire growing contains a lot of grass, and you're worried about it reattaching itself and continuing to live, then you'll have to scramble it.

To do this, we take a rake and pass it over the whole square, pulling and pushing so as to bring all the weeds to the surface, where they dry out and die in a short time. OButter. - To pile up earth around the base of a plant: you can butter the base of an eggplant or tomato to keep it upright and increase the number of its roots to give it more vigor. Potatoes are ridged to increase the number of tubers, which is not always possible; celery and chard are ridged to blanch them and make them more tender, etc. Load a layer. - Placing on it the soil or potting compost necessary for the growth of the plants you wish to cultivate. Cloche. - To put a bell on a seedling to encourage germination; on a newly planted lettuce, romaine or melon plant, to encourage recovery by sheltering it from the wind and cold. A clochee is something that fits under a bell. Coiffer. - When romaine is almost fully grown, the tops of its leaves fold inwards to form a cap, and all these tops, overlapping one another, hide the heart of the plant; we then say la romaine se coiffe or la romaine est coiffée, la romaine se coiffe bien or se coiffe mal. To say la romaine pomme or se pomme is not as accurate as saying la romaine se coiffe. Counter-planting. - In the marshes, you don't always wait until a bed is empty before replanting. When a bed of romaine, for example, is three-quarters planted, other rows of escarole or chicory are replanted between its rows, soon replacing the romaine. Mother layer. - This is the name we give to a layer designed to germinate seeds, particularly those of our melons: we make it square and give it 1 meter 65 centimeters on each side and 66 centimeters high; we place a single-panel box on top and fill it with potting soil, in which we place our germinating seeds, such as melon, cucumber, chicory, eggplant, etc. Nursery layer. - This is the same width and height as the previous one, but three times as long, or long enough to hold a three-panel box. This layer is filled with potting soil, and the seedlings germinated on the mother layer are transplanted into it. Winter layer. - The name of this layer indicates that it must be thick enough to produce heat capable of withstanding the cold of the season, with the help of cots and blankets. It is usually filled with coftres, frames and potting soil, into which we plant crepe lettuces or small black lettuces, and in which we sow carrots, radishes, etc.; but, when we want to plant cantaloupes, small prescotts, we put soil mixed with potting soil in the coftres, instead of pure potting soil. We make our winter layers 54 to 60 centimeters (20 to 22 inches) high. Spring layer. - The only difference between D8 and the previous one is that, given the lesser danger of frost, we only make it 40 to 48 centimeters (15 to 18 inches) thick. Trench layer. - This type of layer is particularly useful for growing second-season cantaloupe melons under a frame. The first trench is 1 metre wide and 33 centimetres deep, and the soil is carried to where the last trench is to be made: a 66-centimetre-high layer is made in this first trench, then a second trench is opened, similar and parallel to the first, 66 centimetres apart, and the soil from it is deposited on the first layer. When the layer in the second trench is done, a third trench is opened, and the soil from this trench is thrown onto the second layer, and so on, until the end, when the soil from the first trench is found to load the last layer. Market gardeners in Paris make a lot of layers in trenches for the second-season cantaloupe crop, which is the one they rely on most.
