Boxes are progressively placed on all these layers, the soil is spread and loosened, the panels are placed, and two melon plants are planted per frame. Couche sourde - We feel we should adopt this term, which is more meaningful than the term "couche à cloches" used in our marshes. It has much in common with the trench bed, but differs 1° in that the trench is only 66 centimeters wide; 2° in that the bed is domed at the top in the shape of a "dos de bahut"; 3° in that the melons are planted under cloches, instead of under frames. These differences are due to the fact that, when the muffled layers are made, it is no longer as cold as when the trench layers are made. Coup de feu. - A layer is said to have reached its "coup de feu" when the manure of which it is composed has developed its greatest heat, and this heat is all the greater when it has been made with newer horse manure. The heat develops three or four days after the layer is loaded, and may last five or six days. A thermometer immersed 8 centimeters deep in the soil of a bed during the firing period can mark up to 50 degrees centigrade. Generally speaking, we wait until the fire has passed, or until the soil has cooled down to 30 degrees centigrade, before planting on a new layer; but market gardeners are less afraid of fire than other gardeners, because they always have piles of manure that has been piled up in advance for two, four and six months, which has lost its fire, but which regains heat when wet and mixed with new manure to make layers, and these layers develop a more moderate heat that is retained longer than in a layer built up with all new manure. O8 We should point out here that inn manure is hotter than barrack manure, that whole horse manure is hotter than geldings, and that we have examples of steam escaping from whole horse manure sometimes killing young melon seedlings under chassis when concentrated or retained there. Déclocher. - Removing bells from plants when they are no longer needed; this is usually done at the beginning of June, when the season has become sufficiently warm. Husk. - Garlic and shallots are dehusked at their base; and, by extension, the same word also applies to plants that proliferate at their base, such as mint and tarragon; they are dehusked by dividing their large clump into several smaller ones to multiply them. Défoncer. - This means ploughing the soil two, three or four times deeper than in ordinary ploughing, and this greater depth requires different procedures. The aim is to improve the soil to a certain depth, to the point where the roots can spread out; this is why we plow deeper where we want to plant trees than where we want to grow vegetables. When the subsoil is of a poor nature, de-sinking can render the land sterile for some time by bringing the bad soil to the surface; but we never expose ourselves to this inconvenience in market gardening, as our land must yield immediately and a lot. If a new plot of land on which we want to establish a marsh has only 30 centimetres of topsoil, and the subsoil is a hard, compact tuff, we don't break it up; but if the soil, without changing its nature, as often happens, loses its fertility as it gets deeper, and is, for example, from 60 centimetres to 1 metre or more thick, then we break it up to a depth of 40 centimetres: if it seems too light or too sandy, we mix in cow manure; if it seems too compact or too clayey, we mix in horse manure. We are well aware that the best way to amend or improve soil that is too strong or too compact is to mix in a suitable quantity of sand, but so far this method has not been used in market gardening. The first is to bring the lower layers of soil to the surface, so that they can be improved by atmospheric influences, cultivation and fertilizers, while the improved upper layer is put back where it was; 08 the second aim is to make all the soil in the ripping more permeable to air, heat and watering. To break up the soil, we use a spade or hoe to open a trench at the end of the ground, the length of which is proportional to the number of workers employed, 5o centimetres wide and 4o centimetres deep, and we remove the soil where the break-up is to end; The same width of soil is then cut along this trench, the top soil is thrown into the bottom, and the lower layers of this new excavation are placed on top of that already in the bottom, mixing the necessary fertilizer into the middle of these layers as they are laid on top of the first. After this operation, the first trench is filled, and we have another next to it, which we fill like the first, and so on to the end of the room where we find the soil from the first trench to fill the last. Setting out a plank means fixing its width and levelling it after it has been ploughed. Since we're interested in working fast and losing as little ground as possible, we make our beds wider than they are in vegetable gardens, and have fewer paths. To set up one or more beds, you need a double metre or stick 2 metres 35 centimetres (7 feet) long, a string, a fork and a rake. With the double meter, we fix the width of an O plank, which in our marshes is always 2 meters 53 centimeters (7 feet). With the chalk line, we mark out the two paths that must run along either side of the plank; each path must be 33 centimeters (1 foot) wide, and we mark it by tamping. With the fork, we break up the larger clods on the surface of the plank, and with the rake, which we use instead of a harrow, we remove the small clods that cannot pass between its tines: in this way, our paths are higher than the planks, and the water used for watering is retained on the planks. Thinning. - This means pulling out some of the young plants that are in each other's way because they have been sown too thickly. We thin out seedlings for three different purposes: 1° We thin out our seedlings by taking some of the plant here and there to transplant it elsewhere; 2° We thin out our forced carrots and radishes by taking some of the most advanced for sale here and there, thus making room for the others; 3° We thin out our sorrel and spinach seedlings when they seem to have sprouted too thickly; in this last case only, what we pull out is lost, but what remains is of greater benefit. Enamel (remove the mesh). - In narcinage terms, the female melon flower is called a carlle, and the male flower is called a false flower. Market gardeners have observed that, when a melon plant has a certain number of meshes, they harm each other, and that often none of them knot: by dint of examination, they have managed to recognize the best condlitioned of all these meshes, and they detach all the others; it is this operation that they call enameling. Greasing. - When a new piece of land is planted in marshland, it is fertilized by all known means, if necessary; but once it is ready to be cultivated in marshland, it is no longer fertilized from outside; its fertility is maintained by topdressing, mulch and the debris of old layers. It is not even necessary for a marsh to be too greasy or too fertile; the proof is that all market gardeners who make a lot of layers sell part of their compost, Fertilizers. - Fertilizers are any number of animal or vegetable substances which, when mixed with cultivable soil, improve it and make it more fertile; but the market gardeners of Paris know no other fertilizer in their cultivation than horse manure and rabbit dung, when they have any; and this manure they bury very sparingly in kind; it is only after it has been used to make layers and mulch that they bury its debris. As we've already said, it's not by dint of fertilizers that we obtain beautiful vegetables, it's by our way of working and our timely watering. Interplanting. - This means planting two species of plant at the same time. For example, when planting a romaine coast, leave two or three rows empty for interplanting cauliflowers. Stuff the bells. - When bells are no longer needed in market gardening, they are first stuffed, then put on the road (see this word): to stuff them, first provide yourself with soft, dry, flexible litter, taken from a pile of new manure; then put a bell upright; put a little litter on its head and sides; put another bell on this filling, pressing it down a little; put a little litter back on its head and a third bell on top. Five bells can be placed one on top of the other in this way, and the whole thing is called a bell pack; all that's left to do is set them off (see this word). But in the course of these operations, we always find a few bells cracked or broken, so we put them aside and mend them in this way. Take the finest ceruse white, thin it into a thick mush, put it on the edge of the bell pieces, bring them together and fix them with one or more small pieces of glass coated with the same white, which you place on the break. When the white is dry, the bell is stronger than before. Etéter. - To cut the head of a plant with the fingernails or a sharp instrument. In market gardening, only melons, cucumbers and tomatoes are topped. Melons are topped when they have two leaves, cucumbers when they have two to four, and tomatoes when the plants are about 1 metre tall. Some old gardeners still refer to the topping of melons as "châtrer" (to castrate). Frapper. - When a melon is close to ripening, you can tell it's ripe by the shade it changes: its color becomes paler, its stem becomes ringed; then it's hit and good to pick; a few days later, it's good to eat. This change, which occurs overnight, from morning to noon, is seen as happening suddenly, or as striking the melon unexpectedly. Smoking. - Burying manure in the top layer of the soil to give it the fertility it lacks or to increase the fertility it already has. We use this method, if necessary, when we want to establish a market garden on land that has never been cultivated in this way; but, once the land has been brought to the right state for our crops, we don't bury any more manure; the debris from our layers is enough to maintain the fertility we need. New manure. - The market gardeners of Paris only use horse manure from the capital's numerous crews, and as the horses of these crews are always kept clean, their manure is never consumed; Market gardeners remove it at least once a week, so that most of them bring in from one to three carts a day, which they place in millstones in their marshes, to use in winter for bedding. We have recognized by experience that the steam escaping from whole horse manure contains something harmful to young plants; we have seen young melon plants killed by this steam; the manure of geldings does not produce the same effect. But manure piled up in this way heats up, throws off its fire, as they say, and, after a month or so, it has lost its heat, dried out and is no longer new manure. Old manure. - From the end of May to November, market gardeners in Paris don't layer, yet they continue to receive from one to three cars of new manure a day, which they pile up in several stacks for later use. By remaining piled up in this way for six, five, four, three, two and one month, it loses its heat and its title of new manure, and takes on that of old manure. In November, we begin to make layers, and as new manure arrives daily, we warm up the old manure by mixing it with the new, watering it if necessary, and by this mixing we obtain layers whose heat is more moderate and lasts longer than if they were made with all new manure. Herser, náteler. - These two words are synonymous among market gardeners. We never use a harrow, but we harrow with a fork and rake: so, when we plough a bed with the intention of sowing it, the surface of the earth is never divided finely enough for the seed to spread evenly. If there are large clods, they are first broken up with a fork, then the rake is thrown in to finish breaking up what escaped the fork, and to bring back onto the path the small clods that serve to make it higher than the board, which is advantageous for watering in that the water is prevented from running off into the path. .Irrígation. -- It might be a good idea to irrigate many of our marshes, especially those where little or no bedding is done, but this practice has not yet been introduced into the market gardening of Paris. Gauge. - This is the name given to the kind of ditch that the ploughman must always have in front of him between the ploughed land and that which has not yet been ploughed. The gauge must be as deep as the ploughing and have a width of about 30 decimetres, 4 so that the worker can see if the spades of earth he is continually tipping over the other edge of the gauge are dividing properly by themselves, and to divide them himself with the spade if they are not sufficiently divided. For us, ploughing is synonymous with beaking. It means to stir up and turn over the soil with a spade to a depth of 18 to 26 centimetres, leaving a gauge in front of you and reversing your spade a little forward, breaking it up and dividing it as much as possible, always keeping it at the same level. As you can't make a bed too soft for the seeds and roots of plants, you must plough the land whenever you need to sow or plant it. If you plow when it's raining hard, the work doesn't go as well, in that the soil doesn't divide well, and even forms lumps which then harden and harm the delicate roots. If you plow when the soil is crusted by frost, the crusts you bury take a long time to thaw out and create cavities in the soil that are no less harmful than clods. Build up a layer. - Market gardeners in Paris, who make a lot of layers, are obliged to amass a large quantity of manure in advance, which they pile up in large heaps or "meules", to be used as needed. In November, which is the time when the first layers are made, a market gardener has two hundred cars of manure, piled up successively in four or six millstones from June until then: this manure has dried out and lost its first fire, due to the time it has remained in the millstones, and is called old manure, but, by stirring it, dividing it, wetting it and mixing it with new manure, it regains its heat. New manure is that which arrives daily in the marsh, for as long as the layers are made, and which does not remain more than 15 to 30 days in the millstone. This manure has not lost its heat, and warms up the old manure with which it is mixed by half, more or less, when making layers. To mount or to make a layer are synonymous, but the first term is more commonly used than the second. So, when you want to mount a layer on the ground, to put in panels, frames or bells, we advise you to always give it a width of 1 meter 65 centimeters (5 feet). The length depends on need and location. First, four small stakes are placed at the four corners of the square; a string is stretched from one stake to the other on each side, and a few stakes are planted along this length, to serve as a guide on one side, and as many on the other; Then bring a strong chain of old manure to the site, and another chain of new manure next to it, which you mix in roughly equal parts, starting at one end and placing it in equal thickness in forks on the site; you lay it down in ever-equal beds, raising the edges of the layer vertically, pressing and squeezing each forkful of manure with the fork. To make the edge of the layer cleaner and more solid, it is assembled in sonchées, i.e. manure forks folded in half, with their backs placed on the edge of the layer. The worker always works backwards; he turns to mix and take the manure behind him, to place it on the layer in front of him; finally, the art of assembling a layer well consists in mixing the manure well, putting an equal thickness of it everywhere, packing it well, raising the edges well perpesadicularly, taking care above all that some places do not sag more than others when the layer is loaded with terne or loam. As for the thickness or height of a layer, this depends on the season and the use to which it is to be put: layers vary in thickness from 40 to 66 centimetres (from 15 poucos to 2 feet); the degree of dryness or dampness of the manure is taken into account to water it, if necessary, and help it to ferment. Mouiller (synonym of arnoser). - The first of these terms is used more often than the second in the marshes of Paris. Nouer. - As long as the meshes or young fruits of a melon are no bigger than a pigeon's egg, they are liable to yellow and fall off; but when they have reached this size, if they are not to fall off, they grow rapidly: then they are said to be knotted. Mulch - This means covering one or more planks or an entire square with a bed of short, half-consumed manure, 3 to 4 centimetres thick, as evenly as possible, so that the soil is no longer visible. Mulch has the effect of keeping the soil moist, facilitating the soaking of water from watering, preventing evaporation and transferring nutrients to the soil for the benefit of the plants. Since mulch attracts moisture more than potting soil, it should not be used until the end of April. Pinching. - In this case, you need to return to the same plant eight or ten times to pinch off all the seeds. Panneauter, dépanneauter - The first of these terms means to put the frame panels on top of the layers; the second means to remove the frame panels from top of the layers when they are no longer needed: they are then stacked under a hanger away from the rain. Planting. - Planting means putting the roots of a plant in the ground under certain conditions. The market gardener's planting is one of the simplest and easiest; always working with very young plants, he is not concerned by the difficulties of planting large trees. First of all, he takes care to plumb the soil where he wants to plant, because he knows that stony or hollow soil is not conducive to the rapid recovery of plants. The market gardener has only two ways of planting: by hand and with a planter. The first is used only for melons, cucumbers, eggplants and tomatoes; the second applies to all other plants. Leading. - This means lowering the soil a little, so that the seeds and roots of young plants can attach themselves better. We always replace this word with "stomp" when we plumb our boards. We always plumb the soil under our frames, on our ados, and sometimes with the hand when we transplant very young plants under cloches. We plumb a newly sown bed by stomping on it before topdressing or mulching, i.e. by walking over it with very small steps, always clamping our feet so as to evenly flatten the whole surface of the bed. Finally, as we never use a chalk line in our marshes to trace the lines where lettuces, romaine, etc. are to be planted, it's still with our feet, stumbling, a market gardener's term, that we trace these lines. Pommer. - Cabbages and lettuces are said to "pomment" when, at a certain age, the inner leaves of these plants press strongly against each other and form a compact, rounded, oval or conical head. The enclosed leaves, deprived of air and light, are then white, more tender and better, according to our taste. To lower the air. - Partially or completely lowering frames or cloches that have been raised on one side, so that the plants they enclose are no longer hit by the air. Rake. - We use the two types of rake known to our marshes, the pull rake and the push rake: The first is used to rake or cut by the root weeds growing in our paths or other hard areas; the other is used, with great economy of time, to cut weeds growing in plantations of large vegetables, such as cabbages, cardoons and tomatoes; this rake also has the advantage of stirring the soil to a depth of 1 or a centimeters, preventing it from crusting or hardening, and preserving its attractive faculty. Rechaud, to heat. - A rechaud is made by surrounding a layer of well-pressed new manure, 4o to 60 centimeters wide and the height of the layer. It differs from accot in that it is made with new, hot manure, or manure that can heat up, warm up or maintain the heat of the layer. Remove air. - V. rabattre l'air. To turn over a layer - There's an ellipsis in this phrase, familiar to Paris marafchers; to convey their thought, we should say to turn over the compost of a layer. In fact, when a bed is empty, you plow it and turn over the soil to plant other vegetables, but you don't turn over the bed. Retransplant - When the months of November and December are mild, plants under cloches, on teens, grow too fast, and, to stop their growth, we unfold them to replant them on other teens, giving them a little more space; this retransplantation delays them by a fortnight. Route (putting bells on the road). - This expression may not be very accurate, and that of putting the bells in line would probably be more appropriate; but it is established in our marshes, and we have no mission to change it. It's usually in early June that the bells are no longer needed, and after they've been stuffed, they're put on the road, where they remain until October, the time of sowing on ados. The idea is to shelter two or three thousand bells for these three or four months; to do this, we choose a spot in the marsh that we don't have to take care of urgently, and which is sheltered from external shocks. On this spot, we spread a bed of unconsumed manure, 80 centimetres wide, 16 centimetres thick, and of a length relative to the number of bells we have to place there. The bells, already stuffed, are brought in in bundles; one bundle is placed on the manure bed, starting at one end; then a little litter is placed in the bottom bell of the first bundle. In this way, and in the same direction, you place two rows of bells, taking care to put fairly thick litter between the two rows: When these two rows are complete, place a 15-centimeter-thick bed of litter on top of them, and on this bed place a third row of bells weighing between the first two, and cover the whole with a large layer of litter thick enough to prevent rainwater from penetrating as far as the bells, because if it did, the straw would attach itself to the bells, and they could break when you tried to remove them. Season. - For the market gardener, season means harvest. If a seedling or plantation doesn't succeed, he'll say: I've lost a season; if it produces four harvests in two months, he'll say: Tai has produced four seasons in two months in this bed. Sarcler, ésherber. - These two terms are virtually synonymous in their meaning. Weeding means scraping the earth with a weeder, a small snake-shaped instrument, and cutting the roots of weeds while sparing the roots of the good ones; weeding means pulling out by hand the weeds growing among the good ones. Weeding is only carried out on thickly-sown grasses, such as sorrel, parsley and spinach, where the hoe could not be used without inconvenience: it is always women and children who weed in marshes, and they usually carry out these operations while picking the same grasses. Sowing. - To entrust seeds to the earth, so that they germinate and develop into plants. The Paris market gardener always plummets or sinks the soil in which he wants to sow seeds, because the roots establish themselves better there than in loose or hollow soil. He sows thickly or lightly, depending on his point of view: lightly, if the seedling is to remain in place; thickly, if the seedling is to be replanted. As for the depth at which the seeds should be buried, the market gardener follows the established rule on this subject. Pathway. - The necessity for us not to leave any uncultivated area in our marshes means that we never find any pathway worthy of the name. Paths 33 centimeters (1 foot) wide are enough for us, and we leave no empty space except for our manure dumps. Carpeting. - When melons planted under a frame begin to lengthen their arms, all the earth or potting soil under the frame is covered with mulch to keep the soil fresh and prevent the melon branches from touching it; this operation is called tapisser. Terreauter. - Spreading a bed of fine potting soil 12 to 15 millimeters thick over a sown bed or a bed to be planted. There are two ways to do this: 1° by throwing the loam obliquely over the soil: it takes skill and experience to spread it everywhere with the same thickness; 2° by depositing the loam in small etas from distance to distance on the bed, and using a shovel or rake to spread it as evenly as possible. The effect of loam is to prevent the soil from drying out, splitting or cracking, and to prevent it from giving up its nutrients to the soil during watering. As it attracts less moisture than mulch, loam is used until the end of April, when late frosts are no longer a threat. Torchée, to make a torchée. - To make a torchée, take a forkful of manure, fold it in half and apply it back-side down to the edge of a rising layer. The entire circumference of an isolated layer must be made into a torchée, so that it can rise perpendicularly without burrs. Twist. - A word used only to refer to onions when the bulge at their base is determined. When this bulge occurs well and on time, we say: the onion turns well; when it occurs badly, we say: the onion turns badly or does not turn. Heavy rains prevent the onion from turning and cause it to remain on the cob. Tracer. - Making lines along the length of a bed to sow or plant vegetables. Market gardeners in Paris don't use chalk lines, tracers or sticks to draw these lines or furrows, which they call "rayons"; they draw them with their feet, walking regularly with their feet apart so as to make two "rayons" at a time. This method has the advantage, in addition to saving time, of sealing the soil where the seed is to be sown or planted, an advantage not always felt by other gardeners. CHAPTER IX. The habits and way of life of Paris market gardeners. To speak of market gardeners is to speak of ourselves; and it is rather difficult to speak of oneself when one has much good to say about it: however, as the nature of our work obliges us to speak of the habits and manners of market gardeners, we are going to say them frankly, effacing ourselves as much as we can. The market gardeners of Paris form the most laborious, the most constant and the most peaceful of all the classes of workers in the capital. No matter how hard, no matter how painful the job, the market gardener never leaves it to take up another. The sons of a market gardener become accustomed to the work, under the wishes and example of their father, and almost all become market gardeners. Daughters rarely marry a man from a profession other than that of their father. Although the trade is very hard, the market gardener is attached to it; however much he toils and watches, it never seems too hard; even when the inclemency of the seasons thwarts his plans, he flatters himself with the prospect of being happier another time; he never despairs of Providence. We are even convinced that it is to the confidence they have in Providence that the market gardeners of Paris owe the tranquility and harmony that exist among them. The springs that stir the passions of other men are unknown to them; their sole ambition is to seek the means to be the first to bring early produce to the market; such an ambition will certainly never disturb public safety..... It's far from the case that the market gardening class remains routine and stationary, as is generally O8 believed. Market gardeners keep pace with the progress and improvements of the century; their well-being and ease of living increase in proportion to the breadth of their intelligence and the soundness of their reasoning. Barely forty years ago, market gardeners were poorly housed, poorly clothed; they ate poorly; almost all of them carried vegetables to the market on their backs; they drew water from their wells by rope and by force of arm. Today's market gardeners are better dressed, they eat better, almost all of them have a horse and carriage to take the vegetables to the market and bring in the manure; instead of drawing water by hand, market gardeners generally have a pump or manege that provides abundant water. But if the market gardener has improved his existence, if he eats better, if he is better clothed than before, if he has even become the owner of his marsh, it's because he works more, he works better, and above all with more intelligence than before. For seven months of the year, the market gardener works eighteen and twenty hours out of twenty-four, and during the other five months, the winter months, he works fourteen and sixteen hours a day, and very often still, he gets up at night to check his thermometer, to double the covers of the bells and frames that contain his dearest hopes, his future, which one degree of frost can wipe out. O8 For the past twenty or thirty years, market gardeners' intelligence has been particularly focused on ways of forcing nature to produce, in the middle of winter, in the midst of frost, what, in its ordinary course, it only produces in the fine days of spring or summer, and it is in this that the science of Paris market gardeners has become truly astonishing. As early as November, and often as early as October, they provide white asparagus for consumption, and green asparagus almost all year round; in January, head lettuces in abundance; in February, romaine; in March, new carrots, turnips, radishes and new chervil, strawberries, etc.; in April, tomatoes, beans, melons, etc. Before the introduction of forced cultivation in the Paris marshes, the market-gardener class, always respectable for its usefulness and purity of morals, was held in low esteem: The market gardener with a reputation for skill in the cultivation of early vegetables often sees a crew at his door, and people of considerable rank and fortune come down to chat with him, consider his work, study the practice with him, and ask him for advice or information to pass on to their gardener. We won't go into what O8 the market-gardening class stands to gain from such communications, but merely hope that they will become more and more frequent. A market-gardening establishment, like many others, can scarcely prosper without a woman: while the man cultivates the marsh and makes it produce, only the woman knows how to make the most of its produce; so a young market-gardener seeking to establish himself begins by getting married. One receives the title of master, the other that of mistress. If they don't receive a ready-made marsh as a dowry, the beginnings are hard for both; for, however small a marsh may be, the first expenses are considerable: They have to take on hired help; they have to feed and bed them, which doesn't exempt the master and mistress from being the first and last to work; they have to set up boxes, frames and bells; finally, they have to make a considerable pile of manure, and it's only when they have all this at their discretion and to hand that our young market gardeners can work with the hope of some profit. But the love of work is so inherent to the market gardening chaece, and the work itself, though violent and prolonged, is apparently so salutary, that we rarely see a young establishment fail to prosper. The master market gardener is always at the head of his boys, and the mistress is always at the head of her women: while the men plough, D8 plant, water, layer, place bells and frames, the women are in constant activity; they weed, they pick sorrel, oerfeil, spinach, lamb's lettuce, they bind romaine, etc., etc., etc., etc. If their part in a maraia seems less arduous than that of the men, it may well be moina sainc; forr women are, by their labors, for part of the day, on their knees or half lying on the often humaide earth, and this frequently results in more or less painful chills for them. In the evenings, while the men work outside well into the night, the women prepare and assemble the tracks, hoods, mannes and mannettes for the next day's hall. It is here, in the preparation and arrangement of vegetables, that the mistress's taste and skill prove superior to that of the master. The next day, at two o'clock in the morning in summer, four o'clock in winter, everyone is up; the mistress sets off for the market with her cart of manchandises, helped by the girl or a boy. If certain vegetables, such as cauliflowers, melons or others, are in abundance, one or two other cars are sent the same night. The interest on the sale is copied to the wife; by the same token, all the money from sales throughout the year necessarily passes through her hands. For the business to prosper, the market gardener must have complete confidence in his wife, and she must never abuse it. The simplicity and purity of morals of the market-gardener class, and their constant desire to do business honorably, are a sufficient guarantee against anything that might disturb the harmony of the household. As soon as they can handle the spade, the children alternate study with work; at the age of twelve, to encourage them, their fathers give them a piece of land where they cultivate for themselves what they consider most profitable. There they make use of their young experience, they help themselves from what they have seen done and from what they have done themselves on behalf of their father, and as, while their plantation grows and grows, they always hear about economy from their father and mother, they become accustomed to not spending the proceeds from the sale of their small crops unnecessarily, and so today many children of market gardeners, from the age of thirteen to fifteen, already have savings placed in the savings bank. In a market-gardening establishment, everyone rises before daylight, so we eat at seven in the morning while we work; lunch at ten; dinner at two; and supper from eight to nine in the evening, depending on the season. Master and mistress, children, hired girls and boys eat together at the same table. Respect and decency are rigorously observed; no equivocal or unseemly words are ever uttered in front of the children; so the boys acquire the habit of being reserved in their words, and refrain from the excesses that are rightly criticized among workers of other classes. The master never takes a haughty tone with his boys, remembering that he was once a boy himself. His authority is felt only in directing the work and ensuring that everything is done properly. We know no jealous rivalry; we know only lively, commendable emulation; we show genuine interest in each other, and frank friendship. Too numerous to gather together every year to celebrate our patron saint, Saint Fiacre, we divide ourselves into several brotherhoods. For a fee, each brotherhood has its church decorated and adorned. The scent of flowers mingles with that of incense. A mass is sung to music, and the priest calls down heavenly blessings on the work of the gardeners. After the church ceremony, each brotherhood gathers for a banquet, often followed by a ball, but which ceases as soon as it's time to leave for the market. The gardeners appoint commissioners among themselves to ensure good order, and these commissioners rarely need to show their authority. We never see old market gardeners resorting to public charity, as we see so many examples in many other classes. It's not, however, that all market gardeners are able to save themselves from need in their old age; but they are so accustomed to working that they don't conceive of living by any means other than work: so those who have been unable to save, who have no family, or who have experienced misfortune - and who never does in life! will, for a small wage, offer their services to their more fortunate colleagues, and the latter will always meet them, and always make it their duty to welcome them and keep them busy to the best of their ability, 10 CHAPTERX. Muratohere cultivation in Parte, month for month. We have now arrived at the most important part of our work, the one that has never been dealt with specifically by any professional gardener: if a few authors have spoken of it, they have done so only incompletely, and by assimilating it to vegetable growing, from which it differs considerably in its maneuvering, its means and its results. Of all the differences between these two crops, we'll just mention this one: in vegetable-growing, most vegetables cost more than you'd buy them for at the market, while in fresh-growing, our vegetables must of necessity cost less than we sell them for. Before we begin, we need to make a few important observations, to avoid any misunderstanding of the plan we have followed in this book. 1° Market gardening in Paris does not include the cultivation of all the vegetables known to gardens; there are even some estixoés that we do not cultivate, for the sole reason that land is too expensive in Paris and we could never sell them at a profit. 2° We don't grow certain vegetables, because they're not to everyone's taste and their sale is uncertain; that's why we don't grow potatoes, peaches or baselle. If one day, as tastes change, these vegetables come to be sought after at the market, then we'll grow them. 3° Although in this book we shall deal only with market gardening as it is practised in Paris, we shall not neglect to talk about certain vegetables that we do not usually grow, because land is too expensive in Paris; but we shall do so in less detail than when talking about our own cultivation. 4° It might have seemed natural to some people that we should begin our Manual in January, but our work doesn't fit in with the solar year; we move faster than the seasons in winter. When the sun returns to warm our hemisphere, we already have several vegetables that have not waited for its return to grow, perfect and become fit for consumption. 4 Although our work has, so to speak, neither beginning nor end, there is nevertheless a time when we begin to work by remote forecast, and this time is the month of August; it is then that the horticultural year really begins for the market gardeners of Paris, and we believe O8 to act naturally by beginning our Practical Manual of Vegetable Cultivation with the month of August, and continuing it month by month up to and including July of the following year. 5° In order to avoid repetition when explaining market gardening, we have defined all our operations in alphabetical order in chapter VIII. So, when, for example, we say: ploughing, tilling, bedding, edging, etc., we won't repeat the explanation of these operations, since you've already read them or can read them in the chapter indicated. 6° We also have a double observation to make: the same vegetable can be sown or planted in several different seasons, and we cultivate genera that contain several species that require sowing and planting at very different times: However, if we had described our cultivation rigorously month by month, we would have been obliged to repeat more or less the same thing in several months, and consequently on pages that are sometimes very far apart, forcing the reader to undertake research that would be as tiring as it would be tedious. To avoid this inconvenience, when a type of vegetable has several species, we 0 plant them upright, and with the same planter we tighten it well with earth on the side. This last operation serves to secure the plant to the soil, and is called "bornering". Its effect is to prevent the plant from being laid down by earthworms, which draw its leaves into their holes, to facilitate its recovery and to make it more resistant to frosts. The distance between each plant in a transplant bed should be 8 to 10 centimetres. The white onion is not afraid of frost; it can withstand the harshest winters, but it fears false thaws. White onions transplanted or planted in November can be sold from May 1st to 15th. You can also leave the young plant to overwinter in the bed where it was sown, and only transplant it when the severe frosts have passed. When, in May, the white onion has grown to about the size of a hen's egg, it is pulled up, bundled and sent to the market; but market gardeners who have a lot of land let some of it turn, grow and ripen, because white onions, being smaller and sweeter than red onions, are more sought-after in good homes. In light, dry soils, onions require frequent watering when the weather turns dry; in rich soils, they should not be over-watered, as they could turn fat, i.e. melt or rot. O8 In vegetable gardens, where it is customary to bury a lot of manure, it is advisable not to plant onions on manure; but, as market gardeners never bury manure, we do not indicate this precaution for our marshes. SMALL WHITE ONION. CULTIVATION. - The small white onion is neither a different species nor a different variety; it is the same species as the previous one, kept smaller by a different culture. Instead of sowing it in August and transplanting it in November, we sow it from February to June, and do not transplant it. As for ploughing, training, sealing and topdressing, it's the same as above; but we sow more lightly, because small onions don't transplant, so they need space to form or turn on the spot. If the soil is dry, the hotter the season, the less you need to spare the soil for wetting. However, onions should not be over-watered, as in rainy years they have difficulty turning, and most remain on the chives. The small white onion is much sought-after by restaurateurs and home cooks; we pull it out and sell it in bunches, when it is as big as a walnut and even smaller, as soon as its tail (i.e., its leaves) begins to wilt: 500 grams (1 pound) of onion seed is enough to sow 112 meters of surface. RED ONION. Paris market gardeners don't grow red onions or any of their varieties, because they're cheap and the rent for our marshes is very high: although we don't grow them, we'll say a few words about them. In vegetable gardens, the custom is to take advantage of a few fine days, at the end of February or the beginning of March, to sow the red onion in a sheltered bed, and when the plant is strong enough, in April and May, to prick it out in the previous year's smoked soil, 15 or 16 centimetres apart; it is hoeed, weeded and watered during the summer; If it doesn't turn fast enough in September, we cut off the leaves with the back of a rake to ripen it; when we judge it ripe, we pull it out, leaving it to dry on the spot if the weather is fine; if it rains, we lay it on a hard path, and, when it's dry, we greenhouse it to protect it from frost for the winter. Red onions are also sown in squares, to let them ripen in place in vegetable gardens: they don't come as large, but they are usually fuller and firmer, and are valued more highly for many culinary preparations. 99 A plant of the same family and genus as the onion, from which it differs only in that it does not bulge at the base and does not form an onion proper: it has the same flavor, but to a lesser degree, and does not rise as high. CULTIVATION. - Spring onions are sown from the end of February to the end of March, in soil that has been ploughed, harrowed, plumbed and top-dressed in the same way as onions; and, as the spring onion we grow does not need to be transplanted, its seed is sown as lightly as that of the small white onion. We usually sow spring onions in 55-centimeter-wide beds, and as early as March we plant romaine in the open ground, when the spring onion seed is sown and potted, we plant eight to nine rows of romaine in the same bed, which, growing its leaves straight or almost vertically, does not prevent the spring onion from growing. Both are properly wetted, the bed is kept clean, and the romaine is ready to be sent to the market before the chives are half grown, i.e. around May 20, about two months after planting. By July zo, the chives are about as big as your little finger : This is the way chives are grown by the market gardeners of Paris; But in the kitchen garden, it is left in place longer, and each plant forms several cloves that clump together in the spring; these cloves are pulled out, replanted one by one, and each of them forms a new clump of chives; so there's no need to sow seed every year. CIBOULETTE, CIVETTE, APPÉTIS. A plant of the same family and genus as the onion: it is perennial, very tough, and grows to a height of only 10 to 15 centimeters: its main use is as a salad dressing. CULTIVATION-This plant, being of fairly limited use, has very little place in market gardening: as it is perennial and proliferates a lot, when you have a few plants, you neglect to collect the seeds and sow them, because by removing the old clumps you find enough planting material to make a new plantation or increase the one you already have. They are usually planted in borders, in shade or half-shade, in fresh, substantial soil, where they require no care other than to be kept free of weeds for four or six years, after which they are replanted, either in full rows or in small clumps. When they are needed, their small leaves, which are like burrs, are cut off just above the ground, and new ones grow; they do not fear the strongest frosts. SPINACH. Plant of the Chenopoda family, annual, 30 to 60 centimeters high, twining, with simple, hastate leaves: the flowers are small, dioecious, greenish; the fruit is formed from the hardened calyx and contains a single seed. In horticulture, the fruit is mistaken for the seed: there are two types or varieties; the leaves alone are edible. ROUND-SEEDED SPINACH OR HOLLAND SPINACH. CULTIVATION. - This spinach is sown from August a0 to August 30; it should not be sown too thickly. One liter of seed is enough to sow 224 shallow meters. The beds where it is to be sown should not be ploughed deeply, as the spinach root likes to meet firm ground: when the seed is broadcast, we harrow it and bury it with a fork, then press it down with our feet, rake it to even out the soil, and finish by spreading a light mulch of very short manure over the whole sowing area, so that it doesn't harm the seed when it rises, which in this season happens after six or seven days. The soil should be kept moist with frequent, small drenches until the seed has emerged, and when it has, the small drenches should be continued as long as the season is dry. It goes without saying that broadcast spinach cannot be hoeed or raked; it must be weeded by hand. Soon the spinach has reached a height of 16 centimeters: then, just this once, all the leaves are cut off 3 centimeters from the ground with a knife: this is the first harvest for the market. If the weather is dry at this time, the leaves are wetted to turn them into sap. It doesn't take long for the spinach to sprout more leaves, and when they are of a certain size, three or four fingers wide, the women pick them one by one by hand, leaving the heart or small leaves in the center. Spinach from this mahière is harvested until the following spring, when it starts to go to seed; then it is destroyed, if we don't want to spare some for seed, and the soil is prepared for other vegetables. We never sow spinach in rays in hos marais, even though they are usually sown in this way in vegetable gardens, because if we sowed them in rays, they would become too thin; whereas when they are sown broadcast, each plant has much more room, and their leaves become more beautiful, larger, greener, and are better nourished. SPINACH WITH PRICKLY SEED, COMMON OR ANGLE SPINACH. CULTIVATION. - We sow this spinach from the first days of February until May; after this time, hot weather makes it thin and yellow; spinach needs a moderate temperature, more humid than dry. Only two harvests are possible from seedlings sown in May; it often sets seed after the first. This type of spinach tolerates hot weather a little better than the round-seeded variety; it yellows less quickly, and its leaves are broader and a little indented. SEPTEMBER. CHOU. Plant of the cruciferous family and the genus whose name it bears. This genus contains many species or varieties, all of which can be used for human and animal consumption; but since humans have chosen a certain number of them, most to their liking, it is only these that we need to talk about; we won't even mention them all, because market gardeners in the interior of Paris can and should only give their attention to those species that they are sure to sell with a chance of profit, without fearing too much competition from outside. Cabbages are generally biennial plants, rising from 70 centimetres to 2 metres or more when they flower; their stems are straight, thick, fleshy and stalky when they are ready to flower; but until then, they are all distinguished by their numerous, large, embossed, whole or variously cut leaves, spread out in a rosette in the case of green cabbages, or wrapped around each other to form a very compact head in the case of glaucous cabbages. Cauliflower, however, has a different way of forming its head, which we'll explain in a moment. Cauliflower flowers are fairly large, yellow or whitish, always divided into four or crosses, which is why their class is called cruciferous; these flowers are followed by twisted siliques containing several seeds. Some cauliflowers eat the aborted flowers, others the leaves, and three others the bottom or top of the stem, thickened into a turnip. We point out here that all cabbages like good, moist soil, and that if the soil in which York cabbage is to be planted is light and dry, it should be ploughed in with a good thickness of cow manure; if, on the other hand, it is strong and compact, it should be ploughed in with horse manure. We say this only for marshes where there is little or no forced cultivation; for, in those where forced cultivation is the main business, the soil is always good. YORK CAU. This is the earliest and smallest species, with an elongated head or head. CULTIVATION. - York cabbage is sown from the first days of September, in a piece of bed prepared for the purpose and kept wet if the weather is dry. When the plant has two leaves, one or two beds are ploughed, levelled, (1) harrowed with a fork, and covered with a light bed of compost; the young plant is then lifted with a spade, pulled slightly out of the earth, and transplanted with a planter at a distance of 10 or 13 centimetres (4 or 5 inches) into these new beds, and watered immediately to help it recover. This young plant remains in the nursery until it's time to put it in place, which happens in the last days of November and the beginning of December. Here's how we proceed. When the square where the York sprouts are to be planted is well ploughed, we lay them out in planks 2 meters 33 centimeters (7 feet) wide, leaving a path 32 centimeters wide between each plank, and we harrow them with a fork. In each plank, 2 meters 33 centimeters (7 feet) wide, the feet are used to trace nine rays 6 to 8 centimeters (2 to 3 inches) deep, so that the cabbages will find shelter during the winter, especially if the rays are directed from east to west. Once this is done, we go to the York cabbage nursery, lift each cabbage with a planter, in order to spare its roots and keep a small clump, if possible; and, when we've lifted a certain number and put them in a basket, we plant them with the planter in the rays, 33 centimetres apart and in a chessboard, so that they have more space. But here's a care that we always take in hos marais and that is neglected elsewhere: when planting cabbages at the start of winter, the planter (if he's not left-handed) must always have the right side facing south, and here's why; when the young cabbage has its roots and its entire stem placed in the hole prepared for it with the planter, you bind it there by pressing the soil to the right of the hole against it with the planter, resulting in a dimple next to the cabbage. During the winter, snow and ice accumulate in this dimple; but, as it faces south, the snow and ice are soon melted, whereas if the dimple faced north, they would not melt, and it is an established opinion among Paris market gardeners that the cabbages would suffer. In this late season, we choose a good day to do this planting, and when it's finished, we water each cabbage plant, to bring the soil down between its roots and help it recover. Once York cabbage has been planted, we don't give it any particular care throughout the winter: however, false thaws can damage it like other plants. It easily withstands 9 degrees centigrade of cold without suffering, and if a more intense cold damages it, it perishes through the collar, close to the ground. If in March and April there is a frost which hardens the soil, it is a good idea to hoe it deeply and give the cabbages a few wettings: they are ready for consumption at the end of April and the beginning of May, or even earlier, if you have been able to plant a few in a coastal area. SUGARLOAF CABBAGE. CULTIVATION. - This species is sown, transplanted and planted at the same times as the previous one, and cultivated in the same way; but, as it grows stronger, instead of putting nine rows in a bed 2 meters 33 centimeters wide, only seven rows are put in, and they are spaced 48 centimeters apart in the rows. Its head is larger and longer than that of the York cabbage, and later by eighteen or twenty days. COEUR-DE-ROEUF CABBAGE. This third variety is the largest and latest of the early cabbages: it is sown at the same time as the two preceding varieties, and planted and grown in the same way; but only six rows should be planted in a bed 2 meters 53 centimeters wide, with the cabbages 65 centimeters apart in the rows. Its head forms eight to fifteen days later than that of sugarloaf cabbage. CONICAL POMERANIAN CABBAGE. This species, still little cultivated in Paris, where it is perhaps not sufficiently appreciated, is nevertheless very interesting for its excellent quality, which is not at all inferior to that of the three cabbages just mentioned, to which it still resembles by its conical head, but much stronger; it has not yet been introduced into market gardening. CULTIVATION - Attempts have been made to sow Pomeranian cone cabbage in September, at the same time as York and beef cabbages, but it has not given satisfactory results in this way. It is recognized as one of those plants that should be sown in spring, from late March to early May; it is not essential to transplant it in a nursery. Planted in rows, at a distance of 48 centimeters and with ordinary care, its apple can be picked in September, and, if left to grow until November, it becomes very hard and can acquire a weight of 8 to 10 kilograms. The only criticism that can be made of this excellent cabbage is that it degenerates easily. You can't take too much care when it's flowering to preserve its clean seed. STUBBY BRUNSWICK CABBAGE. This cabbage is grown by market gardeners who are farthest from the center of the capital and who produce little or no early produce: it can be recognized by its stalk, which is so short that its head appears to be resting on the ground; this head is medium-sized and flattened at the top. The species is robust, endures the cold and is only eaten in winter. CULTIVATION. - Brunswick cabbage is sown in spring, is not transplanted and, when the plant is strong enough, is planted in a row around beds containing smaller vegetables, spacing the plants 66 cm apart in the row; there they benefit from the cultivation and watering given to the plants in the bed. CABBAGE, WHITE CABBAGE. This is the cabbage that makes the largest head of all the cabbages; to have it in all its size, it must be sown in autumn, and it becomes good to eat as early as July. Paris market-gardeners don't grow it, because it occupies the land too long, because a considerable quantity is grown in the surrounding area and sent to the capital's markets from several kilometres away, and because its price would not cover the cost of growing it in Paris: although it is widely consumed, it rarely appears on opulent tables, because its quality is far inferior to that of our small early cabbages. 111 This is a variety of cabbage. Some market-gardeners, near the surrounding wall where land is cheaper, grow a little red cabbage, not because it is to everyone's taste, because of the musky flavor that many people find in it, but because some doctors prescribe it for certain illnesses. It is grown in the same circumscribed way as white cabbage. Both cabbage and white cabbage fear a hard frost when they are plucked: in this case, they are covered with litter, or uprooted, placed in a greenhouse and, after a few days, all the leaves that have not been plucked are removed; they are placed side by side, and keep very well and for a long time. CABBAGE WITH LARGE RIBS. This cabbage, like the next one, belongs to the section of cabbages that have little or no head: it has large, full, glaucous leaves, remarkable for their large, whitish ribs; it is sown in spring and eaten in late autumn and winter. Some market gardeners in Paris grow a small amount of this cabbage for their own use, but they hardly ever send any to the market, and all those seen there come from the small-scale cultivation around Paris. BRUSSELS SPROUTS. Also known as spray cabbage and rosette cabbage, it belongs to the section of green cabbages that do not produce apples, and is the only known cabbage to have the singularity of producing small apples all along its stem. CULTIVATION. - Brussels sprouts are sown in April like Savoy cabbages, and are no more difficult to cultivate; sowing them a little early saves the trouble of transplanting them: while they are growing, they are prone to flea beetles, as are all crucifers sown in spring and summer; but this insect is kept at bay by frequent watering and by other means, which we will describe in the chapter on Insects. Brussels sprouts, like all other cabbages, prefer fairly strong, fertile, fresh soil; but they also do well in less substantial soil. When the plant is strong enough, it is planted in a square, in rows spaced 48 centimetres apart (the plants are 64 centimetres apart in the rows), and watered immediately. But in market gardening, we hardly ever plant vegetables in full squares; we divide our entire plot into a-meter 55-centimeter-wide beds, leaving a 33-centimeter path between them for service purposes; and those of us who grow Brussels sprouts plant a row of them on each edge of our beds, placing the plants about 1 meter apart, so that they don't interfere with the vegetables in the bed. This cabbage soon lengthens its stem and sheds its lower leaves, making room for the surrounding plants. By September, it is 4o to 80 centimetres high, and from all the axils of the fallen or evergreen leaves spring rosettes of small leaves, which are topped with small apples, which can be picked from October and throughout the winter, as Brussels sprouts are frost-resistant. These small apples, about the size of walnuts, are highly sought-after for opulent tables, and sell at a fairly high price. There are two equally good varieties of Brussels sprouts; one grows to a height of just 50 centimetres, the other to 1 metre: one and ★I ~~2 the other are prone to degeneration, if allowed to 7~ flower near other cabbages. ~~~ ~~ ~ ~t t-~- a Meta. ~~ ~~ ~~~ ~~ ~~ ~~ ~~ ~~ ~~ KALE. ~~ ~L ~ ~~~~ ~~ ~~ ~~ au Mhiaaro ~~ile-adye There are many varieties of savoy cabbage, which can be recognized by the height of the stalk, the size and embossing of the leaves, and the size and shape of the head; for, although they belong to the green cabbage section, savoy cabbages are very tasty. CULTIVATION. - Savoy cabbage is grown only by market gardeners in the Vaugirard, Grenelle and Gros-Caillou districts. It can be sown as early as March and April, but market gardeners only sow it at the end of June, to plant it at the end of July, when they have already harvested one or two crops. They plant two rows per bed, one row on each edge near the pathway; and these cabbages do not harm the other plants of the season, which are sown or planted in the bed, such as lettuce, lamb's lettuce, spinach, chervil, etc. Savoy cabbage likes water like the others, but it should be watered only sparingly, so that it doesn't grow too fast, as it needs to be frosted before it can develop all its qualities; it is therefore much better than white or curly cabbage, which often has a musky taste that we don't always like. Curly Savoy cabbage degenerates easily, and every year you have to choose the most honest plants as seedlings. We haven't mentioned Milan-des-Vertus cabbage, the largest of the kites, or a few others, because they are not grown by market gardeners in the interior of Paris. CHOU-FLEUR. This species of cabbage differs from the others in that it is not its leaves that form its head, but rather its flowers, which, before they develop, turn into a compact mass of white, fleshy, tender granules, with a delicaf taste. When this mass or head is fully developed, reaching up to 16 or 20 centimetres in diameter in a convex form, if it is not cut off, several branches emerge, developing flowers that are partly imperfect, partly perfect; and the latter produce siliques whose seeds reproduce the species. Fifty years ago, it was thought that cauliflower seed harvested in France could not produce beautiful cauliflowers, and it was all imported from England. Nowadays, every market gardener collects his own seed, even sells some, and is used to obtaining very beautiful and good cauliflowers. GROWN ON THE FARM. - The most suitable cauliflower for forced cultivation is the one previously known as tender cauliflower; but for some time now, early market gardeners have been recognizing in tender cauliflower a more precocious variety or race, which they have named petit salomon. Ten years ago, one of us found another breed or variety in our fields, larger and almost as early as the petit salomon, and gave it the name gros salomon. Anticipating that the name tender cauliflower will gradually disappear, to be replaced by petit and gros salomon, we are conforming here to what will infallibly happen. So, instead of saying that we sow tender cauliflower, we say that we sow small and large Solomon's at the same time, and that the product of the large immediately follows the product of the small. Small and large Solomon's that are to be harvested in spring are sown in the open ground, from September 5 to 10. The soil in which they are to be sown must be ploughed and harrowed with a fork; the seed is sown into the soil, then harrowed again with a fork, then raked in; finally, a 2-centimeter-thick bed of fine compost is spread over the whole. Immediately give the soil a good wetting, and keep it moist until the seed has emerged, which in this season happens in less than eight days. When the seedling has fully emerged, and is beginning to have two leaves, not including the cotyledons, we plough more planks and place chests close to the ploughing, then harrow the soil in the chests, and cover it with a 4-centimeter-thick bed of potting soil. When the potting soil is well spread out, it is pressed down firmly with the planting board; then the cauliflower plant is transplanted. To lift the plant, it's always a good idea to wet it one or two hours in advance; then, when you want to pick it up, lift it by passing a spade under the roots; by taking these precautions, the plant pulls itself out of the ground with all its roots; a little soil remains between its rootlets, which helps it to recover more quickly when transplanted. When a certain number of seedlings have been lifted in this way, they are transplanted into the trunks. You hold a handful of seedlings in your left hand; with the pointer finger of your right hand, you make a perpendicular hole in the soil, proportionate to the plant's root and stem. Using the left hand, push the seedling through to the leaves, and mark it off with the same pointer finger of the right hand. This first time, the seedlings are planted about 7 or 9 centimetres apart, or in such a way as to fit between one hundred and fifty and two hundred and fifty of them under the span of a frame panel, or, which amounts to the same thing, between four hundred and fifty and seven hundred and fifty seedlings in a three-panel box. After planting, the seedling should be wetted as soon as possible, so that it can be quickly attached to the soil; and if the weather is still hot and dry, it should be properly supported by wetting. By the end of November, the plant has become strong and hardened; but it must not grow too fast. So we plough in other boards, place other trunks in them, prepare the soil as before, and retransplant the same plant, spacing it out a little more or so that there are about fifty less under each board, depending on its strength, and pushing it down to the leaves. The purpose of this second planting is to stunt the plant's growth and harden it at the same time, so that it is better able to withstand the cold. As long as it doesn't freeze, the plant should remain in the open air, i.e. the frames should only be put on the boxes containing it when it starts to freeze. Then, during the day, the frames are held open at the back by means of wooden wedges cut in such a way as to raise the back of the frames from 2 to ro centimetres, and, in the evening, the air is removed by removing the wedges. If the frost becomes severe, we place straw mats on the frames; if it increases, we make an aecot around the trunk; if it increases again, we double and triple the straw mats; finally, we make sure that the frost cannot reach the plant, and that it is protected without any artificial heat. When the sun is shining, the straw mats are removed, so that the plant can enjoy the light: if the weather is mild, a little air is let in during the day; but when the sun is shining, the air must be pulled back and covered, for fear of accidents. Moreover, there are hardly any market gardeners, among those who grow forced crops, who, during the winter, don't get up almost every harsh night to check the thermometer and double the covers of their bells and frames, if they deem it necessary. In certain winters, cauliflower plants are sometimes forced to remain under their frames for more than a month, without air or light: this long obscurity softens the plant and makes it more sensitive to the effects of air and light; this is why, once the season of heavy frosts has passed, air and light should be given only very sparingly, and only gradually increased. In the first days of February, plant one part of this cauliflower plant in place, and reserve the other for planting a month later, as we'll be saying shortly. In February, early market gardeners already have several layers that have yielded lettuce by means of boxes and frames, and the lettuces are replaced by cauliflowers in the following way: first, the frames are removed and the boxes left in place; these layers are turned over, i.e. the soil in the boxes is ploughed up; then the cauliflowers are planted. We have already mentioned that small and large Solomons are sown and planted at the same time; both are planted with the planter, in two rows under each frame panel, one row from below, zo or za centimetres from the wood, and one row from above, at equal distance from the wood of the trunk; if it's a small Solomon, we put four feet of it in each row, because it doesn't come large; if it's large salomon, we put only three feet in each row, and between the two rows of cauliflower, large or small, we plant three rows of lettuce gotte to six per row; as soon as the place of a panel is planted, we wet the plant to attach it to the ground and we put the panel back on immediately. We must warn you that there are early growers who, when planting cauliflowers, sow early carrots, plant Georges lettuce, etc., but these plants never do very well, and we are convinced that gotte lettuce is more profitable than any other vegetable while the cabbages are growing. Cabbages and lettuces are given air every day, as much as possible, and are covered at night if frost is to be feared; the air is increased little by little: soon the cabbages are close to touching the glass of the frames; then the boxes are raised by placing large straw pads under the four corners. Once the end of March or the beginning of April arrives, we take advantage of mild weather to remove the boxes and frames, and use them to grow melons. Cauliflowers laid bare in this way from the first days of April may still be exposed to late frosts and hail: to avoid these inconveniences, careful market gardeners set up rows of gaulettes on their cauliflowers, to receive straw mats in case of need. As the weather warms and the D8 cabbages grow, it's important to increase the wetting. By April 10 or 12, you should see apples forming on the petit salomon, and eight days later on the gros salomon; this is when you should start visiting the cauliflowers every other day, and as soon as you see an apple as big as a hen's egg, you should break off a few of the cabbage's lower leaves and apply them exactly to the apple, so as to deprive it of air and light, so that it retains all its whiteness. As you grow, the head becomes more or less uncovered; in another visit, you cover it by breaking off and dropping onto the head the large leaves that surround it. Finally, cauliflowers need to be visited as often to see if the heads are good to pick as to cover them when they start to show. A head is considered good to pick as soon as it begins to loosen, or flake in market gardener's terms: from this point onwards, the cauliflower decreases in value, in terms of appearance, although it still retains its good quality. Harvesting of small and large salomons, grown as described above, can last from April 25 to May 25. SPRING CAULIFLOWER. This cauliflower is not a specific species; it is either the semi-hard cauliflower or the hard cauliflower, or both, which we plant in the open ground at this time of year. CULTIVATION. - These cabbages had to be sown at the same time, and treated and cared for throughout the winter in exactly the same way as the forced-growth cabbages we've just been talking about; only, having remained in place for about a month longer, and having enjoyed air and light for longer, they are stronger when planted in the ground, than the others were when planted in trays. At the end of February and beginning of March, they are planted in the open ground; they are first planted on the coast to protect them from the cold and to keep them warm, then in beds, in the middle of the marsh, when there is no longer any fear of heavy frosts, and always at the same time as romaine. PĹANTATION EN COTRRE. - In a cótièré with a width of z meters 33 centimeters, where twelve rows of romaine are to be planted (see soil preparation, in the Romaine article), leave two or three of these rows empty, without planting them in romaine: if you decide on two rows, choose the second one on each side; if you prefer three rows of cauliflowers, leave a third row in the middle of the side, and when the romaine is planted as described above, plant the two or three rows of cauliflowers 2 feet apart in the row, with the precautions we'll indicate. When the cabbages have been lifted from the nursery with a hoe and pulled out of the ground, those that are one-eyed or have no heart, those that have protuberances at the crown, because these protuberances almost always contain insect eggs or larvae, those whose roots are damaged or in poor condition, and those that don't look good, must be culled. Once the choice has been made, we plant the cauliflowers in the rows reserved for them among the Romaines, 66 centimetres apart, taking care to press them in right up to the leaves, to line them up well and to water them to attach them to the soil. Little needs to be done to these cauliflowers until the end of April, when the soil begins to warm up in the rows more than in the garden: if, then, the sun is favorable, they can be watered amply; it may even happen that, in the first days of May, the cauliflowers need to be given a sprinkling of water, per three feet, twice a week as their heads start to form; and finally the heads are covered, as we said in the previous article, to keep them white, and most of them will be ready to take to the market towards the end of the month. - As romaine is not planted in beds in marshes until eight or fifteen days after it has been planted in coastal areas, cabbages are not planted there, for the same reason, until eight or fifteen days after coastal areas have been planted. We have to do this because the plants don't find the same shelter in the square as they do in the coast. Apart from that, cabbages can be planted and cared for in beds just as they are in coastal beds; they are more exposed to drying out from the winds, but they are watered more often: their apples are ready to be cut at the beginning of June. SUMMER CAULIFLOWER. This is the semi-hard cauliflower preferred for this season; it can be recognized by its broader leaves, and its usually thicker and shorter stem. CULTIVATION. - This cauliflower is sown at the end of April or in the first days of May, on a piece of old bedding, and as soon as it has two leaves, it is transplanted in a nursery; but if there is enough room to sow it lightly, it is not transplanted. It can be planted in the first days of June, even at the end of May, and is ready for sale in July and August. We would point out that it is difficult to obtain beautiful cauliflowers in summer in the Paris marshes, despite all our care and the most copious watering; the soil is not naturally fresh enough, nor strong or substantial enough, to provide sufficient nourishment for cauliflowers during the hot summer months in the capital. AUTUMN CAULIFLOWER. Semi-hard and hard cabbages are chosen for this season; the former shows its head before the latter. CULTIVATION. - From June 8 to 15, this cauliflower is sown in a prepared bed in the shade, moist if possible so that flea beetles do not eat it too much, and light enough so that it does not have to be transplanted: it is maintained with water to strengthen it and keep it tender until it is ready to be planted, which happens from July 1 to August 1. This season's cauliflowers are the most widely grown, firstly because the season is more favorable, and secondly because their apples are formed from mid-September to the end of November, and can be kept until April, so that they can be eaten for seven consecutive months. Given the importance of this crop, we'd like to explain it at some length, as we practice it in the Paris marshes. When our seedling is ready to be planted, we successively plough the soil in which it will be grown, and as we know that cabbages generally like good soil, we choose the best. When the soil is well tilled, we divide it into beds 2 meters 33 centimeters wide. According to our custom, these are harrowed with a fork, raked and then mulched. Once this was done, the master gardener drew nine or eleven lines or rows with his feet along the length of the boards. The plant should have been watered two hours beforehand, to make it easier to lift with a spade, and to pull it out of the ground with all its roots, and even with a little soil; it will be examined as we said before; the defective feet will be reformed and the good ones will be taken to the prepared beds. If you have decided to plant only two rows of cauliflowers per bed, choose the second row on each side of the bed; if you have decided to plant three rows, choose the middle row for the third row. Then take a planter and plant the young cauliflowers 66 cm apart in these two or three rows. As this young plant has not been transplanted, the stem is a little long, so you'll need to make the hole deeper, push the cabbage in right up to the leaves, so that it develops new roots on the buried stem, tie it off firmly and water it as soon as you've finished planting. When only two rows of cauliflower are planted per bed, this means that chicory or escarole will be planted in the other rows, and these vegetables can be planted immediately before or immediately after cauliflower. If, on the other hand, three rows of cauliflower have been planted per bed, it's often because the plan was to plant nothing more, but to sow lamb's lettuce, spinach or chervil among the cauliflower. In the latter case, you don't mulch the beds; you press them down, sow, harrow and spread a light layer of compost over the seedlings. As autumn cauliflower is planted in the hottest season, and cabbage is very water-loving, it cannot be over-watered until autumn, either at the head or at the mouth; to water in the latter way, make a dimple between two cauliflower plants, and pour in half a watering can. This is also the time when cabbage caterpillars multiply abundantly in certain years, and would do them considerable harm if care were not taken to destroy them continually. Finally, if care of every kind, and especially watering, has not been lacking, the cabbages should have reached their full strength by the end of September; the semi-hard ones will begin to mark, and their apples will set during October and November; they will be covered, as we said for forced-cultivation cauliflower. Hard cauliflower comes only after semi-hard cauliflower; it is in November and December that it yields its product, and its apple, firmer than the others, also keeps longer. In cold autumns, or, if you haven't watered enough, in dry, hot autumns, it can even happen that several plants of hard cauliflower don't yet show their apples when the frosts arrive. So, if you still have a certain number of hard cauliflowers that don't mark, you pull them out, remove the oldest leaves and replant them, close to and up to the leaves, in a coastal plot, where you protect them from frost with litter; or, better still, you remove a spade-head of soil in a trunk, plant the cauliflowers in the bottom, cover them with a frame, make an accot, cover, etc., and the apples form during the winter. BROCOLI CABBAGE. This species also contains several varieties: we will only mention white and purple broccoli, as the other varieties are not known to Paris market gardeners. Broccoli resembles cauliflower in the glaucous color of its leaves and in the way it forms its head, but its leaves are larger and more wavy. It differs above all from cauliflower in that it can withstand fairly heavy frosts and, having survived the winter, produces its head in the first spring. Before the introduction of forced cauliflower cultivation in Paris, the city's market gardeners used to grow broccoli; but since then, gardeners from the south of France and Finistère, favored by their climate, have been sending broccoli to Paris from the first three months of the year, and we can't compete with them because of the high cost of our land, so there are very few market gardeners in Paris who grow broccoli today; however, we'll give you an idea of how it's grown. BROCOLI BLANC. This cabbage is sown at the beginning of July, in the open ground, light enough not to need transplanting. If the seedling is well cared for, it can be planted in the first days of August. In a bed 2 meters 33 centimeters wide, you can plant four rows, with the plants 66 centimeters apart in the rows: you need to wet them well right away, and keep them well moistened until the end of September if the weather remains warm and dry. At the same time as planting broccoli, you can plant escarole in the same beds, or sow chervil, lamb's lettuce or spinach.
