Although broccoli tolerates frost fairly well, it could suffer if it were to freeze hard, and as it would be difficult to protect it if left in place, the usual practice is to pull it out before the ground freezes, and replant it close enough so that the whole stem is buried right down to the leaves. In this position, it will be easy to protect broccoli from frost by covering it with litter, and in spring it will produce its apples, which are less tightly packed, but just as good as those of cauliflower. PURPLE BROCCOLI. This variety can be recognized by the darker hue of its leaves, and above all by the violet color of its apples, which are softer and perhaps better than those of white broccoli. To the best of our knowledge, this variety is not grown like white broccoli for winter and spring; but we do know that if sown in April, it produces its apples in summer and autumn: as its color does not appeal on tables, and it is preferred to the white color of cauliflower, it is not widely grown. Summary of cauliflower characteristics. Tender cauliflower is divided into two varieties that we now call small and large Solomon. These two cabbages do not grow well on the ground, and fear very hot weather; they need a bed and some soil; their leaves are sharper than those of the following varieties. The small Solomon doesn't grow as large as the large one, and produces its head eight or fifteen days earlier. Semi-hard cauliflower grows well in the open ground, producing its head eight to fifteen days after the large salomon and fifteen days before the hard cauliflower. Its leaves are slightly more pointed than those of hard cauliflower. Hard cauliflower does very well in the open ground, and sprouts last, sometimes so late that frost catches it before it has formed. Hot weather forces us to water cauliflowers considerably, and their vegetation becomes so active that the grain of the head, instead of remaining smooth, sometimes becomes powdery, like a sheet of cloth. When cauliflower is in this state, it is said to be mossy or frothy: it has lost none of its quality, but it no longer looks or sells as well. As soon as you notice that a cauliflower is turning frothy, you should stop watering it, to slow down its excessive vigor. How to preserve cauliflower apples during the summer. As cauliflower is an excellent vegetable, it is only natural that people have tried to preserve it beyond the season when it grows abundantly. It has been suggested that cauliflower plants should be uprooted as soon as frost approaches and replanted in a cellar or storeroom; it has also been suggested that cauliflower heads or apples should be cut off, the larger leaves removed and laid aside, on boards or tables, in a place protected from frost and bright light. These two methods may be good for preserving cauliflower apples for a fortnight or a month, but soon the moisture stains them, and once they are stained, they rot more and more, and their preservation is compromised. Here's the method we use, with the greatest success, to preserve cauliflower heads or apples, perfectly healthy and very white, from November to the 15th.

April and beyond. First of all, you need to have a sort of cellar under your house or elsewhere, buried about 1 meter 66 centimeters, with a window at each end, so that you can establish a draught (a vaulted stone cellar would not be as suitable). One or two thousand nails are driven into the sides of the floor joists, at a distance of 27 to 30 centimetres from each other; each of these nails is destined to receive a cauliflower each winter. At the end of November, on a dry day, we choose from a square of hard cauliflowers, as these are the ones that keep best, we choose, shall we say, the most beautiful apples; we cut them a little low, so as to leave them with a core or end of stem 10 to 15 centimeters long; we detach entirely the leaves at the bottom of these cores, but we only shorten, to the length of 8 to 10 centimeters, those that border or surround the cauliflower apple. These bits of leaf protect the sides of the head from knocks and pressure, but do not protect the top. When carrying them and placing them on a table in the cellar where they are to be stored, care must be taken not to crumple them in any way. Once they're on the table in the cellar, the market gardener completes their grooming, i.e. he removes what he deems unnecessary from the leaves and core; then he ties a string 16 to 20 centimeters long to the core of each apple, and hangs the cauliflower apples upside down from the nails on the floor joists. Once the cauliflowers have been hung in this way, from the end of November onwards, they need to be given certain D8 care in order to keep them until April. As long as there is no frost, heavy rain or fog, both cellar windows should be left open, so that, as far as possible, there is a current of air to expel humidity, which is very detrimental to the preservation of cauliflowers; if, even later, when frost forces the windows to be kept closed, humidity appears, we light a few embers in the cellar to dry the air; But it's even more important to visit each cauliflower at least once a week, to remove any leaves that may be rotting without falling off, to see if any part of the head is staining, and to deliver for consumption those cauliflowers that seem likely to keep for the shortest time. While the cauliflowers are hanging in this way, they wither a little and may decrease in volume by about a quarter; but they are returned to their natural state when you are ready to take them to the market: to do this, cut off a few millimeters from the end of the core, plunge the tip of a knife into the flesh of the core in several places, and hold a tub of fresh water in which to immerse the core for twenty-four or thirty-six hours, without wetting the head; by this operation, the cauliflower regains its freshness, its original size, retains its whiteness, and loses none of its quality: it differs O from a newly-picked cauliflower only in that it has successively lost the portions of leaves surrounding it, either because they have fallen off of their own accord, or because they have been removed, in the visits, to oppose rotting. This is the best way we've found to keep cauliflowers until April; but now we've given up on keeping them that long: we are forced to sell out by the end of January, because from February onwards, couriers and stagecoach drivers bring broccoli to Paris from the south of France and Finistère, creating competition we can no longer sustain; and, if railroads multiply in France, this competition may well extend to our spring cauliflowers and cause great damage to the market garden of Paris. MACHE. A plant of the valerian family and the fedia genus. Lamb's lettuce is a small, native, autumnal plant which, until spring, shows only leaves spread out in a rosette on the ground, and, in this state, is good for eating as a salad. In spring, the stalk emerges, branches by dichotomy, rises to a height of 12 to 18 centimeters, blooms its very small bluish flowers, ripens its seeds and dies. There are two species, round and regency. O8 ROUND MACHE. GROWING. - This species is also known as doucette. It is very easy to grow: sow the seed at the beginning of September on unploughed land, or on very low ground if it has been ploughed, and spread a light layer of compost over the seedbed, keeping it moist by watering if the weather is dry, until the seed has emerged. Some market gardeners don't even put potting soil on their lamb's lettuce seedlings: they just scrape the soil with a rake to bury the seed and water as needed. The only precaution to take is to sow them light enough so that the plants can spread their leaves in a round or rosette shape over the soil, with a space of 6 to 7 centimetres, and so that the seed is very lightly buried; otherwise, it won't germinate. You can sow lamb's lettuce where cauliflower, Brussels sprouts and escarole have already been planted; remove the large mulch if there is any, sow between the plants and then harrow the soil. These mâches can be picked from the end of October to the end of March; after this time, they go to seed and are no longer for sale: to pick them, cut the root between two plots of land, remove any dead or defective leaves, and carry them to the market in calais. 16 137 This type of lamb's lettuce grows later than the previous one; it also differs by its crowned seed, its longer, broader leaves, and their less green, blonder color; it is also more highly esteemed. CULTIVATION. - Regency is sown in October, even lighter than its predecessor, because it becomes larger; apart from that, it is absolutely the same crop. It sets seed less quickly than round lamb's lettuce, and can be harvested for sale until mid-April. It is somewhat sensitive to the extreme cold of our crops: when heavy frosts occur, it is a good idea to cover it with a little mulch. LEEK OR PORREAU. A plant of the Liliaceae family and of the garlic genus; its base is a tray producing simple roots below and sword-shaped leaves above, about 40 to 50 centimeters long, interlocking at the bottom and forming a kind of stem. When this plant goes to seed, a straight, 1-metre-high spike emerges from between its leaves, ending in a ball of greenish flowers followed by small capsules containing the seeds. By cultivating the same seed in three different ways, we can make three different types of leek, each with its own name in market gardening: leeks are eaten with the leafy stem. SHORT LEEK. CULTURE. -From September 15th to 20th, as many planks are ploughed and separated by paths as oh wants to sow into a porreau: the seed is sown on this ploughing; but, as this porreau is not to be transplanted, it must be sown very lightly or in such a way that each seed is about 5 centimetres apart. To achieve this, we use 92 grams (3 ounces) of seed to sow 56 shallow meters. When the seed has been sown, we harrow it with a fork to bury it and break up the clods, then we plumb the whole area; we can sow a little lamb's lettuce after the soil has been plumbed; the raking stroke we give afterwards, to even out the surface of the soil, will bury the lamb's lettuce seed sufficiently; after that, all we have to do is spread a thickness of 12 or 15 millimetres of fine compost over the whole area: If the season is dry, it's important to moisten the soil to help germination. If, after emergence, the plant appears to be too thick in some places, it will be necessary to pull out what is in the way, D8 so that each porreau has enough room to develop. During the winter, the lamb's lettuce will be picked and sold; in March and April, we'll prevent the growth of weeds in the porreaux; we'll water them often if the weather is dry and warm enough, and in the first fortnight of May the porreaux will be ready to be sold. This piglet is small and does not have much white, but it is nevertheless sought-after as an early crop, and because in May the previous year's piglets are all mounted. LONG PIG. CULTIVATED - From December 20 to 30, a layer 48 to 54 centimeters high and as long as the number of seeds to be sown, is made with half teuf manure and half old manure, mixed well together. One or more trunks are placed on top of this layer, and terracotta is added in such quantities that, when it is spread evenly throughout a trunk, it is about 8 centimetres thick; On top of this potting soil, we sow very coarse porreau seed, i.e. 61 grams (a ounce) for each frame panel, cover the seed with a few millimeters of potting soil, press the whole thing down with the plasterboard, place the frames on the chests and cover with straw mats until the seed has emerged, which happens after seven or eight days. Once the seed has emerged, all you need to do is let it enjoy the light during the day, and the air when the weather permits, and cover it at night to protect it from frost. By mid-March, the plant should be ready for planting: the soil is ploughed, made into planks, plumbed, raked and covered with 15 millimetres of potting soil; the porreau plant is then pulled out, the roots cut to a length of 2 centimetres, and the tips of the leaves cut off so that the plant is no more than 18 centimetres long. Then take a planter and plant it into the prepared boards, 8 or 9 centimetres apart, taking care to make the holes perpendicular and to push the porreau in so that only 5 or 4 centimetres remain above ground, because the deeper the porreau is buried, the more white it will be and the more valuable it will be; as soon as it is planted, it must be watered, and when drought occurs, it must be supported with water. When well cared for, it is ready for consumption at the end of May and the beginning of June: market gardeners call it spring porreau and new porreau. AUTUMN PORREAU. CULTIVATION - In March, plough a bed, harrow it, sow the porreau seed in a fairly thick layer, bury it by harrowing a second time with a fork, press it down and cover it with a 15-mm-thick bed of compost. If there is frost-free sunshine, we water to help germination; then, when the weather has become quite mild, we water to activate vegetation. At the beginning of June, the plant is strong enough to be planted permanently; then we plough, make planks and plant this young plant, treating it absolutely as we said when talking earlier about the long porreau, with the difference that, as the autumn porreau has to withstand all the heat of summer, it must be watered much more abundantly and receive some hoeing in its youth. This piglet is delivered for consumption in autumn and throughout the winter: it is consumed in huge quantities. CERFEUIL. Plant of the Umbelliferae family and of the genus whose name it bears: it is an annual with a taproot and compound leaves; when it is ready to bear fruit, its stem rises to about 66 centimeters, branches out, and each branch ends in a small umbel of whitish flowers which are followed by dried, subulate fruits: only the leaves of chervil are used. WINTER CHERVIL. CULTIVATION. - It is not necessary to plow the soil to sow this chervil: we sow it in September, where there are cauliflowers planted, in beds of bound escarole and chicory; we sow it very lightly in order to obtain beautiful, large clumps; we bury the seed by a simple harrowing with a rake or fork. Chervil is sold from December to February; to deliver it for consumption, we cut it by the foot between two irons, remove the bad leaves if any, and carry it to the market in bundles or in calais. Chervil can withstand harsh winters; however, it can be sheltered with a light covering of litter. SPRING CHERVIL. CULTIVATION. - In February and March, sow this chervil in the ground as follows: plow the land to be planted, lay out the soil in plots a meter 33 centimeters wide; use your feet to mark out eleven or twelve rays along the length of the plots, and sow the chervil seed fairly thickly in these wide rays; when the seed is sown, cover it by raking out the soil in between the furrows; then use the same rake to level out the entire surface of the plots, over which you spread 6 millimeters of potting soil. When the chervil is up, the spring frosts don't damage it in the slightest. In an ordinary year, this chervil will have reached a height of around 15 to 20 centimetres around forty days after sowing; it is then cut to 3 centimetres of soil, tied in small bundles and sent to the market. Chervil grows back quickly in this season, and if supported in the wet, will produce a second crop thirty days after the first: then, if you don't need to let it go to seed, turn it over to replace it with other vegetables, as it is no longer able to produce beautiful leaves. SUMMER CHERVIL. CULTIVATION. - When May arrives, chervil goes to seed so quickly that it is difficult to obtain a passable harvest; however, it is necessary to have some in summer and autumn. To succeed, we sow chervil in the shade, in the coolest spot of our marshes, in the same way as summer chervil, but a little at a time, and every eight or ten days, until September, when we start sowing again for the winter. LETTUCE. Plant of the Compositae family and of the genus whose name it bears. There are several species and varieties of lettuce, all with taproots and lactescent juices. Most have numerous, broad, interlocking leaves that form a head or apple, which is their edible part. When the time comes for them to bear fruit, the apple opens at the top, and a stalk emerges, 60 to 80 centimetres high, covered with yellowish florets, several of which are united in a common involucre, and to which the seeds follow. Of all these species or varieties of lettuce, only five are known in Paris market gardening. = HATIVE LETTUCE KNOWN AS PETITE NOIRE. CULTIVATION. - In our marshes, we call this lettuce petite noire, not because it is blacker than any other, but because its seed is black. It is also known as crepe lettuce, because its leaves are very embossed. It apples under airless cloches; we grow it cold in the following way: In the first days of September, we plough a small corner of land to suit the number of bells we want to place on it; after raking the ploughed land, we spread 3-centimeter-thick potting soil over it, and press it down, not with our feet, but with a shovel or sideboard; then we take a bell, place it on the potting soil, and, by pressing a little on the top of the bell, the round of its base imprints itself on the potting soil; then lift the bell, place it next to it again, and make a second impression, and so on for as long as you need; then sow the seed of small black lettuce fairly thickly in all the circles marked on the potting soil; cover the seed with half a centimetre of potting soil and place a bell on each circle; if the sun shines strongly on the bells, shade them with a straw mat or, better still, with light litter: In just a few days, the seed has sprouted; we continue to take care that the plant is not scorched by the sun, without however giving it air. Soon the plant has two leaves in addition to its cotyledons, and it's time to transplant it. To do this, prepare an ados (see this word, chapter VIII) or several ados 1 mềtre apart and all inclined to the south, and, when they are covered with 3-centimetre-thick, well-plumbed compost, place three rows of 10 98 bells on each ados, starting with the back or highest row and lining it up with a chalk line; The other two rows are then placed in a chessboard pattern, so that all the bells touch each other and each one makes its mark on the soil. When the adolescent(s) is (are) cloistered in this way, we carefully pull out the seedling we've just mentioned, and transplant it under the adolescent cloches of the following manjère; we remove a few bells from one end of the adolescent, leaving the imprint of the circle they've made; we calculate the distances so that twenty-four or thirty seedlings fit into each circle, and the ones closest to the circle are still 5 centimetres apart: then you take a seedling with your left hand; with the first finger of your right hand, you make a hole in the mould and soil proportionate to the length of the seedling's root; you insert this root into the hole, and immediately mark it off by pressing the soil and mould against it with the finger of your right hand; When the whole ring has been planted in this way, the bell is placed back on top, another is planted, and so on until the whole ados is finished. This plant doesn't need to be watered, but it does need to be protected from the sun, when it is shining brightly, by mats which are unrolled over the bells in the middle of the day. After about twenty days, the plant should be 1:1 the length of a 5-franc coin or more, and it's time to plant it permanently. It is always planted in the same way, but under three different conditions. First condition. - The seedling is lifted with the precaution of leaving a small root ball, and is planted by hand, four by four, under each bell of the new ados, taking care to place them so that there is enough distance between them to allow them to develop fully, and at the same time far enough from the glass so that their leaves do not touch it when they grow and are not exposed to being burnt. Second condition. - In this season (October), we usually have old beds which are no longer occupied and which have lost all their heat; then we raise their compost in the shape of ados, we plombe it with the planking, we place three rows of bells in a chessboard and we plant under each of them four lettuce plants with the care and precautions indicated above. Third condition. - Instead of planting the lettuce plant under a bell, it can be planted under a frame: in the first condition, you could have made gutters and sunk the boxes until the soil inside was only 10 centimetres (4 inches) from the glass, so that the lettuce planted in them would not wither; in the second condition, we could also have replaced the bells on the beds with boxes and their frames, always bearing in mind that black lettuce only grows to a height of 8 centimetres (3 inches), and that it is very advantageous for the glass of the frame to be only a to 5 centimetres away from the lettuce. This being understood, forty to fifty lettuces are planted under each panel of the frame; the panels are replaced immediately, so that the air or the cold does not seize the young lettuces, and, as at this time of year there may be a few light frosts, it will be necessary to guarantee this by covering the frames and cloches with straw mats. The small black lettuce treated in this way reaches its full size at the end of November and the beginning of December: it does not apple as well as in spring, but it is very tender and highly valued in this late season. We must warn you that the term "lettuce petite noire", or simply "lettuce petite noire", is an ellipsis; we should say "lettuce with black seed", although there are other lettuces that also have black seed. OCTOBER. We have just described the cold culture, during winter, of the early lettuce known as petite noire or crepe, and now we are going to describe the forced culture of this same species, which is the only lettuce that has the property of eating under cloche and under frame without needing air; we therefore only cultivate it under frame and under cloche until March. SMALL BLACK OR CREPE LETTUCE. FORCED CULTIVATION. - The seed of this lettuce must be sown from October 5 to 15, not in the open ground as previously, but on prepared soil as described in chapter VIII, and under a bell; as the plant must be transplanted very young, the sowing can be done quite thickly. In this season, the seed will germinate in four or five days, and eight or ten days later, the seedling will be ready for transplanting: it should then be transplanted onto an ados similar to the one in which it was sown, and only twenty-four seedlings should be placed under each bell, the average term. By giving this seedling the usual care, it should be as large as a 5-franc coin or more, around December, and ready to be planted out; when the temperature has reached the right point, the soil is well prepared, the boxes placed on top are full enough so that the soil is only 10 centimetres from the glass, the plant is lifted with a small clod and planted on these layers in the boxes, so as to place 50 to 65 of them at equal distances under each frame panel, and so that the bottom row is 16 centimetres from the wood, so that the shadow from the front of the box doesn't stretch it; as soon as you've planted the width of a panel, you must immediately place this frame panel back over the young lettuce, to protect it from the air. Once the lettuce has been planted in this way, it should be visited at least every eight days, to see if any insects or molluscs are eating it, and to remove any leaves that might rot. At the first frosts, surround the beds with a bundle to keep them warm and prevent the outside cold from penetrating; cover the frames with straw mats; if the cold increases, fill the paths with dry manure up to the height of the trunks, and double the straw mats; If snow falls, we shake the straw mats out of the layers, pushing the snow that has fallen into the path by the ends before it melts, so that it doesn't chill the layers, which would be very detrimental to the health of the lettuces. Market gardeners who take every possible precaution to manage their plants properly three weeks after the lettuce is planted and 151 as its heart begins to form, remove the two or three leaves with which it was planted, which have by then become very large and can no longer contribute to forming the head; this operation is called peeling: in March plantings, it is less necessary, and can be neglected. For some years now, several market gardeners have been in the habit of sowing early carrot seed on their beds at the same time as planting lettuce, and these carrots succeed the lettuces; but others hardly approve of this cultivation, and prefer to sow their early carrots on a separate bed with radishes. Black or crepe lettuce, sown from October 5 to 15 and planted at the beginning of December, is picked and ready for sale towards the end of January and the beginning of February. We also plant our cauliflowers, pettit and gros salomon, as mentioned above, on these beds. However, during December and January, the weather may not allow us to make beds, nor to plant, while we still have many lettuce plants transplanted on the ados, and we must protect these plants from the rigors of the season, in order to plant them later. We start by making an accot on the back of the ados, and cover the bells at night with straw mats. If the frost increases, very-short, very-dry and very-tight manure is placed between the bells, first to a thickness of 10 to 12 millimeters, and finally to the height of the. When the sun is shining through a fine frost, we remove the mats, loosen the bonnet of the bells, so that the sun's rays can penetrate, warm and revive the lettuce plant underneath, and by three o'clock in the afternoon, we cover it for the following night. But then the weather turns milder, and when there are no more severe frosts to worry about, we make what we call lettuce beds, i.e. beds that are only 32 centimeters (1 foot) thick of manure filled with 10 centimeters (4 inches) of compost; Three rows of bells are placed on top of this compost, and four black lettuces are planted under each bell, with one romaine in the center. Depending on the atmospheric temperature, the lettuces are covered and uncovered until they are ready to eat. Black or crepe lettuces planted on a bed and under a frame around December 10th are picked and ready to sell in January; those planted later on a bed and under a bell are picked and ready to sell in February and March. GOTTE LETTUCE. This species can't be treated like the previous one, because it can't peel without air; moreover, it's less early, gets fatter and apples better; it's highly valued. CULTIVATION. - Gotte lettuce is sown from October 20 to 25, in the same way as small black lettuce in forced cultivation, and receives absolutely the same care and treatment, although it is less sensitive to frost. As it is less early than the petite noire, we leave it on the teens until the petite noire is sold, and put it in its place on the layers. To do this, we don't touch the manure in these layers, we just plow the soil in the trunks with a three-pronged fork, and when it is well levelled, we plant gotte lettuce in it, at the end of January and beginning of February, at the rate of thirty lettuces per frame panel: We protect them from cold and frost by the same means as for black lettuce; but we don't neglect to give them air whenever the weather permits, by putting the wedge under the edge of the frames from behind. However, if gotte lettuce has not yet set when the weather is fine, remove the frames entirely, and let it finish setting in the air. Instead of planting gotte lettuce under a frame, it can be planted on the same beds, but under cloches arranged in three rows and in a chessboard pattern; three feet of lettuce are then placed under each cloche, on condition that they are protected from the cold by the means indicated for teens, and given air by means of small wooden racks as often as the weather allows. Finally, gotte lettuce can also be planted in the open ground and under cloche. To do this, plough and divide the soil well; rake the soil; spread a 3-centimetre layer of potting soil, levelling it with a rake; then press it down well; place cloches in rows and in a chessboard pattern on top of the potting soil, and plant three lettuces under each cloche: when they are well established, give them air if necessary. To sum up, gotte lettuce planted under a frame at the end of January is ready for harvesting at the end of March; lettuce planted on a bed and under a bell in February is ready for harvesting at the beginning of April; lettuce planted on the ground under a bell or under a frame at the end of February is ready for harvesting around April 15th. GEORGES LETTUCE. This lettuce grows stronger and withstands light frosts better than gotte; it is distinguished by its whitish-green color. CULTIVATION. - This lettuce is sown in the first two weeks of November, on teas, and is cared for in exactly the same way as the previous varieties: it is transplanted at twenty-four per cloche. In the course of February, there are usually empty beds after the crêpe or petite noire lettuces have been brought in; the soil in these beds is then ploughed with a fork; cloches are placed in rows and in a chessboard pattern, as we have already said, and three lettuces are planted under each cloche. When the lettuce is harvested, it is given as much air as possible, because of all the lettuces, only the little black one can peel under a bell without air. Georges lettuce, treated as described above, is ready for harvesting at the end of March. However, as this lettuce can withstand a few light frosts, we harden the plant that has remained on the backs, first by lifting the bells from behind, then by removing them completely during the day. In March, we plant this plant on the south coast, where, with proper care, it will head and be ready for consumption in May: we don't sow it in spring. RED LETTUCE. This lettuce differs from the previous one in its red color, its larger size, and the fact that it is grown until June. CULTIVATION. - Red lettuce is sown around October 15th on teas, then transplanted on teas and under cloche, and cared for in the same way as the preceding lettuces. As soon as the weather gets milder towards the end of February, we get it used to the air by lifting the bells on one side, and in the first days of March, we remove the bells if the weather 6 allows. Then prepare a coastal and plant the lettuce, taking the precaution, for all lettuces, of burying only the root and not the leaves, so that the head is larger at the bottom than at the top, which contributes to their beautiful shape and makes them more valuable. At the end of March, they are planted in the open, i.e. without shelter. After tilling the soil well, dividing it into plots 2 meters 35 centimeters (7 feet) wide, plumbing them and covering them with 2 centimeters of potting soil, draw ten or eleven lines with the feet on each plot, and plant the lettuce, leaving a distance of about 45 centimeters between each foot in the rows. In this season, watering is still moderate, and you need to consider the state of the atmosphere to judge whether or not to water the lettuce you've just planted: in any case, it should be ready and ready to send to the market towards the end of May. GREY LETTUCE. This differs from the previous varieties in that it is greener and more speckled, and can be grown all summer long, even into October. CULTIVATION. - This lettuce is sown from March to July and beyond, so that it is available until the frosts. It is sown either on the ground or on a piece of conch, and lightly, as it does not need to be transplanted. Sow only a few at a time, every ten or twelve days, so as not to run out during the summer. When the plant is ready for planting, prepare beds by ploughing in the spring and mulching during the summer. As this lettuce is larger than the previous one, plant only nine or ten rows in a bed 2 metres 33 centimetres wide, and place the plants 40 or 48 centimetres apart in the rows. If any lettuce is prone to speckling, it's this one, so we don't water it in the heat of the sun. LETTUCE FOR CUTTING. Since market gardening has been supplying head lettuces throughout the winter, it no longer produces lettuces for cutting, but we'll say a word about them. Many lettuces of a pleasant blond hue, such as gotte lettuce, can be used for cutting lettuce; but there is one particular species that is very blond, does not head, has a white, very long seed and is called nothing else than cutting lettuce, because it is good only for this purpose, since it does not head. The seed of this species is sown fairly thickly, in February and March, on a warm bed; if it is covered with bells, the lettuce comes on more quickly and is more tender; when it is 4 to 5 centimetres high, it is cut a few millimetres from the ground and used in salads. You can also sow lettuce on a bed or under a bell, to follow on from that sown on a bed; but, we repeat, as soon as you see head lettuce, you don't want any more lettuce to cut. PASSION LETTUCE. Paris market gardeners don't cultivate this lettuce any more than they cultivate cutting lettuce; but, since both are grown in kitchen gardens where there is little or no forced cultivation, we must also mention Passion lettuce. When the plant is strong enough, it is planted in a sheltered bed, where it usually overwinters without shelter, because it is the hardiest of all lettuces and it takes a hard winter to damage it. In spring, it is given a hoeing and apples around Holy Week, hence its name Pasaion lettuce. There are many other lettuces, more or less esteemed, which we won't mention here, because they are not grown by market gardeners in the Paris area. ROMAINE, CHICON. Botanists place romaine in the lettuce genus; Indeed, whereas lettuce forms a rounded head, only jo to ¡a centimeters high, with round leaves pressed together, romaine forms a column 20 to 25 centimeters high, with long, narrow leaves of a stronger consistency, and whose top, curved into a cap, covers and preserves the heart of the plant. Lettuce and romaine rank first among salads, and both contribute to supplying our markets. There are several varieties of romaine, more or less esteemed, but Paris market gardeners grow only three, each with a characteristic name: ROGIAINE VERTE DITE DE PRINEUR. This one is the most suitable for forced cultivation, because it buds or tops earlier than the others, and is also a little smaller: it is only grown until the beginning of May. FORCED CULTIVATION. - The seed of this romaine is sown in the first days of October. Its cultivation is almost entirely similar to that of little black lettuce: we could refer the reader to it, but we prefer to repeat ourselves, for fear of leaving some obscurity. In the first week of October, in a well-sheltered spot facing south, plough an area of land proportionate to the quantity of seed you intend to sow; when this ploughing is well harrowed with a fork and the rake has been passed over it, spread a 2-centimeter-thick bed of potting soil, levelling it well and pressing it down with the back of a spade or board; Then place a bell on top, pressing down hard enough to mark the round at its base; lift it up, place it next to it to mark another round, and so on; when enough rounds have been marked, sow the seed in each round, cover it with 1 centimetre of fine compost, and place a bell on top of each round. This is the most general way of sowing green romaine, but it can also be sown on a piece of old bed, with the mould turned over and plumbed, or on a bed prepared as we said for black lettuce. In October, the seed should emerge in three or four days, and although the sun is not to be feared at this time of year, care should be taken to ensure that it does not tire the young plant under the cloches. Twelve or fifteen days later, the plant should be ready for transplanting: at this point, we form a suitable number of ados (see this word, chapter VIII), cloche them and proceed with transplanting. We remind you here that you must always lift the plant you wish to transplant, by passing a hoe under the roots and weighing them down, so that when you pull the plant by the leaves, the roots do not break and take some soil with them. As soon as a small seedling has been lifted, it is immediately transplanted under the cloches of the ados prepared for this purpose; twenty-four or thirty romaine seedlings are transplanted under each cloche, with the care and precautions we explained earlier, and, as soon as a cloche is transplanted, the cloche is immediately put back on. In just a few days, the plant is up and running again, and as the weather is still fairly mild and the romaine loves air, we give it to it by lifting the bells from behind, by two fingerbreadths, in the middle of the day, by means of a rack suitable for this purpose, and lowering them in the evening. However, it sometimes happens that the weather remains fairly mild throughout November and part of December, as, for example, in the year 1843, and that, despite the air given to the romaine plant, it grows too quickly; But this time, instead of twenty-four or thirty plants, we only put eighteen or twenty under each bell, and we continue to give it air day and night. But then the hard frost arrives: first we remove the air, cover with straw mats, and make a bundle behind the bells; we put very short, very dry manure, well pressed between the bells, 10 to 15 centimetres high; if the cold increases, we put this short manure, called dust, up to the top of the bells, and double or triple the straw mats. By taking all these precautions in good time, it is rare for the plant to be affected by frost. When the worst of the danger has passed, we carefully uncover the plant little by little, first giving it light through the top of the bells; then, all around, we give it air, weather permitting. In January and February, we select the best of these green romaine plants for planting, one between four of black lettuce, under cloches, on the lettuce beds, and they become the first romaine plants that we release for consumption, in the first week of March: this is our forced romaine. But there are still a lot of plants left on the teens, which we'll be using later, and we have to keep looking after them. If the cold is still to be feared, first protect them from it, then take every opportunity to give them air, by raising the bells on one side during the day by about 4 centimetres to start with, then by 6, 8, 10, 15 centimetres, depending on the atmospheric temperature; Finally, when the weather no longer seems to be a cause for concern, the bells are removed altogether, as the plant needs to become firmer and stronger in the air, before it can be planted permanently, either on a bed or in the ground. As early as the end of January and the first fortnight of February, if the ground is not frozen, all market gardeners plant green romaine on the coast, to the south. This coast is ploughed and levelled as usual; short carrot, parsnip or light porridge seed is sown in it, then harrowed with a fork to bury the seed, the rake is passed over, a centimetre of compost is spread over the whole, and when it is well spread, the rays are traced on it with the feet. There are coasts of varying widths; those protected by a wall are usually the widest; on one that is 2 meters 66 centimeters (8 feet) wide, fourteen or fifteen spokes can be traced; after which, green romaine seedlings are lifted at the teens, and planted in these spokes about 33 centimeters (1 foot) apart. It goes without saying that you've chosen the best plant, lifted it with all its roots and a little soil, carefully placed all its roots in the hole, and edged them properly with the planter. However, in well-exposed coastal areas, we rarely plant all the rays of green romaine; we usually leave two or three rays for semi-hard cauliflowers, which are planted at the same time. Green romaine, planted in this way at the beginning of February, is topped in the first fortnight of May, and the cauliflowers planted at the same time bear their apples in the first fortnight of June. Although green romaine does well on its own, it can be made firmer by tying it three-quarters of the way up with a piece of wet straw. In some years, April and the beginning of May are hot; in others, March and April can be drying, so coastal plantings such as romaine and cauliflower need frequent watering. In March, green romaine is also planted in full squares, but is no longer grown in the summer; it is replaced by the following. GREY ROMAIN. This is larger and more sensitive to frost than green romaine: it is sown and planted throughout the summer. CULTIVATION. - Grey romaine is sown at the end of October, a fortnight or three weeks after green romaine, in the same way, on soil or on ados, and is transplanted in the same way on ados and under cloche, and is protected from frost during the winter by the same means; but it is only planted in the first fortnight of March, in full squares, i.e. without shelter, in the following way: Plough and draw up one or more planks, and sow in them, if you wish, radish or parsley seed, or sorrel; bury these seeds by harrowing with a fork, and press the earth down with your feet; after raking in the soil and spreading a bed of compost 1 to a centimetre thick on top, trace the rays with your feet. If you've sown seeds in a bed 2 metres 35 centimetres wide, trace out only eight rays; if you haven't sown any seeds, trace out nine or ten rays, and plant the grey romaine plant, putting 48 centimetres between each foot, in the rows, if there are no seeds sown; but, if there are, each foot should be spaced 54 to 60 centimetres (20 to 22 inches) apart. When the sun begins to set or the heat begins to make itself felt, this romaine should be given a little wetting from time to time, in the morning rather than in the evening, because it is sensitive to frost, and evening wetting in March and April, even up to May 15, can become dangercuses by attracting frost to the hu mid spots: grey romaine, planted in March, coifes in May; it is tied with a sprig of straw and is good to sell at the end of the month. But this isn't the only harvest or the only season that can be made with grey romaine: in March we sow it on a piece of bed, and plant it without having transplanted it; in April we sow it light enough in the ground to be able to plant it without prior transplanting; finally we sow it every fifteen or eighteen days, until the end of July or the beginning of August, in order to have some all summer and until the escarole and chicory yield; because then romaine is no longer so sought-after that we cultivate it until the frosts. It goes without saying that, throughout the summer, we plant this romaine at the distance and in the manner indicated earlier, with the difference that, when we don't mix in seed, we cover the bed with good mulch instead of compost, because mulch retains moisture in the soil better than compost, which, because of its black color, absorbs heat and dries out the soil; we cover our beds with potting soil in winter and spring, because it absorbs heat, which the soil needs at that time, and attracts less moisture than mulch; but when the heat arrives, we prefer mulch to cover our beds before planting, and only use potting soil to cover our seedlings and prevent the soil from hardening. Another observation: practice has taught us that, during the summer, if we water our romaines in the hot sun with cold water from our wells, when they are about to be capped or have already been capped, this causes spots of rot on the inside; we then say that the romaine is speckled: in this state, it is no longer fit for sale. The same observation has been made of escarole and chicory, when their hearts fill up, when they are good to bind or already bound; so that it is the rule in our marshes, when these plants have reached this state of growth, to stop watering them in the heat of the sun. BLOND ROMAINE. This is the most pleasing to the eye because of its blond green color; it is also larger and more tender, but it has the disadvantage of sometimes staining or rotting on the inside when capped. CULTIVATION. - Blonde romaine can be sown at the end of October and the beginning of November, just like gray romaine. It can be transplanted on ados and preserved throughout the winter with the same care; it can also be transplanted under cold frames, and up to three hundred can be placed under each panel, provided that all known means are taken to prevent frost from penetrating the trays. However, we would point out that romaine seedlings transplanted under a cold frame are never as beautiful as those transplanted under a bell. In March, we plant blond romaine in a square, and treat it in exactly the same way as we have just described gray romaine; we sow both of them every ten or twelve days, from March to the end of July, so as not to run out of seedlings, and they are planted in the same way throughout the summer. In very hot weather, blond romaine is prone to speckling in the center, if watered in the middle of the day. NOVEMBER. In Paris market gardening, as it is generally practiced nowadays, there is little sowing to be done during this month and the next. In this month, it may be necessary to resow a little seed from grey romaine and blonde, gotte and Georges lettuces, which are transplanted onto ados as previously mentioned. The main occupation of market gardeners in this month is to continue transplanting the lettuce and romaine plants from the last sowings, and to make new ados to replace the romaine plant transplanted in October: small and large Solomon's cauliflower, half-hard and hard cauliflower are also replaced, i.e. put under other frames; lettuce alone is never replaced. Especially when November and December are mild, we are obliged to change or transplant these types of plants a second time, so that they don't grow too fast before the season when it will be possible and convenient to plant them either on beds or in the ground; This second transplanting delays them by about a fortnight, and, as in this second operation the collar of the romaine is sunk into the ground by about ra millimetres and that of the cabbages by about 3 centimetres, these plants form new roots which strengthen them and make them more robust to resist the cold; moreover, they continue to receive the same care as before. This month, Paris market gardeners also plant white onions permanently (see his article, page 94); they also plant York, pain-de-sucre and cœur-de-bœuf cabbages permanently (see the article on Cabbage, page 104). Only the following vegetables are sown in this month. PEAS. A plant of the legume family, of the viciate section and of the genus whose name it bears. There are several species of pea, but only one is cultivated for human consumption, and this species has produced several varieties: all are annual, stunted, 30 cm to 1.60 m high, with winged leaves whose common petiole ends in a tendril; the flowers are axillary, usually white, quite large, and are followed by pods 6 to 12 cm long, which contain round seeds known as peas: it is these peas that are eaten, and sometimes the pods of certain varieties. MICHAUX PEAS FROM HOLLAND. We choose this variety, which has so far been considered the earliest and the most suitable for cultivation under frame; but if another variety were to compete with it for earliness, such as the Prince Albert pea, and yet another, we would abandon it and give preference to the newcomer, even if it were only twenty-four hours earlier. Besides, early peas are not expensive to grow, and require no artificial heat, just the warmth of the sun through the frames. Here's a simple way to grow peas around March 15th. Around ao November, plough as many beds as you want for first-season peas, and divide the soil well; surround these beds with melon boxes, and sow in each box five rows of peas, lengthwise along the boxes, placing the peas about 3 centimetres apart; fill the paths with cold old manure, not likely to heat up; then place the panels on the boxes. When the cold arrives, the manure in the paths is kept at the same level as the boxes, to prevent frost from penetrating under the frames, and straw mats are placed on the frames for the same purpose; but these are removed whenever the sun shines, so that the peas wither as little as possible. When, towards the beginning of February, the peas are close to touching the glass, we lay them down towards the back of the trunk, placing laths on top at a height of around 16 to 18 centimetres; in a few days, their ends straighten out, we remove the laths, and the bottom of the stems stay down: this operation makes them branch out and become stockier. Soon the peas will rise to the glass; but we raise the boxes by putting straw plugs under the corners, and we fill the paths with old dry manure, to prevent air and cold from penetrating to the peas from under the boxes. We'll give air by lifting the frames a little from behind, whenever the sun shines a little brightly. As is customary, peas are topped above the third or fourth flower, and small oars are added if necessary, so that light can better circulate between stems and leaves. By sowing peas in this way, every two weeks from November to February, you'll have peas until those sown in the open bear fruit. We must point out, however, that the cultivation of early peas is not very lucrative, in that it requires a large quantity of frames that could be used for other, more profitable crops: we make more money with a panel of lettuces or carrots than with a panel of peas, and this explains why so few market gardeners grow early peas. DECEMBER. While there is little or no sowing to be done in this month, on the other hand there is a lot of forced cultivation to be done, and if the season is not too rigorous, the early market gardener has a lot to do. First of all, whatever the weather, he has to look after and preserve all the plants he has under cloches on the teens; if a ray of sun appears, he has to uncover it, so that the plants can enjoy it through the cloches, and cover before nightfall: this is the month when you have to make a lot of layers for lettuce and layers for turnips and radishes, planting some and sowing the others. It's the little black lettuce, or hâtive, or crèpe, that is planted in layers and under chassis in this rigorous season, because it's the only one, as we said earlier, that can grow quite well under cloche and chassis: Remember, too, that between fifty and sixty-five lettuces are planted per frame, and that they need to be protected from the cold by all the means indicated, without, however, generating too much heat under the frame panels; for, while lettuce generally rises very well in the high damp heat of manure, it does not apple well in this same heat; it therefore needs only moderate heat: So, if you manage this black lettuce well, planted on a bed and under a frame in December, it will be ready for consumption towards the end of January and the beginning of February. RAVE, RADISH. Rave and radish are two varieties of the same species and of the horseradish genus, of the cruciferous family. They are annual plants, with spreading, pinnatifid leaves at the base, and whose stems grow to a height of about a metre, bearing purplish, cruciate flowers, which are followed by swollen, tortuous, acute siliques containing the seeds. In cultivated varieties, the root is simple, taprooted, fleshy, fusiform or rounded, white, pink, violet or black. The 4. roots alone are edible and eaten raw. There are two auricles at the top of the rabe and radish, the origin of which, very unusual, was not adequately explained until 1838 (1). HATIVE VIOLET RABE. Different varieties of rabe have been grown at different times, but for some years now we have been sticking to the early violet rabe for our forced cultivations, because we find it more advantageous, in that it is more sought-after: we say the same for the pink demi-long radish. FORCED CULTIVATION, first season. - At the end of December, sow a layer or several layers, as described in Chapter VIII; place the boxes on top; put in potting soil 14 to 16 centimetres (5 to 6 inches) thick, and cover with the frames, so that the layer warms up more quickly; even add straw mats if frost is prevalent. When the chateur has fallen to the right point, level out the potting soil, sow the turnip seed light enough so that the turnips are 2 centimetres apart, cover with 12 millimetres of potting soil, and replace (1) By P. J. F. Turpin, draughtsman, botanist physiologist, who died a member of the Académie des sciences in 1840. the frames immediately. In less than five days, the seed has sprouted; then you need to give it air every day, if possible, because rabe doesn't do well without air. On the other hand, you must not neglect any of the means indicated to prevent frost from penetrating under the frames. If the weather is not too adverse, rabe will be good after forty days of sowing. HALF-LONG PINK RADISH. Radishes are grown in much the same way as turnips; they also need air, and if there were a month in January when the frames could not be opened more or less, the season would be compromised. Second season. - To grow a second season of early rabe or radish, at the end of February or the beginning of March, lay down one or more layers as before, but don't put in a box or frame. Load it with potting soil, and when it has warmed up a little, edge it in and add as much as you need to make it 14 to 16 centimeters thick everywhere; sow it as a rabe or radish, as described above, and cover the seed with 12 millimeters of potting soil. As soon as this operation is complete, spread straw mats over the entire layer, to facilitate and hasten germination by concentrating heat and humidity. As soon as germination has taken place, the doormats are lifted, and, as there is no protection from frost at this time of year, on the same day as the doormats are lifted, two rows of gaulettes raised on small stakes 8 or 10 centimetres above the soil are set up along the length of the bed, to support the doormats at this distance from the plant when it is necessary to cover it. Sown in this way in March, turnips and radishes will be ready in thirty or forty days. RABE AND RADISH IN THE GROUND. CULTURE. - From the first days of March, sow turnips or radishes in well-sheltered coastal areas. They are rarely sown on their own in coastal areas; the most common practice is to sow them at the same time as lettuce, romaine and cauliflower, because coastal crops are too precious in March to be planted for just one season; but, at the end of the month, we start sowing them in full plots, where the sun is shining. After ploughing and levelling, harrowing and plumbing as many beds as you need, you sow them and harrow a second time to bury the seed, after which you spread 15 millimetres of compost over the whole. In March and April, you don't usually need to water; but if there's a frost-free haze, you'll need to water because of the drought, and to keep off the aphids (flea beetles) that devour crucifer seedlings, especially those of turnip and cabbage in dry springs. Turnips and radishes are sown from March to September, and as they are only good when young and tender, and then rise very quickly in summer, sow them every eight days, keep the paths higher than the beds and water them abundantly every day, first in the evening, then around eleven o'clock in the morning during very hot weather. In summer, turnips and radishes are good to eat twenty-five days after sowing. BLACK RADISH. This one becomes very large, oval or elongated, with black skin, white flesh, very firm, not very juicy and with a more pungent flavor than the others; its foliage is also larger, and it does not usually go to seed in the year it is sown; so it is necessary to keep a few roots sheltered from winter, like carrots, and plant them in spring to obtain seed. GROWING. - Black radish requires stronger soil than other radishes: it is sown only once a year, from May to June, in a prepared bed as for other radishes; but, as it becomes much stronger, it must be sown lighter or thinned out when it has emerged 12 so that the plants are about 10 cm apart. If they are in light, dry soil, they need to be moistened a lot and often; it takes three months for them to reach roughly their natural size, so that radishes are eaten only in autumn and winter, because they keep in a cellar as well and better than carrots. Besides, not everyone eats black radishes, and only good stomachs make use of them; the weak fear them, so they are not widely cultivated. We are not talking about many other turnips and radishes of different sizes and colors, which, like our pink radish, are cultivated in vegetable gardens, because they are not sought after at the market and market gardeners should only grow what they know will sell. OBSERVATIONS. -Although we don't usually sow our first melons until the first days of February, we can nevertheless sow them around the 20th or 25th of December, as our colleague Mr. Gontier, a very skilled grower of several types of crops in Petit-Montrouge, Paris, has been doing for several years. He prefers the small white prescott for this season, and obtains ripe fruit around April 15th. At the same time, he also sows dwarf Dutch beans, a variety of our flageolet bean, but earlier; sown in this season, they produce green beans six weeks later. This very intelligent grower uses the thermosiphon in most of his forced cultivations under chassis. We are waiting for experience to confirm the advantages or disadvantages of this new heating system before adopting or rejecting it; moreover, the ever-increasing price of horse manure may soon force us to make a decision in this regard. 179 Now is the time to plant on beds a very large proportion of the lettuce and romaine plants that we have so carefully preserved under cloches on teens: there will still be some to plant until March; but January is the time when we begin our forced cultivation under cloches. To this end, during the course of the month, we make a number of winter layers, of the same thickness as those with frames, but on which we place only three rows of cloches (see the composition of these layers, chapter VIII); as we make them (1), we load them with potting soil which we let warm up before tucking it in, and which we level out afterwards by adding a little if necessary, so that it is 12 or 13 centimeters thick everywhere. Three rows of bells are placed on top of this compost, almost touching each other in a chessboard pattern, to save space. If the weather is frosty, you can (1) Some gardeners leave a distance of 33 centimeters between each layer, or a path 1 foot wide, and then fill it with new or mixed manure, which forms what is called a rechaud; but market gardeners, when putting up their layers, press them against each other, finding that they warm up just as well this way as with a rechaud. spread straw mats over the bells, to bring the soil up to the right temperature (30 degrees) as soon as possible; when the soil has reached this temperature, after the shot of fire if any, proceed with planting: we could first plant a romaine in the middle of the bell and then plant four small black lettuces around the romaine at a suitable distance; But our custom, and that of all early market gardeners, is to first plant the four lettuces at suitable distances from the center and edge of the bell, and then plant a romaine in the center, with the precaution that the lettuces are 8 centimeters from the glass of the bell, so that, as they grow, their leaves do not touch the glass, as they could be wilted by frost or burned by the sun. We also take the precaution of choosing the most beautiful romaine plant with a nice root ball to plant between the four lettuces. It's clear that in January, this planting must be done quickly, that when a bell is removed, the bell must be planted and the bell put back on immediately, but that in this season, we must take care not only that the frost doesn't reach the planting, but also that the bed doesn't cool down too quickly. These two disadvantages are countered by making a good accot around the beds, filling the paths with new, dry manure, carefully covering the bells with mats at night and even on days when it freezes or snows, not letting the snow melt on the mats or in the paths, and not neglecting to let the plant benefit from the sun through the glass whenever it shines. When the weather permits the bells to be lifted to visit the plants, we take the opportunity to remove dead or defective leaves and anything else that might cause rot. Black lettuce treated in this way is ready for sale towards the end of February; romaine is ready for binding four days after the lettuce is removed, and eight days later, in the first week of March, it is ready for consumption. In this way, layers are made successively from January to March, and planted with black lettuce and romaine in the manner just described; but in March, the layers can be made thinner, and eight days after a layer is planted, another foot of romaine is planted in each empty space left outside the bells : this last romaine doesn't come as quickly, of course, as the one under the bell; but, when the latter is removed, the bell is put back on the one that was left in the air, and this makes it move forward and cap quickly, and it can be delivered to the trade at the beginning of April, if it was bell-marked at the end of February.  CARROT. A plant of the Umbelliferae family and of the genus whose name it bears; its root is large, simple, taprooted and fusiform; its leaves are multifid, thrice winged and small; the stem rises to nearly 1 meter, branches a little, and each end is crowned with an umbel of small white flowers, followed by bristly fruits or seeds: only the root of this plant is used in the culinary arts. There are a dozen varieties of carrot in vegetable and field gardens, each with its own merits; but in market gardening, we grow only two. CARROT HATIVE. FORCED CULTIVATION. - It is sown in the first days of this month on winter beds (see this word in chapter VIII), on which chests are placed; In these boxes, a bed of potting soil 11 to 14 centimetres (4 to 5 inches) thick is placed, and when the temperature of the potting soil has reached the right point, the carrot seed is spread a little thicker than when sown in the ground (1), because the soil is too thick, when the carrot is sown, the seed is covered with a centimetre or so of potting soil, which is then sealed with a plasterboard, as the carrot seed, being bristling with hooked hairs, needs to be sealed more than any other so as not to leave a gap between it and the soil. Once the seed has emerged, we give it as much air as the season allows, and whenever there is a day or a few hours of mild weather, we remove the frame panels entirely and replace them promptly when the weather changes. It goes without saying that these young carrots are protected from frost by all the means already indicated. If you leave no stone unturned in this cultivation, you can harvest these carrots, which are good for sale, in the course of March and continue into April; they are highly prized and much sought-after at this time, firstly because they are not large, and secondly because they are better than carrots from the previous year that have been kept over winter, because of their newness, tenderness and red color. They mix thirty black lettuces per frame panel, and for this purpose they have sown the carrot a little light; but as the black lettuce needs no air to peel, and the carrot needs a lot to come well under the frame, these two plants can hardly be grown together as early crops, as the air is favorable to one and harmful to the other. However, the need to increase the number of early seasons means that they can be grown together under frame. O8 HATIVE CARROT IN SECOND SEASON. CULTIVATION. - This carrot is still sown in February and March on spring beds (see this word in chapter VIII), without a frame and without a bell; but two rows of gaulettes must be established on these beds to support the mats that are covered at night, and to protect the carrots from frost: these carrots succeed the previous ones until the coastal ones replace them. HALF-LONG CARROT. This is grown only in the open ground by Paris market gardeners, and we sow it from the end of January, weather permitting, until August. We sow our first half-length carrot in January or February, in a coastal plot, where we plant our first romaine in the open ground. Remember that we've already said that, when you sow and plant a bed at the same time, you start with the sowing. So, when the coast is ploughed and harrowed, we sow the carrot, harrow it a second time and press it down with our feet; we then spread a centimetre-thick bed of potting soil over the whole coast, and press it down again, because carrot seed, in particular, needs to be well pressed down; after which, we plant the green romaine, and even the cauliflowers, if March is approaching: carrots sown in this way in the coast start to yield towards the end of May. Once March arrives, we sow the carrot in a square, mixing in a few radish, lettuce or romaine seeds, or we plant lettuce or romaine right away. All these vegetables are removed before the carrot is ready, giving it room to fortify itself. Half-long carrots are sown every month until August. The product of the last sowing is sold in the autumn, and is more tender and sought-after at this time than the large carrots sown in spring. We must not forget to mention that carrots are very water-loving, and that all summer seedlings must be supported in the wet. OBSERVATION. - We have already said, on page 178, that melons can be sown in December; they can also be sown in January, with greater hopes of success, especially if the thermosiphon is used in the frames. But this device has not yet been introduced into market gardening, although Mr. Gontier has been using it on his fermented crops for several years. Once it has been clearly established that the advantages of the thermosiphon outweigh the expense of its installation and maintenance, market gardeners will inevitably introduce it into their crops with all the more eagerness, as the ever-increasing price of horse manure is beginning to cut into their profits: so far, we haven't sown our first melons until February, FÉVRIER. MELON. In the climate of Paris, the cultivation of melons is always more or less forced, because the heat arrives too late in spring and the cold returns too early in autumn for this plant to produce its fruit with the perfection appropriate to its species. We are therefore obliged to give the melon the warmth and shelter that our climate denies it, during its first growth and often during its first five or six months of existence. The melon is an annual plant with a single flower (1), belonging to the cucurbitaceae family and the cucumber genus; it has slender roots that trace (1) I.e., the plant bears two kinds of flowers, some male and others female: the male flowers are the first to appear and are always the most numerous; they can be recognized by the fact that they lack an ovary; the female flowers are solitary, larger and can be recognized by the fact that they have a large olive-shaped ovary below them. In gardening, the male flower of melons and cucumbers is called a false flower, and the female flower is called a maille. The melon is the only part of the plant that can be eaten: it grows up to 2 meters around the stem, a few centimeters below the surface of the earth; its stem, which is twiggy, has alternate leaves and axillary flowers, crawls on the ground and branches out; its fruit, which is oval or rounded, smooth, embroidered, grooved and larger or smaller depending on the variety, is the only part that can be eaten. As melon growing is one of the main branches of market gardening in Paris, we're going to go into all the details of our practice, as we generally do it today, because it hasn't always been the same, and it may undergo modifications in the future: For example, when we only grew brodé or maraîcher melon, cultivation of this variety was quite simple; now that cantaloupes are rightly preferred to maraîcher melon, cultivation has been enriched with new processes to obtain cantaloupes in all their perfection. A new species may one day require new ways of cultivating it successfully and profitably, for without profit there can be no market gardening. It is pointless to prove that market gardening cannot be sustained without profit; but it is not irrelevant, if not to prove, then at least to show that we cannot and must not grow certain melons which are highly prized for their earliness, but so small that, even if they were of superior quality, we would not be able to sell them for what their cultivation would cost us; we are talking about the orange melon or cantaloupe and some of its varieties. Certainly, these melons are earlier than the ones we grow: they are sown from the first days of December, and ripe fruit is obtained from the first days of April; but these fruits are as big as apples or as big as a fist, and certainly their sale at the market would not come close to paying for their cultivation costs, supposing, however, that we could sell them. Quite apart from the price, the Paris public does not easily become accustomed to new horticultural productions with which it is unfamiliar: how long did it take to accustom it to preferring cantaloupe to market garden melon? In short, market gardeners can and must only grow vegetables that have an established price at the Paris market, and must wait for other horticultural or agricultural products whose qualities are recommended by those who know them, or by a few lovers of novelties, to be sought after or quoted at the Paris market before starting to grow them. The melon is the vegetable plant with the most varieties; some are highly valued in one country, others in another. In Paris, a few varieties of cantaloupe are now the most sought-after, and of course these are the ones that market gardeners prefer to grow. The manner and timing of melon sowing and rearing are fairly uniform among most gardeners; but the need for and manner of pruning are judged very differently by many gardeners and theorists. The market gardeners of Paris are the ones who reason least about melon pruning; but the constant success of their practice is there to assure that they are on the right track. We divide melon cultivation into three seasons: 1. early, 2. in trenches, 3. on beds. EARLY SOWING. In the first days of February (1), a mother bed is made (see this word in chapter VIII), loaded with a one-panel box in which a 10-centimeter-thick bed of potting soil is placed, and covered with a frame. When the soil has cooled down to a temperature of 30 degrees centigrade at a depth of 8 centimeters, we (1) This is generally the time when we sow our first melon; but it could be done as early as December (see our observations, page 178). sow the melon seed in rows or florets, covering it with 15 millimeters of potting soil; replace the frame, place a mat on top and leave it until the seed has emerged, which takes four or five days; As soon as the seed is out of the ground, the mat is removed during the day, so that the young plant can benefit from the light and doesn't wither, and put back every evening at the right time; moreover, if frost is to be feared, this is counteracted by known means. When the envelope lifted by the cotyledons has fallen away, it's time to make another layer, called the nursery layer, of the same thickness as the previous one, but long enough to accommodate a two- or three-panel cofret, as it takes seven or eight days for the compost in this new layer to reach the temperature indicated above, and during this time the plant has grown sufficiently to be ready for transplanting into the nursery. There are two ways of transplanting, and as each has its advocates, we'll explain them both, followed by our own opinion. FIRST WAY-When the potting soil in the nursery layer has reached a suitable temperature, go to the mother layer, lift the seedling by passing your hand or a trowel under the roots and weighing it down; then pull the melon seedling out of the ground, taking care not to damage its roots, and transplant it by hand (which is preferable to using a planter) into the potting soil in the new layer, pushing the stem in as far as the cotyledons and placing the seedlings 12 centimetres apart. As soon as the width of a panel has been transplanted, the frame is immediately folded back, covered with a mat and left like this for three or four days to help the plant take root; after this time, daylight is restored and the plant continues to be managed according to the season. SECOND METHOD. - When the nursery bed is made, the box placed and the potting soil spread out, don't wait for the shot to be fired; immediately push in empty melon pots (seventy-five to eighty per panel), fill them with good soft soil mixed halfway with potting soil, compact a little by hand, and immediately close the frames, covering them with straw mats if necessary to hasten fermentation. When the soil in these pots has reached the required temperature, we transplant a single melon seedling into each of them by hand or with a planter, with all due care, and close the frames, on which we place mats as in the other method and for the same reason. We'll now give you our thoughts on these two methods. If the melon, transplanted in this way in a pot, could be O planted on the first day it is ready to be planted, these two ways of transplanting it could be almost indifferent; but it very rarely happens that a melon can be planted as soon as it is ready to be planted; it is most often planted only six, eight or ten days later, and sometimes even later: When you want to plant it permanently, you pick it up with both hands, using a good clod, and place it in the hole that has been prepared for it, without its roots being twisted. If, on the other hand, a melon plant transplanted into a small pot remains there for eight or ten days longer than necessary, its roots are forced to curl; this, in our opinion, delays its proper establishment in the hole where it is to be planted permanently. This latter planting has the advantage, it's true, of being able to be done more quickly, even in unfavorable weather, but we prefer transplanting in potting soil. PLANTING EARLY MELONS. We grow the small grey prescott as an early or first-season melon, but whatever the season and the melon, we always cut off the head four or five days before planting, while it is still on the nursery bed, because in this state, the wound caused by removing the head heals more quickly and much more reliably than when this operation is postponed until after planting. To carry out our first planting, here's how we proceed; in February, we usually have winter beds that have already yielded small black lettuce or radishes, and which are empty. Then we take the soil covering them elsewhere, and, as the manure with which they are composed has not yet been used up, we break it up, bring in new manure with which we mix it well, and make other winter layers in place of the old ones. When the first layer has been built up, we place boxes in it, and in these boxes we spread 13 to 14 centimeters (5 inches) thick of good, loose soil, then place the frames; when the heat has fallen to the right point (around 30 degrees centigrade at a depth of 10 centimeters in the soil), we plant the melons that we have left on the nursery layer in potting soil or in pots: first make a row of medium-sized holes in the middle of the layer, at the rate of two per panel, or only make the holes as 13 you plant them. However, the holes must be made in advance to accommodate the melon plants transplanted in potting soil: we'll start with these. You go to the nursery bed, push both hands into the soil, one to the right and the other to the left of a seedling, lift it with a good lump of soil and then place it with the lump in the hole prepared for it on the new bed; you spread its roots properly, cover them and press the soil lightly; then you go and find another seedling which you plant in the same way, and so on. When the planting is finished, or even better, while it's being done, you sprinkle about three quarters of a liter of water on each planted melon, to help it take root, and you immediately replace the frames, to avoid wind or cold. Similar planting of melons transplanted into pots is less time-consuming and less difficult, because you can go to the nursery bed and pick up a wheelbarrow of pots in one go and take it to where the melons are to be planted: There, the market gardener takes a pot with his right hand, inverts it into his left, opening his fingers a little to let the melon stem pass through, and, tapping a little on the bottom of the pot, the melon and its rootball come out easily; then holding this rootball with his left hand, if the hole is not made, he makes it with his right hand, places the rootball of the melon in it, bounds it appropriately, waters it and replaces the frames as before. We should point out here that, although when transplanting melons to the nursery bed, they have been pushed down to the cotyledons, the stem has often grown by 4 to 7 centimetres, and that when planting them permanently, this part should be buried until close to the first leaf, as new roots should develop which will increase the plant's vigour. We should also point out that some gardeners have raised the question of whether to leave or remove the cotyledons (coquilletons, in market gardener's terms) from melons before or after planting: As these parts naturally dry out on the plant, some people are not in the habit of removing them; but these organs are sometimes attacked by rot, so they remove them to the core and cauterize the wound with ashes, plaster or powdered lime, so that the rot does not spread to the stem: as for pruning, we'll deal with that at the end of the various melon plantations (see our opinion on removing cotyledons, page 207).
