It is not enough to have planted melons as we have just said; six or eight days after planting, you need to establish a good bed around the layers, fill the paths with dry manure 6 well pressed up to the top of the trunks, give the melons some air during the day, whenever the weather permits, and cover the frames every night, double or even triple the mats if frost is threatening, uncover when the sun is shining so that the melons can warm up in its light through the glass, and beware of humidity, which is no less harmful to melons than frost; To prevent it, we have no other means than to provide air whenever the weather permits, and to ensure that the melons are always clean, by carefully removing any leaves that might deteriorate and cause rot. Petit prescott melons, sown and planted in February, can, if the year is not too unfavorable, produce ripe fruit from May 10 to 15. SECOND-SEASON MELONS. Market gardeners in Paris prefer cantaloupe gros prescott fond noir and cantaloupe gros prescott fond blanc for second-season cultivation in trenches, i.e. they grow these melons in trenched beds (see this word, chapter VIII), covered with soil instead of compost. These melons are sown from February 20 to 25, on a mother bed, and transplanted to a nursery bed in exactly the same way as the small prescott we've just been talking about: they are also topped in the nursery five or six days before planting. When the time comes to plant the melon seedling permanently, we make trenches in the square intended for it, which must always be exposed to the sun. We direct these trenches, as far as we can, from east to west, so that the glass of the frames receives direct midday sun. Here's how we make these trenches: we open the first one on the edge of the square, make it 1 metre wide and 33 centimetres deep, and carry the earth to the place where the last trench is to be made. When this first trench is made, a layer of half new manure and half old manure, well mixed and wetted if necessary to develop the appropriate heat, is put in. This layer must be 66 centimetres thick or 33 centimetres above the ground; as soon as this is done, a second trench is opened, 66 centimetres from the first and parallel to it, and the soil drawn from it, after dividing it well, is thrown onto the layer in the first trench, and a layer is made in this second trench similar to the first, which is covered with the soil from a third trench, and so on until the last trench is reached, where the soil from the first trench is found to load the last layer. But let's go back to the first two layers, because there's an important observation to be made here: these two layers rise 35 centimetres above the ground, and between them there is a path 66 centimetres wide, which will be reduced to 33 centimetres when the chests are placed on top of the layers; the layers themselves, as they settle, will be level with the ground, and, as the melon roots run far out near the surface of the earth, we must assume that they will extend into the paths by passing under the boards of the chests: With this in mind, the soil in the paths should be ploughed to make it soft and permeable, and then filled with manure similar to that in the layers, well trodden down to the height of the layers themselves. It goes without saying that before laying the trunks, you must make a bank along the outside of the first layer, so that they can be supported on that side, since they are 33 centimetres wider than the layers, and must overhang by 16 centimetres (6 inches) on each side. When a layer is loaded with soil, we place the chests on it, lining them up with a chalk line, break up the soil inside and spread it out so that it is 12 to 14 centimetres thick everywhere, and place the frames; when -~-. R - two layers have been made, and the chests and frames are on top, manure is again placed in the path between the two layers, which is now only 33 centimetres wide, right up to the top edge of the chests, compacting it well with your feet as you did the first time. The manure placed in this way in the paths is called rechaud, while we call accot that placed around the layers; as it sinks, we recharge it with new or mixed manure, but we never reshuffle it; that is, we never heat our layers as is done in many vegetable gardens. On these trenched beds, we can plant the small prescott cantaloupe we mentioned on page 192; but for this second season, we prefer the white-bottomed large prescott cantaloupe, and the black- or gray-bottomed cantaloupe, because they are consistently better than many others, and more certain of defeat: we raise them, as mentioned above, on a mother bed and on a nursery bed, and, when the warmth of the soil in the trench beds has dropped to a suitable point, we plant them at two plants per panel, with the care and precautions already indicated, and leave no stone unturned to preserve them from the cold during the bad season. As for pruning, we'll explain that below; but we must say straight away that these melons, sown between February 20 and 25, transplanted around March 2 or 4, and planted around the 25th of the same month, give ripe fruit in the last fortnight of June. THIRD-SEASON MELONS. Third-season melons are sown from the end of March to the first days of May, and always in the same way, i.e. on the mother bed and transplanted to the nursery bed, where they undergo the "étêtement" process: for this season, we choose cantaloupe gros prescott fond blanc, cantaloup gros galeux vert and others. And, instead of planting them in trays, we plant them in cloches on deaf layers (see this word, article VIII); in market gardening, deaf layers are made in this way. They are planted successively in a well-exposed square; a trench is dug 65 centimetres wide and ho centimetres deep, and the soil is brought to where the last layer is to be finished. trench with half new, half old manure, well mixed, well spread and well pressed with the feet, and so that the middle of the layer is 33 centimeters above the ground, and the sides -- - - ==iAAM -A" - go down in the back of a chest almost at its level; when this first convex layer has been made, a second trench is opened 66 centimetres from the first, the earth is well divided and deposited on the first layer, keeping the manure's curved back shape, and using a fork and rake to give it a smooth surface under the curved shape; then place a line of bells 66 centimetres apart on the highest point or in the middle, and let the layer warm up; the second trench is treated in the same way, and so on down to the last. When there is no longer any fear of fire, plant a melon plant under each bell, water it immediately and cover the bell with litter or a straw mat during the day, to protect it from the sun until it picks up again, which should happen after three or four days. When the branches of the melons ask to come out of the cloches, a good mulch should be spread over the whole extent of the layers, so that the soil doesn't dry out and the roots of the melons, which will soon extend close to its surface, find it soft and fresh; at the same time or a few days later, the paths should be ploughed and also covered with a good mulch, because the roots of the melons, after having crossed the soil of their layer, extend as far as the paths, and even as far as the neighboring layer. Melons planted in this way in early May ripen their fruit in August. FOURTH-SEASON MELONS. In this fourth and final season, we grow the large black mangy cantaloupe; we also include a few brodé or market garden melons, although this ancient species is far inferior to cantaloupes in terms of price and quality; as its cultivation is also different from that of cantaloupes, we will treat it separately. This season's melons are sown and transplanted in exactly the same way as their predecessors, but later, because they can only be planted permanently at the end of April and during May, as the space intended for them is occupied until then. They must follow lettuces, romaine, cauliflower, radishes and carrots planted or sown in layers, and these layers are only emptied successively from the beginning of April until May. As the layers are emptied, the top soil is removed: at this time, their manure is not consumed; We then break up the layer, divide and shake out the manure, mix in a third or half of new manure, and rebuild the layers in the same place, one against the other, always giving them 1 meter 66 centimeters (5 feet) in width, but only 40 centimeters (15 inches) in height, as we do for our spring layers which, given the season, don't need to be as thick as the winter ones. When a layer is set up, as described in chapter VIII, it is covered with compost, and after this compost has been edged, the entire layer must be 1 meter 32 centimeters wide and 12 or 15 centimeters thick. The compost leaves 16 centimeters of empty space on each side of the layer, so that two layers set against each other leave a 32-centimeter-wide space between them where there is no compost, and this space forms a necessary pathway common to two layers. As for fire, there is little to fear in layers assembled like these with at least half the manure already used, and which are only lo centimeters thick; however, it is always wise to check the degree of heat they may have in the potting soil four or five days after they are loaded, and to plant them only when you no longer fear that the heat of the potting soil will rise above 30 degrees centigrade, at 8 or 9 centimeters deep in the potting soil. But as soon as the potting soil has been edged, filled and levelled, draw a line down the middle and place a row of bells 66 centimetres apart from center to center. When the heat under the bells has reached the right point, plant a melon under each one, water it immediately and shade it with flexible litter placed on top of each bell. Some market gardeners fill the paths with well-crushed new manure as soon as they've lined the soil of their beds, for fear of having it crumble as they walk along the paths; but most wait until the melons have been planted before doing this, for fear of increasing the heat of the bed at that first moment when it's sufficiently warm; as the paths deepen, they are filled with new manure to keep them as high as the soil in the layers, and the layers are always covered with straw mats whenever manure is brought into the paths, so that it doesn't fall on the plants, and the square of layers is surrounded by a bundle. MELON BRODÉ OR MARAICHER. Since the introduction of cantaloupes into market gardening, the embroidered melon has become much less prized and its cultivation greatly diminished, because it is inferior to them in quality, and even lacks that inherent to its species. Today, you wouldn't dare present an embroidered melon on a table of any distinction, and many market gardeners have given up cultivating it; if a few of them still grow it, it's because it's simpler to cultivate, and it's easier to grow.

and that these fruits are more affordable than cantaloupes. In market gardening, the embroidered melon is sown at the beginning of May, transplanted around the 10th or 12th, and planted in place towards the end of the month, i.e. the last of the melons, on beds made like those of fourth-season cantaloupes; but, instead of putting just one row in the middle of the bed, two rows are planted 35 centimetres from the edges and 66 centimetres apart in the rows (1). Although we have just assigned the end of May for the planting of embroidered melons, there are still some market gardeners who sow them as early as the first days of April and plant them towards the end of the same month; They then obtain ripe fruit in the last fortnight of July, whereas those who plant it at the end of May only obtain (1) In the past, it was planted less closely, with only one row per layer, and the fruit was larger, because the roots found more room to spread. that in August and until September 15th, when the police forbid eating melons, because they believe that in this season, embroidered or market melons can give fever to those who eat them. THE SIZE OF MELONS. So far, we've only talked about melons up to the time of planting, but they require further care up to the time when their fruit ripens to compensate us for our trouble, and of all this care, pruning is the most important and the one on which there are widely differing opinions, which can be summed up in these two questions: Should we prune little? Should we prune a lot? Paris market gardeners answer yes to the second question, because since melon cultivation is entirely artificial in our climate, we must continue to use unnatural methods to force this plant to give us fruit that is bigger and earlier than it would naturally. The three or four types of cantaloupe that we grow, either under frames or under cloches, are pruned in the same way, so what we are about to say is applicable to all of them. -- -O First pruning. 207 The first pruning we count is the topping we give our melons before planting them, when they are still in the nursery. Many gardeners don't do this until the melons have been planted; but then moisture is more of a problem, and rot can set in on the wound, whereas it heals much more easily and quickly when the melons are still in the nursery. All gardeners perform this first pruning in much the same way, cutting the young melon plant about 12 millimeters (6 lines) above the second leaf. As for the removal of cotyledons, auricles or coquilletons, in market gardening terms, it's a different matter, not all gardeners agree; some leave them or only remove them if they see rot; For us, we always remove the two cotyledons and any buds they may have in their axils at the same time as topping them on the nursery bed, and we do this double operation, as far as possible, on a sunny day, and at the same time give them a little air, so that the wounds heal more quickly. From the time the melons are planted and taken up again, until it is necessary to prune them a second time, there is nothing else to do but to preserve them from the cold by all the means already indicated, to preserve them from sunburn if it is already strong, and to give them air whenever the temperature will allow it. Second pruning. The first pruning has led to the development of two opposite branches which naturally extend over the ground; when they are about 33 centimetres long, they are pruned above the fourth leaf; this is the time to mulch melons planted under a frame, because after the second pruning their branches will extend on all sides, and later it would not be so easy to spread mulch under their branches to keep them away from the ground and drier. The time to give the second pruning to melons planted under cloche coincides with the time when these plants need to extend their two arms outside the cloche; When these two arms have also been pruned to four leaves, mulch is spread over the whole layer, and the cloches are lifted with one, two or three racks, so that the melon branches can emerge and spread over the mulch; but, if at this time there is a fear of cold, do not neglect to unroll mulch 2 over the cloches during the night. 2n1 14 r -5 n at(. Third size. .2" "." -1 "..El 2.1.1.1 1,.1162 61 The two arms cut above the fourth leaf at the second pruning should have branched out and produced three to four branches each, making six to eight branches in all; when the largest of these eight branches have lengthened by about 33 centimetres; they are all pruned above their third leaf, and laid out properly around the foot, so that they do not cross and confuse each other. We've already seen a few false flowers, perhaps a mesh or two; but it's only ~ ~ after the third pruning that the meshes show up in sufficient quantity to make a choice. We must warn you that the humidity of the bed is sufficient to keep the soil or loam covering it in a state of freshness suitable for melon vegetation, and that it is hardly until the fruits have reached a certain size that watering becomes necessary; however, if by some circumstance the soil were to become dry before this time, watering would have to be brought forward : we must also warn 14 that we must not neglect to give air to melons under frames every day, by raising the panels by 2, 4, 6 and 12 centimetres according to the temperature; as for melons under cloches, air is not a problem for them, since at the third pruning, the cloches covering them had .had to be lifted to a height of 6 or 7 centimetres by means of three racks. Fourth pruning. Branches which have grown after the third pruning have most of the meshes when the fourth pruning is carried out; in general, this pruning is done above the second leaf, but the presence of meshes sometimes obliges us to make a certain modification to this rule; thus, if there is a mesh in the axil of a second leaf, we are obliged to prune the branch above the third leaf, because we must always leave an œit above the young fruit to attract the sap. Fifth pruning. All branches born after the fourth pruning are cut off above their first leaf, and this fifth pruning may be repeated three or four times, so that the branches DE PARIS. 214 of the melon under frame do not exceed the width of the trunk and those of the melon under cloche do not exceed the width of the layer, and also to maintain vegetation in the body of the whole plant. Now back to the mesh: we've just said that it's after the third pruning that they appear in greater numbers; now we have to say that, in Paris market gardening, we generally leave only one fruit on each melon plant, because we want to obtain the best-formed and fattest melon possible, and when two are produced at the same time, neither reaches the volume that a single one would have obtained; and it's after the third pruning, when the meshes come in large numbers, that we choose the one that seems best placed and best shaped to become a beautiful fruit: when this mesh or another has reached the size of a pigeon's egg, we say, such and such a mesh knots; when it's as big as a hen's egg, we say, such and such a mesh is knotted; then we remove the other meshes, and it's this operation that we call enameling. But it takes a tact that can only be acquired through practice, to recognize whether a knotted or knotted mesh will produce a beautiful fruit; the rapidity of the mesh's growth, the fresh tone of its green are good omens; as for shape, 0 7 if the end that holds the tail is bigger than the other end, this is not a bad sign; but if the end opposite the tail is bigger, and the mesh is even slightly bottle-shaped, then it's a bad omen, and we pin our hopes on another mesh. We even sometimes remove large melons that are not growing as fast as we'd like, or that don't seem to be well formed, and replace them with more attractive meshes. Although we generally keep only one melon on each plant, we sometimes allow a second one to grow, if available, when the first has reached at least three-quarters of its size. All melon pruning is carried out within eight to fifteen days of each other, depending on temperature and the progress of vegetation; but, during this period, melons require further care from the first pruning to the last; First of all, when they are under frame or cloche, they must be given air whenever the outside temperature allows, and taken out of it during the night, as long as frost is to be feared, and they must not be watered until they have set fruit. We don't recommend keeping them free of weeds, that goes without saying; but we do recommend being careful from the third pruning onwards, removing 13 of the old grey or yellowish leaves which no longer perform any useful function, and which produce nothing but confusion and harmful shade; spacing the branches well apart and making room for the young fruit. When the fruit has set, the soil under the frames needs to be moistened, so give half or a whole watering can of water for each panel, drenching all the soil in the panel and the leaves of the melons, because at this time the root tips are already far from the base, and it's these extremities in particular that need moisture. As for the frequency of watering, this depends on atmospheric temperature: if it's cold, water only every ten or twelve days; but if the weather is fine, and the sun shines often and for a long time, water every five or six days. .1 As for melons planted under cloches, since the soil on their bed can be soaked by rain, even more than necessary, it's up to experience to teach when they need watering. In ordinary years, melons are depanelled in trenches at the end of May or in the first days of June, and the bells are left for eight or fifteen days longer on the melons planted the latest. CULTIVATION. - The last name of this melon reminds us that, some time ago, market gardeners only cultivated it, even though the cantaloupe was already known from other crops; but finally, for the last fifty years or so, the cantaloupe has been generally cultivated by Paris market gardeners, and the cultivation of the melon maraîcher is tightening up every day and will probably end up being abandoned one day. In the meantime, here's how it's still grown today: This melon is sown and transplanted like the cantaloupe, but only at the end of April or in the first days of May; it is planted on spring bed following the large green mangy cantaloupe; but, instead of putting only one row in the middle of the bed, two rows are put in, each pang 33 centimetres from the edge and 66 centimetres apart in the rows, and they are bell planted as usual: all other care, up to and including pruning, is the same as for cantaloupes under cloche. Pruning market garden melons. - We prune this melon as many times as the cantaloupe, but instead of varying the length of the pruning at four, three, two and one leaf, we do them all above the second leaf. If one plant seems too bushy, we remove a few branches to make room for the others. In any case, we give it the same care as the cantaloupe. Melon ripening. - The time needed for a melon to ripen is counted from the moment it is tied to the moment it is struck, and this distance is usually forty to fifty days. As for the length of time a melon remains ripe, this depends mainly on two things: firstly, the temperature of the place where it is placed, and the amount of water it has inside; low temperatures tend to preserve, and high temperatures tend to decompose; but, without taking any extraordinary care, a cantaloupe melon will keep in a good state of ripeness for five or eight days. If we now want to summarize our various melon plantings and the various ripening periods, we'll find that the February planting gives fruit in May; the March planting gives fruit in June and July; the April and May plantings give fruit in August and September. CONCOMPER. Annual, creeping plant of the Cucurbitaceae fa mille and of the same genus as the melon; but it differs from the latter in that its fruits do not have the same shape or flavor, and are only eaten cooked or candied. There are far fewer varieties of cucumber than melon. .11 - WHITE HATIF CUCUMBER. i FORCED CULTIVATION. - From the first days of February, a mother layer (see this word) is made, on which the cucumber seed is sown. A few days later, we make a nursery bed, and when it is at the right temperature, we transplant the young plant, at a rate of fifty to fifty-five plants per panel, leaving a distance of 16 to 18 centimeters between the front of the trunk and the plant, so that it is not too shaded, and we give it the same care as for melon plants, i.e. we shade it while it is recovering, and then let it enjoy the sun; As cucumbers are less delicate than melons, they are not usually topped on the nursery bed, but rather waited until they have been planted before being topped. Early cucumbers are planted on a winter bed and under a frame; four per panel are placed at a suitable distance from the wood and between them, with a good motté, as far as possible, and shaded during the two or three days of recovery, if the sun comes to shine; when the plant begins to work, we top it off above the second leaf, forcing it to grow two arms; then we cover all the soil with the when the arms have lengthened to 33 centimetres, we taylle them above the second or third leaf. When the pruned branches have sprouted branches about 33 centimetres long, we prune them a second time, after the second or third leaf: we even prune again, and for the third and last time, a fortnight later and always in the same way; during this time we protect ourselves from frost and cold by the means indicated, and we give air as often as the weather allows: but each time you prune, take care to spread out the branches well, to prevent them from growing, and to remove any yellow leaves that may be present. Cucumbers are more mesh-like than melons, and knot more easily, but only one is allowed to knot at a time. When the first fruit has reached two-thirds of its size, another mesh is left to knot, but never two at a time in forced cultivation at 4 feet per panel. Although we only let one fruit tie at a time, a cucumber plant soon has a dozen, Du. and that's all we allow it to bear 98 in this season and under panel to have them beautiful and of a good size. As they grow, we continue to remove the yellow leaves, shelter them from the cold, give them air; and, if their soil dries out, water it sufficiently, as a cucumber plant with ten to twelve fruits to feed needs moisture at its roots. A cucumber fruit should not ripen on the vine like a melon; we pick it long before it is ripe, but we wait to pick it until it has acquired almost all its natural length and size; and this happens about twenty-five days after it is tied, so that we have cucumbers of this season from April 15 to June. LARGE SLICED CUCUMBER. CULTIVATION. - In the first fortnight of April, sow the seed on a piece of warm bed under a frame, or under a choche, if you lack a frame. As soon as the seed has emerged, prepare a spring bed, cover it with a frame or cloche, and when it has reached the right temperature, transplant the young cucumber plant; In a fortnight, the seedling is ready to be planted out; a trench 66 centimetres wide and 35 centimetres deep is then made, in a well-exposed spot, and as long as desired. In the trench, make a 50-centimeter-high layer of half-new, half-old manure, which is wetted to ferment properly (this should be done when raising all the layers, when the manure is dry), and load it with the soil from the trench, 16 to 20 centimeters thick; divide and level this soil well, and place a row of bells in the middle, 1 meter apart. A few days later, the heat will have risen in the soil, and by sticking his hand into it, the experienced market gardener can tell whether its temperature has reached the right point for planting. When the time comes, he removes the bells, makes a hole where each one used to be, lifts the plants by hand, with a good clod, and plants them in the holes, pushing them down to the cotyledons, watering them immediately, replacing the bells on each plant, and shading them, during the recovery period, with straw mats or litter. After three days, the seedling must be taken up again; then it is given light and air, by lifting the bell, on the side opposite the sóleil, with a rack. When the plant has six or eight leaves, it is topped above the third leaf, sprouting shoots which soon fill the bell; this is the time to spread a good mulch on the bed and allow the shoots to spread, by making them come out of the bell and supporting it above the feet, by means of three racks. These cucumbers, planted on soil warmed by a layer, grow more vigorously than those previously planted in potting soil under a frame; they are only subjected to two successive, but longer, prunings; They are usually pruned after the fourth leaf, and the plant is allowed to set two fruits at the same time, until each plant has up to fifteen; during this time, the season has allowed the bells to be removed, and the cucumbers have to be carefully watered. It's understood that the branches must always be well spread out, and all yellow leaves removed as they come into view. This plantation can bear fruit from the last fortnight of June to the end of August. If you run out of manure, instead of planting the cucumber on a trench bed, proceed as follows: make round or square holes, 66 centimetres in diameter and 33 centimetres deep; fill these holes with well-pressed, mixed sumier, load it with soil and plant a cucumber plant on each hole, treating it as described above. 221 CULTIVATION. - This type of cultivation does not work well in strong, cold soil; it should only be undertaken in light, warm, fertile and sheltered soil. The market gardeners of Paris practice it in their "côtières", with seedlings raised on beds and calculated in such a way that they are only ready to be planted around the 15th of May. At this time, if the coastal planting still contains lettuce or romaine, one of these is pulled up every 53 centimetres; the soil is turned over and divided with a spade to a depth of 33 centimetres, and a cucumber plant, which has been lifted with a good clod, is planted in each place; We water immediately, cover the plant with a bell to deprive it of air, and shade the bell to protect it from the sun; three days later, we give it back light and air by removing the shade and the bell; soon after, we head it, pruning it like those planted on a trench bed, keeping it even more moist because of its more desiccating position. This plantation bears fruit from August to September. GHERKIN. The gherkin is of the same family and genus as the cucumber, 7" CULTURE MARAICHÈRE: it is even just a species of cucumber, the fruit of which is verucose, can be picked while still green to the size of a finger, and can only be used candied in vinegar; in this state, it keeps from one year to the next. CULTIVATION. - At the beginning of May, gherkin seeds are sown on a spring bed, under a frame or bell, and transplanted to another bed, like cucumbers. By the end of the month, the plant is ready to set. In market gardening, the gherkin is generally planted in beds that have already been planted in the marsh; small plots are ploughed, 1 meter apart, in one or two rows of these beds, and a gherkin plant is planted there; We water it, shade it for a few days with a little bedding, and when it's well established, we top it off above the third leaf; soon the board is empty and the gherkin's arms "have room to spread out on the soil, which we've been careful to clean and cover with mulch. Some do a first pruning, as they do with cucumbers, while others don't prune at all, simply spreading the branches over the mulch. Gherkins should not be neglected at the wet end, nor in the retrenchment of their yellow leaves. Planted in this very simple way, the corpiIEI( chon bears fruit abundantly, from the 03 beginning of August to the end of September, and can be picked every two days, because it takes only eight days after it is tied to reach the right size for sale. CHICORIES. Plants of the Compositae family and of the order whose name they bear: there are annuals and perennials; some grow to less than 1 meter, others up to a meter; the radical leaves, whole or more or less cut, are the only edible ones; all have blue flowers and crowned seeds. FINE ITALIAN CHICORY. CULTURE PORCEE. - The seed of this chicory is sown in the first days of February, on a very warm bed under a frame. Some cover the seed with a or 5 millimeters of compost; others do not cover it at all. As soon as the seed is sown, the frames are lowered and covered with straw mats, so that the seed is in the dark and germinates more quickly. In twenty-four or thirty hours, the seed is germinated: light is then given back to it so that the shoots turn green; in the evening, the mats are put back in place and the plant is protected from frost by all known means. The seedling grows quickly in the high temperature of the bed, and twelve days after the seed is sown, the plant is strong enough to be transplanted. This period had to be planned for, and a nursery layer filled with 10 centimetres of potting soil and covered with a frame had to be set up; the potting soil is pressed down and the young chicory plant is transplanted into it with the finger, while it is still warm, at a rate of three hundred and fifty to four hundred plants per panel, taking care that the front row is 16 centimetres from the wood of the trunk: as you transplant, you replace the frame and cover it with a mat, and so on, until all the transplanting is done; the day after, you can give light back to the seedling, continuing to cover it in the evening and protect it from the cold: when the seedling is well established and you can see it growing, you give it air during the day whenever the weather permits. After the plant has been transplanted for about thirty days, it's time to think about planting it out; we then make spring beds, fill them with 14 to 18 centimetres of potting soil and cover them with frames, and plant the chicory in them at a rate of thirty-six to forty plants per panel. We must make one observation here: when we go to the nursery bed to pick up the seedling, we mustn't pull it out like turnips; on the contrary, we must pass our hand under the roots, lift it up, so that plenty of compost remains between its rootlets (this is what we call a clod), Then plant it by hand on the new layer, pushing it in only as far as the collar, because chicory, like lettuce, has to spread its leaves over the soil, and would be hindered in its growth if the lower part of its leaves were buried. As soon as you've planted a panel or two, replace the frames and shade for two or three days; then give it light during the day and cover every night. When you see the chicory growing, you give it air every day, for as long as the season allows. This chicory has the most finely cut leaves, and should not come as large as autumn chicory; when it is full and about 22 centimeters wide, we tie it with a piece of straw; ten days later, we untie a foot to see if it is white enough, and, if it is suitably white, we uproot it, pare it and deliver it for consumption. Fine chicory, sown in the first days of February and treated in this way, can be sold towards the end of May. SEMI-FINE CHICORY. This is distinguished by being less curly, less cut and larger than the previous variety. 15 CULTIVATION - In the first half of March, sow the seed of this chicory on a very hot mother bed, and transplant it to an equally hot nursery bed, as you did for the previous variety. When the plant is well established, it is given air and, if the weather is fine, the frames are removed to allow the plant to harden off, and put back on at night if frost is feared. In the first days of April, the plant will be ready to be planted in the ground: we plow and set up a few beds, cover them with 2 centimeters of compost, and on each bed, 1 meter 33 centimeters wide, we trace twelve or thirteen rays with our feet. When the rays are made, we go to the nursery bed to pick up the chicory plant, with a little clod, and plant it in the furrows, placing the plants 40 centimetres apart in the rows; we water immediately, and, when the chicory has recovered, we water it because of the heat and dryness. If drought or heat require continued watering, don't wet it with cold water from our wells in bright sunshine, as this would spoil its heart; water it in the morning or evening. Eight days after it has been tied, a second tie is placed at the top, 0 827 to hide the heart, and a few days later it can be removed, trimmed and sent to the market. This chicory is usually ready for sale in the last fortnight of June. SUMMER CHICORY. This species is less sensitive to cold than its predecessors; it grows much larger and its leaves are wider. CULTIVATION. - This species is sown in the first days of April, on a very warm bed covered with 10 centimeters of fairly light potting soil, as it should not be transplanted. When the seed is sown, it is covered with 15 millimeters of potting soil, but not with a frame as before: the plant is left to strengthen in the air, and watered as needed. When the plant is strong enough to be replanted, plough and plant beds as usual; but, instead of covering them with compost, mulch them, for the reasons already explained above, and, as this chicory should become large, trace on each bed, still with the feet, ten rays, instead of twelve or thirteen, and plant this chicory 45 centimetres apart in the rows. It's important to water immediately at this time of year, and to support the plants throughout their growth. You can start binding this chicory at the end of June and take some to the market at the beginning of July. ROUENNAISE CHICORY. This species has only been cultivated by market gardeners for seven or eight years; it is greener, finely cut and less frizzy than the others. CULTIVATION. - This species is sown in May on a fairly warm bed, and as it does not transplant, it must be sown light. At the beginning of June, plough, straighten and mulch boards, on which you trace nine or ten rays with your feet, and plant this chicory 45 centimetres apart in the rows. Water immediately and whenever necessary, until harvest, but don't wait until then to sow more. After the first sowing on the bed in May, sow on the ground every two weeks until August. This chicory is the most common, the oldest cultivated, the largest and the hardiest; it is the one that is kept well into winter, first with a few blankets in the garden, then in the cellar. Gardeners who grow a lot of chicory sow it every ten days, so as not to run out of plants. CULTIVATION. - We sow Meaux chicory from the beginning of June to the end of July, in the ground, in a well-prepared and well-divided area. After the seed has been harrowed, we spread a light mulch of very short or almost consumed manure over it, moisten lightly and repeat the moistening often, if it doesn't rain. When the plant is 8 or 10 centimetres high, we plant it in well-ploughed, upright and mulched beds, on which we trace only nine rays with the feet, instead of ten, because this chicory becomes stronger than the previous one; when it is planted, we water it immediately, and afterwards, all the more often as we are now in summer. We won't go into the rest of the care until sale, since it's the same as for the previous species. WILD CHICORY. This differs from the others in that it is taller, perennial or at least tri-annual, its leaves are lanceolate, do not pomace, are more bitter and of more limited use as food. PORCED GROWING. - In the course of February, we build up a spring layer (it would be pointless to make it stronger); we place boxes on it, cover it with 10 or 12 centimetres of compost and fold the frames; when the compost is warm, we sow the seed in it very hard and cover it with 15 millimetres of compost; after which, we spread straw mats over the frames. When the seed has risen, it can be given a little light, but we would point out that as this chicory must remain tender and blond-green, and as air and light destroy both these qualities, this little chicory should be given little light and no air. Within a month of sowing, the leaves have grown to a length of 8 or 10 centimetres; they are then cut with a knife or scissors, a centimetre above the soil, and taken to the market. Twelve or fifteen days later, the plant has grown a second crop, which is harvested in the same way as the first; the bed is then used for another product. NASTURTIUM CHICORY. Although market gardeners in Paris are no longer involved in growing chicory, since local growers have taken over this branch of the industry, we feel it necessary to summarize here how to obtain the chicory that appears on the capital's markets in winter, and which is eaten as a salad, usually with baked beet, in 08. How to obtain capuchin beard. - At the end of March and beginning of April, wild chicory is sown in the open field, broadcast and a little light. During the summer, the leaves are sold to herbalists. In October, we start to pull up the roots to grow them in cellars, and we continue to do so in November, December and even in July. These roots are brought into the house; all the leaves near the head or crown of the root are broken off; the roots are then gathered into large bunches 33 centimetres in diameter (1 metre in circumference), with all the heads level, and tied with wicker. In the meantime, a layer of horse manure, x5 to 30 centimetres thick, has been prepared in a very dark cellar; no soil or compost is placed on this layer; a first row of these large bunches is placed on the manure, upright against the wall, then a second row against the first, then a third row against the second, and so on and so forth, always in a chessboard pattern, to leave as little space as possible between the bunches: all that's usually left is a small empty path in the middle of the cellar for service purposes. In twenty or twenty-two days, the roots of all these boxes have sprouted narrow leaves, 25 to 30 centimetres long, very white, very clean. O Then we take these large bunches, bring them to the light, divide each of them into six or eight other small bunches, each containing a hundred roots and their leaves, and bind each one with a wicker; it's in this state that we deliver them for consumption, under the name of barbe-decapucin. Even though there is no light and very little air in the cellar, all chicory leaves lean towards the place where there is the most air; this is why all these leaves incline to the same side, an inclination that people who put them in small bunches keep for the purpose of making them more pleasing to the eye. There are several ways of blanching chicory, but this is the most general, the simplest and the safest if you do as much as the growers who supply chicory to the capital's markets from the end of October to the end of January, when head lettuce replaces it. CURLED-LEAF OR ROUND-LEAF ENDIVE. In gardening, this plant is simply called escarole; but we must classify it here with the chicories, since it has all their characteristics. There are a few well-known varieties of escarole, each with its own name, but we only grow this one, because we find it fatter and more plump; escarole salad is less tough than chicory. CULTIVATION. - Scarole is sown for the first time in June, on a small layer of soil made on purpose, no more than 33 centimeters thick, covered with 1 to 12 centimeters of potting soil; the seed is sown a little light, because the plant is not to be transplanted, and is covered with 15 millimeters of potting soil; no cloche or frame is put in; it is sown in the same way until July 10, after which it is sown in the open ground until August 8. The reason why we sow in beds first is that the roots of the plants make more hair in the compost than in the soil, and that hair is favorable to the growth and beauty of the plants; after twenty or twenty-five days of sowing, the plant is ready to be planted, if it has not been neglected during wetting. We prepare beds and cover them with mulch, as we've already said several times; because, in market gardening, it's always the same thing: we plough, we level, we harrow, and we cover with mulch in summer and with compost in winter all the land where we want to plant. Returning to the escarole, when the mulch is well spread out, trace out with your feet, and always with your feet, seven or eight rays per bed, and plant the escarole 66 centimetres apart in the rows; water the planting immediately, and continue to wet it often, as long as it remains on the ground. If the escarole holds up well to wetting, it is good to bind fifty days after planting, and ten days after binding, it is good to deliver for consumption. Seedlings, successively planted every twelve or fifteen days until the end of August, can be planted and treated in the same way; the last plantings can withstand a few degrees of frost, and, with a few precautions, can be kept until the first days of January. HARICOTS. Plants of the legume family, of the order and genus whose name they bear; the species and varieties are numerous: some are large, voluble and need stakes or stiles; others are dwarfed and stand upright without stakes: all have ternate leaves or leaves composed of three leaflets. The flowers, in axillary clusters, are very distinct from all the others in their family, in that they have the carina, stamens and style contoured in a spiral. The fruit is a two-valve pod, long and narrow, containing several seeds, most of them kidney-shaped. 1 Paris market gardeners only grow the suivaute species for green eating. FLAGEOLET BEAN. PORCED CULTIVATION. - Some of us call this bean the Dutch dwarf, the Laon dwarf, and all agree that it is the earliest and most suitable for forcing. In the first eight days of February, we make a spring bed capable of holding a two- or three-panel box, in which we spread a bed of potting soil 11 to 14 centimeters thick; after four or five days, the greatest heat has subsided; then we even out the potting soil, plombe it lightly, sow the bean in it fairly thickly, and cover it with 15 millimeters of potting soil; after this operation, we place the frames and cover them with straw mats. The beans don't take long to sprout; when they're out of the frame, we give them light during the day and cover them at night. Eight or ten days after the beans have been sown, it's time to plant them, as they grow fast and you don't want to wait until they're big enough to plant them. In a well-exposed spot, make one or more trenches, 1 meter 50 centimeters wide and 4o to 48 centimeters deep, in each of which you lay a 66-centimeter thick layer, on top of which you place boxes in which you put 16 centimeters thick of well-divided soil, taken from the trench you've made or from the one you open next to it, and, when it's well spread out in the boxes, you place the panels: When the soil is sufficiently heated, the young beans are planted by hand, up to the cotyledons, at a rate of five rows per panel; two are placed in each hole, 27 millimeters (1 inch) apart, to form a clump, and each clump 18 or 20 centimeters apart in the rows. As soon as one or two panels have been planted, the frames and a straw mat are placed on top, and so on until the planting is complete. When the beans have recovered, we remove the straw mats during the day and put them back on at night, and give them a little air whenever the weather permits; to do this, we use wooden wedges 3 centimetres thick, 8 centimetres wide and 18 centimetres long, and we place them flat, sideways or upright, under the edge of the panels, depending on whether we want to give more or less air to the beans, as well as to all the plants under the frames. When the beans are strong and starting to flower, they need to be visited often, to remove dead or yellowing leaves; even green ones need to be removed, to give the plants air and allow the sun's rays to penetrate them, so that the flower doesn't run and the needles (small fruits) form; When these fruits (small green beans) are 6 to 7 centimetres long, it's time to pick them, and, as they come on quickly, they can be picked every three days. The soil can sometimes dry out, and the fruit would sink if we didn't water it from time to time; one watering can of water per frame is enough. This well-managed plantation produces tender beans for two months, and once the same beds have been turned over, a final season of melon can be planted. RARICOT FLAGEOLET IN THE OPEN GROUND UNDER A FRAME. CULTIVATION. - In the last fortnight of April, make a piece of bed, place a box with two or three panels, and sow the bean as described above; When the bean has fully emerged, we plough one or more beds, each 1.40 meters wide, with good exposure, and when the soil is well harrowed and divided, we cover it with 2 centimeters of potting soil, and place boxes in it, in which we plant the beans as we have said, and cover them with frames on which we spread straw mats during the day, until the plant has recovered; then we give the soil more and more air, and moisten it because of the drought. In this season, the frames are usually removed before the beans flower: the air makes them more robust, and they bear fruit abundantly until the end of July, when market gardeners stop growing beans because so many of them arrive on the market from the countryside that they can no longer compete. If there is a lack of frames for this last bean season, after germinating them on a bed, they can be planted under cloches at 4 feet per cloche, in soil prepared as for frames; after shading them to make them recover, they are given air by lifting the cloches on the north side by means of racks, and you don't wait until the cloches are full before uncloaking, as the leaves that touch the glass would burn. PERSIL. Plant of the Umbelliferae family and of the genus whose name it bears; it is a biennial, tap-rooted plant, about 1 meter high, with decomposed radical leaves: whitish, umbellate flowers, followed by geminate, oval, ridged fruits. The plant's radical leaves are used as a condiment in cooking. Among the different varieties of parsley, the market gardeners of Paris grow only the bloud and the vert; they value the vert more than the blond. FORCED CULTIVATION. - This cultivation method is so simple that it hardly deserves the epithet "forced"; in fact, it is rarely used by Paris market gardeners, no doubt because of our fickle winters. It consists in making a bed in the first days of February, even January, loading it with 16 centimeters of potting soil, a box and a frame, and planting fresh parsley vines, where they quickly produce these leaves. And, as parsley is an indispensable ingredient in the kitchen, when long, harsh winters bring lethargy or death to parsley grown in the open ground, parsley kept in a frame acquires a very high price which greatly rewards its owner, but which, in mild winters, only causes loss. The same applies to sorrel, which we'll talk about in the April crops. PARSLEY ON THE COAST. CULTIVATION. - From the first day of February, sow one or two rays of parsley at the foot of a wall with good exposure; it will only come up at the end of March, and can be cut at the end of April and in May. It is more tender and less strong than old parsley, which comes up at this time. PARSLEY IN THE GROUND. CULTIVATION. - In the first few days of April, plow a board of the usual width, and use your feet to make twelve furrows " centimeters deep, into which the seed is sown; when it is sown, go back between the furrows, and use your feet to drop the soil right and left onto the seed; then rake the whole thing to consolidate the soil, and cover it with a centimeter of compost; Once this is done, nine to ten rows of romaine are planted on a bed, at a distance of 66 centimetres between rows, which are watered immediately and kept wet because of the drought; as the parsley takes a long time to emerge and grows slowly, the romaine is ready and sold before the parsley can be picked. As long as the romaine exists with the parsley, it benefits from the romaine's watering; but when it's on its own, don't forget to water it from time to time. Throughout the summer, parsley can be picked several times, by cutting it with a knife at about 3 centimetres above the ground; but, during the winter, it is picked by hand, peeling the leaves from the stem and leaving the heart intact. In spring, it comes up and is destroyed if the seed is not needed. ASPERGE. 241 A dioecious plant of the Liliaceae family and of the genus whose name it bears; it produces a large perennial rhizome, commonly called a claw or paw, from which grow annual, stalky stems, 1 meter or more high, with very small leaves; the flowers are small, whitish; they are followed (on female plants) by small red, pisiform fruits, which contain the seeds. The edible parts of the asparagus plant are its young stalks, when they are only 6 to 12 centimetres above ground. OBSERVATION. - As the maralchers of Paris only grow asparagus in forced cultivation, their way of planting it should appear different from that generally followed in vegetable gardens. CULTIVATION. -In the course of March, plow a bed of good, loose soil that is neither in the shade nor in too hot a spot, and broadcast the seed of asparagus called Hollande, Marchiennes, or the most beautiful species possible; harrow it to bury it, and spread 3 centimeters of potting soil over the bed. While the young plants are growing, 16 they are weeded as needed, and if the summer is dry, a few good wettings will be very useful. After a year's sowing, the plant is ready for planting; then, in March, we make a pit 1.35 meters wide and lo centimeters deep, dumping the soil on one side; we put 20 centimeters of well-pressed cow manure in the bottom of the pit and cover it with 8 centimeters of the best soil from the pit, which we compact well with our feet, then rake it in. Once this operation has been completed, we pull up the asparagus sown a year earlier; the roots of the asparagus are intertwined with each other; we separate them and place four rows on the earth in the pit, which is 1 metre 33 centimetres wide and of any length; but the two rows on either side must be 16 centimetres from the edge, because as the asparagus grows older, it would spread out into the path, which would be a disadvantage when it came time to heat it. Leaving 16 centimetres (6 inches) on each side leaves only 1 metre (3 feet) to plant four rows of asparagus, which puts the rows 24 centimetres (9 inches) apart, and, to increase the distance from one foot to the other, they are placed in a chessboard pattern, leaving a space of 4o centimetres (15 inches) between each foot in the rows, and the whole is covered with 8 to 10 centimetres of soil. Once the distance between rows and between plants has been calculated, we proceed with the planting; we take a claw, place it in the indicated position on the prepared soil, spreading the roots well around the circumference, and fix it in this position by putting a handful of soil on top. When the whole pit has been planted, we fill it with the soil we've taken from it, holding it a little higher, because the manure at the bottom has to go down as it rots, and the pits must then be level with the paths (1): then there will be about 8 or r0 centimetres of soil on the claws. After this first pit is planted and filled in, we open a similar one next to it, 66 centimetres apart, plant it in the same way, and so on, until the square is planted, always leaving a path 66 centimetres (2 feet) wide between each pit. (1) There are several other ways to plant asparagus, especially when you don't want to force it and want it to produce for twenty years or so: we plant the asparagus on small cones of earth so that its roots are more or less perpendicular, which is the direction they naturally take afterwards; we only fill in the pits little by little over three years; but we only ask the asparagus to exist for a dozen years, and plant it younger and closer than those who have a lot of room to give it. When the whole square is planted in this way, we let the asparagus grow and strengthen for two campaigns, taking care only not to let any weeds come into it, to keep the square clean and to give it a very small ploughing, rather with a pitchfork than a spade, in March, the second year. If the plant was good, if the planting was well done, in the autumn of the second year, the size of the asparagus stalks will indicate that they are strong enough to be forced in November (1). How to force white asparagus - In the first fortnight of November, dig the paths of the asparagus patch 66 centimetres wide and as deep; put 18 to 22 centimetres of this well-divided soil on the asparagus beds, to make them elongate, and deposit the rest at the end of the paths. When these paths are empty, we fill them with good new horse manure, arranged and well packed as when making a layer; we place the chests on the asparagus boards; we spread well the earth we have deposited on the boards; we put again new manure in the paths up to the upper edge of the chests; similar ditches are dug at the (1) Many people let asparagus grow freely for three years and don't start cutting them until the fourth year of planting; but we can't wait that long: we cut them off at their third shoot. They are then placed at the end of planks, which are also filled with manure. Many market gardeners also fill the trunks with new manure before placing the trays, in order to warm up the top of the soil at the same time as the manure from the paths warms up the sides: finally, once the trays have been placed, they are covered with dry litter or straw mats, so that the heat that will be released into the soil does not evaporate in vain; manure is even put back into the paths to raise it higher than the top edge of the trunks. Fifteen or twenty days later, the asparagus begin to emerge from the soil; those market gardeners who have filled the trunks with manure remove it to leave the soil bare and covered only with frames, so as to see the asparagus grow; but every night, the frames must be covered with single or double mats, depending on the degree of cold that may occur. Cut the asparagus when it is about 8 centimetres (5 inches) long, outside the soil: you dig with your hand, removing the soil at the base of the asparagus and taking care not to break or injure those which have not yet emerged from the soil; by digging and removing the soil several times, you end up freeing the asparagus to a length of about 20 centimetres; You then grasp it completely at the bottom with your hand, pull hard and twist very lightly, and it falls away from the claw or rhizome several centimetres below the point you're holding; so that a good asparagus, just picked, should be about 24 centimetres long. Asparagus forced in this way is called white asparagus, because it is much whiter than asparagus grown in the open air in spring. White asparagus can be harvested every two days for two months, after which time harvesting must cease to avoid exhausting and weakening the plant. So, if you want to have white asparagus all winter long, you'll have to divide a square of asparagus into three batches and heat them up successively, five or six weeks apart. Remember that an asparagus plant that has been forced over winter must not be picked again in spring, otherwise it will be exhausted very quickly. We've just said that white asparagus can be picked for two months, but only on condition that the heat is maintained in the stoves or in the manure surrounding the asparagus beds. Practice teaches you to judge when this manure has lost its heat; you'll notice when the asparagus is no longer growing as fast as you'd like, when it's not as fresh as you'd like, and when there are a few rust spots: then you need to hurry up and reshuffle the manure in the paths, mixing in half new manure or even more, and it may be necessary to repeat this operation two or three times during the two months it takes to pick the asparagus. Once harvesting is complete, the boxes and frames are removed from the asparagus beds; the manure is also removed from the paths, and the soil from them is put back in, some of which had been deposited on the beds to increase the length of the asparagus, and some of which had been deposited at the end of the paths. When the soil that had been deposited on the planks is brought down into the paths, we find asparagus that is more or less advanced and not yet above ground: these asparagus are not suitable for sale, but the gardener puts them to good use in her kitchen. Asparagus is sold by the bunch, and a bunch is about 4o centimeters in circumference. To produce beautiful asparagus and avoid overtaxing the claws, asparagus should be heated only once in two years; however, some market gardeners heat them two years in a row and leave them to rest only in the third year. Forcing green asparagus or asparagus with peas. - It's not the market gardeners of Paris who sow and raise this asparagus; it's the growers in the outskirts of the capital who raise it in open fields, in Les Vertus, Saint-Denis, Saint-Quen and elsewhere, in huge quantities: After a year's sowing, they plant it and cultivate it for three years, without picking it; then they pick it every O year, according to custom, and it's when these asparagus are in their first years, or have been planted for one to six years, or are at their most productive, that the market gardeners who force asparagus buy it. Asparagus that has only been planted for a year or two sells for 8 to 900 fr. per half-hectare, while asparagus that has been planted for five or six years sells for only 3 to 4 and 500 fr. per half-hectare; and, as the market gardener is very interested in ensuring that the asparagus is well grown, it is usually he and his men who will pull it out. Green asparagus can be forced from October onwards, until the asparagus in the field is ready. Here's how: You make layers 1 meter 60 centimeters (5 feet) wide, 54 to 64 centimeters (20 or 24 inches) thick, leaning one against the other, with two-thirds of good inn manure, well soaked with urine, and one-third of ordinary manure, well mixed together, and you place on top of them trunks 4o to 50 centimeters high at the back, and 32 to. 35 centimetres in front. When the shot has been fired, asparagus claws are taken and placed upright on their roots, on the manure layer, pressing the claws and their roots against each other, so that all the claws are at the same height and 450 to 500 are held under each panel. Some growers slip a little potting soil between each claw; others place nothing there; finally, as this kind of planting is carried out, the frames are placed on top, and they are covered with straw mats because of the cold. The warm humidity of the manure soon rises in the roots and between the asparagus claws, forming a hot atmosphere under the frames, penetrating all the claws and putting them into vegetation; then the claw buds, which were only supposed to lengthen in April or May, stimulated by the heat and humidity, lengthen successively and rapidly; but their sprays are. much thinner than if the claws had not been pulled out of the ground. Although the green asparagus forcing boxes are higher than the others, when the asparagus starts to grow, straw plugs must be placed under the boxes, so that the glass of the frames is 33 centimetres from the top of the claws, as many asparagus plants are 28 to 32 centimetres long when picked; but at the same time as the boxes are being raised, the paths must be filled with manure up to the top of the boxes, both to prevent outside air from penetrating to the asparagus and to keep it warm : even if, during harvesting, you realize that the layer is no longer providing enough heat, you should reshuffle and mix new manure with the old in the paths, once or twice. Fifteen days after the claws have been placed 08 on layers and under panels, we start picking asparagus, if the heat has been well maintained, and they can produce for six weeks, which is about the time it takes for all the eyes of the claws to be exhausted, and, during this time, we can pick green asparagus every two or three days; but the last pickings are less abundant and the asparagus is smaller than the first. On average, one claw can produce ten green asparagus plants, and as a panel can contain from four hundred and fifty to five hundred claws, it follows that from four thousand five hundred to five thousand green asparagus plants can also be harvested under one panel. This asparagus is sold in 52-centimeter bundles, under the names of green asparagus or pea asparagus, because it is most often eaten broken into small pieces that look rather like green peas: the claws that produced it are rejected by Paris market gardeners, because they don't have the time or the need to use them in potting soil. The cultivation of green asparagus, like that of white asparagus, is very dependent; only a very small number of Paris market gardeners grow it, and in some years they barely make a profit. EGGPLANT OR MELONGENE. A plant of the solanaceae family and the selanum genus, native to the Chands. It is apnual, growing on a stalk about 45 centimeters high, twiggy, lined with large, oval, tomentose fogils; its flowers are bluish and their calyx is grooved; these flowers are followed by large, pendulous, fleshy, oblong, purple or yellowish fruits, which are the only edible part of the plant. CULTIVATION. - As early as the first days of March, or even as early as the end of February, we set up a mother bed on a board, and place a small box on top, in which we spread 8 centimetres of compost, which we cover with a frame on which we unroll one or two doormats to speed up fermentation. When the shot is fired, the eggplant seed is sown on the potting soil and covered with 1 centimeter of it. The eggplant seed, sown in this way, sprouts in four days. While waiting for the plant to strengthen, we prepare a nursery bed with two or three panels, and, eight or ten days later, we transplant the plant, so that it holds one hundred and fifty under each panel. If the sun shines before the plant is taken up, it is shaded and covered at night with straw mats, and a bundle is made around the bed to keep it warm. As eggplants grow quite slowly, a single seedling is sufficient for two plantings, some time apart. So, if you have empty beds at the beginning of April, plough the soil and plant eggplants at four feet per panel, if the bed is & frame; or, if the bed is with bells, plant two rows and the feet at 66 centimetres in the rows. In both cases, the plant is watered right away to secure it to the soil: in the first case, the frames must be placed right away and shaded during recovery; in the second case, a bell must be placed over each plant and shaded for the same reason, and the cold must be kept out by the known means. Eggplant requires frequent watering during growth. After May 15, when frosts are no longer to be feared, the frames and cloches are removed, and if the plant has not lacked the necessary wetting, it will be able to produce fruit fit for consumption at the end of June and during July. Eggplant grown in this way on a bed produces more beautiful fruit than that grown in the ground, provided that it does not lack the necessary wetting. The second way to grow eggplants is to wait until April 15 or 20 to plant them in the ground, in a coastal or sheltered spot, and to cover each plant with a cloche, which is lifted at the right moment, with a cré -1 maillère, to give it air, and which is not removed until the end of May: then a small basin is formed around the base of each plant, mulch is spread over it and it is wetted often and abundantly. Eggplant cultivation is very limited in Paris, and few market gardeners grow them, because the production is small and unreliable. TOMATO OR POMME D'AMOUR. A plant of the solanaceae family and solanum genus, native to South America. It is annual, twiggy, diffuse, grows to a height of over 1 meter by means of a support, has winged, incised leaves, and has small, dirty-white flowers arranged in simple clusters, which are followed by red, rounded, twisted, succulent fruits, which are the only edible part of this plant. There are several varieties of tomato, but we only grow the one with the red, twisted fruit. CULTIVATION. - Tomato seed is sown at the end of March; it is rarely necessary to make a mother bed for this, as at this time there is no shortage of widowed or reworked beds that have enough warmth, and there is always enough room on one to sow two or three bells of seed, a quantity more than sufficient for the strongest market gardener. When the seed has emerged and the plants are 5 or 6 centimeters high, they are transplanted onto another warm layer under a bell or under a frame; twelve feet can be planted under a bell, and two hundred under a frame panel; they are watered and shaded immediately; two or three days later, they are given light and air, and successively increased until the first of May: if the weather is then mild, you can remove the cloches and frames, but don't keep them too far away, so that they can be replaced at the slightest sign of frost, as tomatoes are very sensitive to it. After May 15th, there is usually no more frost to worry about, so it's time to plant them indoors and in the open air. Market gardeners who sow tomato seeds at the beginning of March plant them on an old bed, in two rows, a foot apart. Market gardeners who sow at the end of March plant in full squares or on the sides, placing the plants at least 66 centimetres (a feet) apart; if planting in full squares, four rows can be planted in a 2-metre 33-centimetre-wide bed, with the plants chessboarded in the rows at a metre apart. As for the planting itself, it doesn't require any special care; the plant is wetted as soon as it's in the ground, and it protects itself from the sun. Tomato stems are weak, do not naturally grow upwards of their own accord, and branch out at the base; so, when they are 40 centimetres (15 inches) long, choose two or three of the most vigorous branches, and remove all the small shoots below them and along their length; then screw a good 1-metre-high stake to each plant, and tie the prepared to mate stems to it with a strand of straw: these stems continue to grow, and are given a second, third tie, until they are as high as the stake, or about 1 metre; then they are stopped by cutting off their heads: the lateral branches strengthen, grow; but they are also stopped at a certain length, and the flowers show. As long as the tomato has no fruit, it should be watered only moderately, so that it doesn't grow too woody and is better disposed for flowering; but when the fruit is set, it should be watered abundantly to make it swell, and anything growing above the stakes should be removed. Tomatoes planted in beds produce ripe fruit in the first fortnight of June, but those planted in the ground only start to bear fruit at the end of June, and continue to do so in July, August and even until the frosts, if you wish. Tomatco are picked when they are fully red, the first ones being sold by the dozen and the others by the calaie. OBSERVATIONS. - We have just described how maral growers cultivate tomatoes, but we know that some people tie them to a trellis, trellis them against a wall, against a windbreak, and that others let them come and go as they please; this is because the tomato is a very fertile plant, and no matter how you cultivate it, you always get a lot of fruit. It would not be out of place for us to note here that the tomato grafts perfectly onto the stems of the potato, producing a double harvest in the same space of time and land; for, when the fruits of the tomato are harvested, another crop of potato is made in the ground. PERCE-PIERRE. Plant of the Umbelliferae family, of the Seselina section and of the genus whose name it bears. It is a native, perennial plant with a herbaceous, twiggy stem, about 30 centimeters high, fleshy as are its leaves, which are twice winged, with linear leaflets: the flowers are small, whitish, terminal, and are followed by small, ovoid, ridged fruits; the leaves and young stems are used in salad supplies and with pickles preserved in vinegar. SEA STONE BORER. It can be sown in March at the foot of a wall, facing east or west; its roots will penetrate the wall, and it will sprout stems, leaves and flowers every year. It can also be sown in a border like tarragon, and it will live equally long; the leaves and young stems are picked in the same way. PEPPER. A plant of the solana family and the genus whose name it bears. It originated in India, and was introduced into Europe around two hundred years ago. There are several species and varieties of chili pepper, but only a very small number of market gardeners cultivate the large-fruited or coral chili pepper, which is an annual, twining plant, ho to 50 centimeters high, with numerous oblong leaves and small, greenish-white flowers, followed by 8-centimeter-long, very red fruits, used as a condiment in cooking. CORAL PEPPER. In March, sow a fairly light pinch of this seed on a piece of warm soil, and when the plant is 8 to 18 centimeters high, plant it in a clod on the edge of another layer ho or 50 centimeters apart: 17 it could be planted in the ground, but it wouldn't grow as quickly or as well as it does in a layer of potting soil; it requires a lot of water, and its fruit blushes and ripens successively from August until the frosts. BONNE-DAME. Plant of the Chenopoda family and of the Arroche genus. It is an annual plant, native to Asia, from 1 to a meters high, not very stalked, with insignificant flowers, followed by small fruits enclosed in the calyx. The edible parts of this plant are its young, triangular, fairly large leaves. GARDEN LADY'S SLIPPER. This plant is of little importance in market gardening. At the end of March or in the first few days of April, a few seeds are thrown into the beds of other seedlings and plantations, and it comes up unattended: when it is 12 to 15 centimetres high, it is pulled up, the bottom of the stem is cut off, it is put into bunches and sent to the market; or, the plant is left to grow, the leaves are picked one by one and sold in mannettes. There is a variety with red stem and leaves that is just as good and requires no more care; when both are introduced into a garden and left to grow, they reproduce by themselves for several years in a row. APRIL. PIMPRENELLE. Plant of the Rosaceae family, tribe Sanguisorbia and genus Poterium. It is a small, native perennial with compound, radical leaves, and a stem only 5 decimeters high; divided at the apex, each division terminating in a ball of monoecious flowers, succeeded by small tetragonal seeds. The edible parts of this plant are its leaves, which are used to garnish salads. CULTIVATION. - This plant is cultivated in very small quantities by Paris market gardeners, because its consumption is very minimal: it is sown in beds or borders in April; when it is covered with a few millimeters of soil, a light mulch is put on top, and watered as needed. When the leaves are 12 to 15 centimetres high, they are cut close to the ground and sold in small bunches. As soon as the leaves are cut, give the plants a good wetting to encourage them to regrow new ones quickly, and water them from time to time if the season is dry. Burnet is not difficult to grow in the field, but to keep its leaves a fresh, appetizing green, it must be sown in shade or half-shade. ESTRAGON. Plant of the Compositae family, of the Artemisia section and of the Artemisia genus. It is a perennial, native to Tartary, with tracing roots, linear, lanceolate leaves, and stems rising to a height of about 4o centimeters; the flowers are very-small and insignificant. The young stems and tender leaves are the only edible parts: they are used to garnish salads. CULTIVATION. - Tarragon is sown as a broadcast crop on a piece of ground in April, and when the young stems are 16 to 20 centimeters high and well covered with leaves, they are cut close to the ground, gathered into small bunches and carried to the market. After this first cut, the stalks are pulled up and replanted in rows: ten rows can be planted in a bed 2 meters 33 centimeters wide, with the stalks 27 centimeters apart in the rows. Elsewhere, tarragon is most often planted in borders; but Paris market gardeners can't plant anything in borders. Tarragon is better planted in part-shade than in full sun: in summer, it can be picked every two weeks, and can remain in place for three years; but, as it leaves a lot of trace and always tends to grow out of the rows, it is pulled out after three years and replanted elsewhere. This plant ranks very low in market gardening. ALENOIS CRESSON. g Plant of the Cruciferae family, section Lepidinae and genus Thlaspi. The plant is an annual of unknown origin, with small, sharply-cut leaves and a stem 2 to 3 decimeters high, bearing clusters of small white flowers succeeded by small orbicular siliques indented at the top. The edible parts of this small plant are the lower leaves, which are used in salads. CULTIVATION. - We distinguish and cultivate in our marshes, albeit in very small quantities, the simple cress alénois and the double cress, i.e. the one without curled leaves and the one with curled leaves: The seed is sown in the open ground in the last days of April or the first days of May, in rows rather than broadcast, to make it easier to pick, and is covered with a light layer of potting soil; this seed rises very quickly, and as the plant grows excessively fast, frost can reach it, so a few precautions are taken to protect it in this first season. When the leaves are 8 or 10 centimetres high, they are cut off for sale: the plant grows new ones, which can be picked a second time, but that's all. The plant goes to seed, so it's left to grow if it's needed, otherwise it's destroyed. This little plant grows quickly and for a short time, so you need to sow it every ten or twelve days during the summer. Before watercress appeared on the Paris markets, for twelve months of the year we sowed watercress from the beginning of March, as an early crop; but now there would be no profit in doing so. SORREL. A plant of the polygon family and of the geurea whose name it bears. There are many species of this genus, all perennial and more or less acidic; those cultivated have oblong and oval radical leaves, more or less sagittate at the base, succulent, ridged stems, about 70 cm high, paniculate, with small greenish flowers, monoecious or dioecious, succeeded by triangular, shiny seeds. Leaves and very young stems are the only edible parts of sorrel. COMMON SORREL. CULTIVATION. - Sorrel seed is sown from the beginning of April to August, in plots a meter 33 centimeters wide; these plots are ploughed and levelled as usual, and ten flat-bottomed rays are traced in each of them with the feet, and the seed is spread across the whole width of the ray, taking care not to sow it too thickly; We then slide our feet between each spoke, so that the soil on either side falls into the sown spokes, and the seed is covered; once this operation is complete, we rake the surface to level it, and cover the whole with a 12-mm-thick bed of compost. We always plant six or seven rows of romaine, which benefits from the watering we're obliged to give to romaine, which comes and sells before harming sorrel. The first harvest of sorrel is done with a knife 2 centimetres above the ground; but the second and subsequent harvests are done by hand, removing the leaves, one after the other, with their petioles close to the stump, sparing the small heart leaves which, a fortnight later, can be harvested in their turn. Although sorrel has a taproot that makes it fairly drought-resistant, it's a good idea to give it plenty of water during the summer, especially after harvesting, which can take place every fortnight until autumn. As it ages, sorrel can live for a very long time, but we usually only keep it for one year. VIRGIN SORREL. CULTIVATION. - This sorrel is dioecious, i.e. it bears male flowers on some plants and female flowers on others.
