For a long time now, only male plants have been grown in gardens, so they do not produce seeds, hence the name "virgin sorrel". Its leaves are broader, rounder, blonder green, more appetizing and less acidic than those of common sorrel. However, in our marshes, we don't plant anything in borders, we plant everything in beds: so, when we have an old plant of virgin sorrel, we pull it out, divide the roots, choose the youngest parts, best furnished with eyes, and replant them in beds, 16 centimetres apart. This planting is more successful in October than in spring, and we only repeat it every four or five years. CLOVER. FORCED CULTIVATION. - As early as November, on the assumption that the ground will soon freeze and that we won't be able to do it later, we pull up a supply of old sorrel stumps, taking good care of the roots, and put them in a place where they can be easily sheltered with bedding, should the frost set in; immediately or later, according to need or speculation, we make a good layer on which we place chests, and we put in these chests a thickness of at least 16 centimeters of compost. When the layer has given off its great fire, we take clumps of sorrel put in gauge, divide them if they are too big, and plant them in gutter or otherwise in the potting soil of the layer so that all the heads are at the same height and covered with a few millimeters of potting soil; then we put the frames. Soon the leaves start to grow, and three pickings can be made at some distance from each other, after which the plants are exhausted. Throughout the winter, not only sorrel, but also old parsley, chervil and other plants that have become numb due to the harshness of the season, can be warmed and grown in this way. POTIRON. Plant of the Cucurbitaceae family and of the squash genus. There are several species or varieties of pumpkin; all are annual, creeping plants, with large leaves and tendrils; the flowers are monoecious, large, and are followed by very large, fleshy fruits, often rounded, oval, elongated, fluted, yellow, green or gray, which are the edible part of these plants. LARGE PUMPKIN. GROWING. - In the first week of April, sow the seed of this pumpkin on a piece of warm bedding; it soon sprouts, and eight days later, transplant it to another bed in a nursery; if cloches or frames are available, cover the young plant to help it take root, but this is not essential; just protect it from frost at night. By the first week of May, it's ready to be planted, so we make holes 1 metre square and 55 centimetres deep, in each of which we place three hottées of well-packed manure, which we cover with the soil from the hole. There's no set rule for the distance from one hole to the next, but you can expect a pumpkin's stem to extend 4 to 5 meters in the direction you want it to, and that's enough to place the holes properly: Once you've understood this, form a sort of basin in the middle of each hole, go to the nursery bed, use both hands to lift a pumpkin plant with a good root ball, and plant it in the middle of the basin, pushing it in as far as the cotyledons; water it immediately, and if the sun is shining or it's windy, protect it from both with a little dry litter for three or four days, to help it recover. You don't cut off the pumpkin's head, as you do with melons and cucumbers; you let it run on the ground in the direction you want it to go, but you must water it often and abundantly. As the stem lengthens, it sprouts offshoots or small branches on the sides: Our custom is to remove them so that all the sap passes into the main stem; we are also in the habit, when this main stem is a or 5 meters long, of marcotting it, in order to multiply the roots. This marcotting consists of opening a small oblong pit, 16 centimeters deep, into which we lower the part of the stem where we want the roots to develop; If necessary, a small wooden hook is used to hold it in place; the soil is then replaced on the portion of stem in the pit, and the plant is watered. A second marcotting is performed 65 centimetres further on, or even a third, as the stem lengthens. When a fruit is knotted and continues to grow, we judge that it will hold, and when it has reached the size of a child's head, we cut off the head of the branch two or three leaves above the fruit, and, if watering is not lacking, we see the fruit grow rapidly. By cultivating in this way, and allowing only one fruit to appear on each plant, we obtain pumpkins weighing 100 kilograms or more. However, it must be said that not all gardeners proceed in this way: some let two fruits come from the same plant, keeping two branches; others don't use layering; still others don't remove any branches, and obtain more fruits, but smaller ones. Pumpkins can be stored up to January and February, in a dry, frost-free place. The large pumpkin mentioned above has pale flesh and does not usually embroider. We also grow 1° the red-fleshed pumpkin, which becomes smaller, embroiders a lot and is better; 2° the green or Spanish pumpkin, which is smaller, very flat and of excellent quality: we don't grow it very often, because most consumers are unaware of its excellent qualities; we can grow two or three on the same plant; 3° the Turkish bonnet or turban pumpkin, much smaller, remarkable for its turban shape: It is very firm and highly prized; three or four are left on the same plant; 4° And finally, the small Jerusalem artichoke pumpkin: the plant does not run like the previous ones, and its fruit, even smaller than the turban, is variously gadrooned and highly prized; up to six or eight are left on the same plant. In our marshes, we don't cultivate the n°s 3, 4 and 5 very much, not because we don't think they're very good, but because they're not eaten in sufficient quantities: besides, the cultivation we've indicated for the large pumpkin is more than adequate for them; it can even be simplified. SCORSONER. Plant of the Compositae family, of the order and genus whose name it bears. It is biennial or trisannual, native to Spain; its root is simple, black on the outside, white on the inside, swiveling and very long; the leaves are lanceolate, acute; the stem grows to 1 meter high, twining at the top, and each branching ends in a yellow, compound flower, to which long, sharp seeds follow. The edible part of this plant is the root; the leaves can also be eaten. SPANISH SCORSONERA. CULTIVATION. - The seed of this plant is sown at the end of April or in the first days of May, in market gardening; but elsewhere, sowing can be delayed until August, for the reason we'll explain shortly. When you're ready to sow, you plow one or more beds deeply; once they're upright, you broadcast them and bury the seed, raking it in with a fork; then you press it down with your feet, pass the rake over it and spread 12 or 15 millimeters of potting soil over it. When the beds have been sown in this way, the feet are used to trace nine or ten spokes, which are planted in Roman style at a distance of 40 centimetres in the rows. Watering the romaine helps accelerate germination and development of the scorsonera. After the romaine has been removed, the scorsonera is weeded out, and grows quite rapidly, thanks to its long roots, which can be pulled up and sold in bunches during the winter. With the second type of sowing, broadcast or ray sowing, scorsonera occupies the land for eighteen or twenty months, which explains why Paris market gardeners can no longer grow a plant that occupies the land for so long. Sown in August, scorsonera goes to seed the following summer: when the seed is collected, the stems are cut, the plant sprouts new leaves, the root grows, becomes fleshy and succulent again, and is ready for consumption the following winter. Now, as this scorsonera is obtained at a lower cost in the surrounding countryside than we could obtain in our marshes, and as it sells at the same time as that which we could have sown in the spring, competition has forced almost all market gardeners in the interior of Paris to give up cultivating scorsonera. OBSERVATION. - As long as the roots of these plants are young, and until they go to seed, they are soft, fleshy and break very easily. As they go to seed, they become hard, tough, stringy and no longer break. When the seed is harvested and the stems cut, the roots sprout new leaves, and these same roots become soft, fleshy, brittle and succulent again, just as they were before they went to seed. Now we ask the scientists what has become of those thick fibers that used to extend throughout the root, making it tough and unbreakable during flowering and fruiting. PANAIS. Plant of the Umbelliferae family, of the order Populaceae and of the genus whose name it bears. It is a native plant, with a simple, taproot, fusiform, 1-meter-high stem, long, winged leaves with broad, lobed or incised leaflets; the flowers are yellow, small, arranged in umbels, followed by elliptical, compressed fruits. The edible part of this plant is the root. Paris market gardeners produce very little parsnip, because its use is very limited in Parisian cuisine; it is only used to flavour soup. CULTIVATION. - Parsnips are sown at the end of April, like many other plants, when there is no longer a heavy frost to fear; but they can be sown earlier in coastal areas, by planting romaine as early as the end of February. When sown in full squares in April, and in previously ploughed and trained beds, it should be sown fairly lightly, as its roots become large and its leaves large. Once the seed has been buried with a fork and plumbed in with the feet, you can sow radishes or spinach on top, plants that don't occupy the soil for long, or plant romaine, with eight or nine rows per bed. As soon as the parsnip is as big and tall as a turnip, you can start taking it to the market and continue until March, when all the roots start to set seed and harden. POURPIER. 273 Native plant of the family and genus whose name it bears. There are several varieties of purslane, all edible; but only the one called golden purslane is cultivated, which is an annual, low-growing, stalked, spreading plant, tender, fleshy, with wedge-shaped leaves; the flowers are soap-box-shaped and shed black seeds. The edible parts of purslane are the leaves and young shoots. GOLDEN PURSLANE CULTURE. - This cultivation is not very widespread and not very lucrative, because purslane is used only to garnish salads. Once it has been introduced into a plot, it perpetuates itself by sowing, and it is said that it is more beautiful when it sows itself than when it is sown by the gardener's hand; nevertheless, a little is sown in some marshes from the first days of May to the first days of August. After ploughing a piece of land, sow the seed more or less thickly, bury it with an 18 pitchfork, press it down with your feet, rake it over and cover it with a few millimetres of compost; if the season is dry, water immediately to encourage germination, and keep the young plant moist to hasten its growth. It's not that purslane fears heat and drought; but watering keeps it tender, and that's a quality. If sown thickly, all the purslane's branches are forced to rise straight up, and when they are 16 to 18 centimetres high, they are cut at a centimetre above the ground and sold by the bunch: In this way, the plant doesn't grow again, or grows so little that it's not worth keeping; but if you've sown lightly, the branches spread out and become stronger: then you pick them one after the other by hand, and you can make two or three successive pickings. PEAR. Plant of the Chenopoda family and the beet genus. It is a biennial, indigenous plant, with a taproot, broad, oval, petiolate leaves, and a thick, fluted, stalk, 1 meter or more high; numerous, clustered, very small, insignificant flowers, followed by small, kidney-shaped seeds. There are two cultivated varieties of pear, and their edible parts are the radial leaves, under the names pear and chard. CULTIVATION. - In the first days of May, at the latest, plough one or more plots of the usual width; trace, on each plot, with your feet, nine wide rays, the bottom of which is flat; sow the pear seed in the whole length of the ray, but clear enough so that the plant is not too tight; then pass your feet again, sliding, between each ray, to make the earth fall on the seed, pass the rake, finally spread on the plot 1 centimeter of compost or a very fine mulch. If the season is dry, wet as often as necessary. When the pear is 20 to 24 centimetres high, cut it off with a knife, this first time, at a or 3 centimetres from the soil, and sell it in small bunches. It grows again, but this time and the next, it is no longer cut, but the large leaves are picked one after the other by hand, and the central ones are kept for the next harvest, and so on. When you intend to grow pears during the winter, instead of sowing in beds 2 meters 35 centimeters wide, you sow in beds only 1 meter 66 centimeters wide, and in November, you place ooffers on them and cover them with frames; when the frosts arrive, we surround the boxes with a good O8 accot, cover the frames with straw mats, and, by this means, we have pear all winter and until May, when the plant goes to seed: even if you were more interested in growing pear in winter than in summer, it would be enough to sow the seeds in July. CARDE POIRÉE. This differs from the previous one in that the main rib of its leaves is wide and thick, and it is this part that is eaten cooked and seasoned like cardoons. GROWING. - We only sow it in June, because we expect the best product in April and May of the following year. So, in June, we sow cardoon pear in a piece of bed, and when the plant is 8 or 10 centimeters high, we water it, and an hour later, we carefully lift it and counter-plant it in one or more half-planted beds of romaine, or, better still, in a newly ploughed bed, where we put in eight rows, with the feet 56 centimeters apart in the rows, and water copiously during the dry weather. When the frosts come, we cover with large bedding, uncover during the mild weather, and, when there are no more frosts to fear, remove the bedding and clean the plants of damaged straw and leaves. At the end of April and in the first fortnight of May, the pear-shaped cardoon has regained its vegetation; its leaves have grown in size and appearance; it's then that we pull out the stalks, trim them, put three or four in a bundle and send them to the market. CARDON. Plant of the Compositae family, Carduaceae section and artichoke genus. A few varieties of cardoon are recognizable: they are biennial plants from the south of France, with a simple, taproot, very long, whitish, pinnatifid, often thorny root leaves; the stem grows to over 1 meter high, twining, and each shoot ends with a head of blue flowers to which fairly large, oblong seeds follow. The edible parts of the cardoon are the leaf stalks and ribs. CARDOONS DE TOURS. CULTIVATION. - In the past, only the Spanish cardoon was grown in our marshes, as it is not very thorny and has the disadvantage of hollow ribs. Today, and for a long time now, we only grow cardoon from Tours, which is thornier, but has the advantage of full ribs, making it far superior to the other. As cardoon is a very vigorous plant, it should be sown in the best and most substantial soil in the garden, and given frequent and abundant watering. If we sow it in April and neglect to wet it, many plants go to seed during the summer; this is why we wait until the first days of May to sow it, and sowing can be done in two ways, i.e. in the field or in a nursery. We sow it in place, because cardoon, with its simple, taproot and no hair, is difficult to pick up and may die several times during planting. Those who do not fear this difficulty sow it in a nursery and then plant it permanently. In a ploughed bed, 2 meters 33 centimeters wide, make two furrows 1 meter 33 centimeters apart; mark on a radius, every meter (3 feet), the place for a foot of cardoon; if the soil is sufficiently divided, soft and good, make a small basin in the places indicated, in which you place two or three cardoon seeds, which you bury 5 centimeters deep. If you want to do even better, remove a spade-head of soil from the marked places, and replace it with a layer of potting soil, in which you place two or three seeds, as before. When a row. O8 is sown, we sow the second, a third, etc., taking care that the whole sowing is in the same row.

chessboard. If the season is dry and hot, the seedling is held in water and sprouts in a few days: soon you can judge which will be the most beautiful plant in each sown spot, and you remove all the others. Those who, for whatever reason, have preferred to sow in a nursery, on a piece of bed or in the ground, wait until the seedling has 12 to 15 centimetres (4 to 5 inches) long leaves; lift it carefully, as its root is already very long, and plant it at the distance already indicated for sowing in place: when planting it, take care of its long root, insert it perpendicularly into the prepared trunk, lead it, water immediately and shade the plant until its recovery is assured. It's hardly until August that the cardoon begins to take on a rapid and vigorous growth, and, until then, the land wouldn't seem sufficiently occupied by cardoons 1 meter apart: so we spread a good mulch over the whole square and plant lettuce or romaine. We repeat, it's only through copious and abundant watering that we obtain beautiful cardoons in the Paris marshes; as they grow, we increase the dose, and, if the weather is dry and hot, we give a watering can of water to each plant every two days. In September, there are already cardoons strong enough to be blanched, and they are successively blanched until November. So, when we want to blanch cardoons from Tours, here's how we go about it: as we can hardly touch them because of their thorns, we bring their leaves together with a rope, and bind them at the bottom with a straw tie, then with another tie in the middle, then finally with a third tie towards the top; when the stalk is thus bound, we wrap it with large litter so as to deprive it of air and light, except for the tops of the longest leaves, and we tighten this litter with three ties like the cardoon itself. Market gardeners call this operation "swaddling" the cardoon; if the weather continues to be dry, we water the base of the cardoon, albeit swaddled, but only once. The cardoon needs three weeks of air and light deprivation for it to acquire the right whiteness and tenderness; then it is cut by the root between two plots of land, de-swaddled, its outer leaves and any defects removed, its collar trimmed, and delivered for consumption. If, in November, you want to preserve cardoons for the winter, bind them as described above. If, in November, you want to preserve cardoons for the winter, you bind them as described above, but do not swaddle them; you pull them out with part of their roots, and a root ball if possible, you take them to a cellar where you have deposited potting soil for this purpose, and you replant them in this potting soil almost without them touching each other; seven or eight days later, you visit them, to remove the outer leaves which usually rot first, and in fifteen or eighteen days they turn white : by continuing to visit them often to remove the spoiling leaves as you go along, you can keep them for two months. CELERI. A plant of the Umbelliferae family, in the Aminineae section, and of the parsley genus. There are several species or varieties of celery, all European, biennial: their roots are fibrous or tuberous, their leaves radical, large, twice winged, with broad, shiny leaflets; the stem is 3 to 6 decimeters high, twiggy, and bears umbels of small yellowish flowers followed by oval fruits. The edible parts of celery are the petioles, leaves and, in one species, the roots. LARGE-RIBBED CELERY. CULTIVATION.-In the first week of May, plow a piece of ground in the shade, and sow the seed of this celery, because, if sown in the sun, the plant could burn when it rises; when the seed is buried, spread a light layer of compost or very-short mulch over it, and water; We continue to give it a little wetting every day until it has risen, and we water it again very often to prevent it from hardening, until it is 10 to 14 centimetres high; then it's time to plant it. After ploughing one or more beds and covering them with mulch, use your feet to mark out ten or eleven radii on each bed, 2 metres 53 centimetres wide, and plant the young celery ao centimetres apart in the rows, immediately giving it a soft wetting; if the season is dry, we must water the celery abundantly at least every two days, firstly to prevent it from hardening off, secondly because it naturally likes water, and thirdly because we planted it close together to force it to grow quickly. When the celery is 40 to 46 centimetres tall, we need to think about blanching it, and we have two ways of doing this: 1° If we have old beds whose manure has been used up, we empty them by throwing the compost and manure over the edge to the right and left, and this creates a sort of trench in which we replant and govern the celery, corame O as we shall say in the following way, which is the most commonly used. 2° We dig a trench 1 meter 33 centimeters wide and 35 to 38 centimeters deep, at the sides of which there must be an empty space, 1 meter wide, for depositing the soil from the trench, and we loosen the bottom of this trench by plowing. When this is done, each celery plant with its root ball is lifted with a spade, and carefully planted in the bottom of the trench, making a dimple with the hand to receive the celery root ball, and covering it with earth which is slumped to sx it vertically: if some plants have side shoots or small eyelets, these are detached before replanting. In a trench 1 meter 35 centimeters wide, you can replant eight or nine celery plants per row, spacing the rows 18 to 22 centimeters apart; once the trench is planted, you immediately give it a copious moistening and, if the weather is dry, you water every day until the celery is well established. Twelve or fifteen days later, the celery should be sprouting from the heart; at this point, break up the earth deposited on either edge of the trench, and, if there are any leaves altered by the celery, remove them, and pour a 16-centimeter thickness of earth between the rows and stalks of celery, taking care that each stalk always retains its vertical direction. This first 08 operation is called "empiéter" (to encroach): twelve or fifteen days later, you can see that the celery has grown from the heart again; then you put loose earth back in between the rows and stalks of celery, to such a thickness that you can only see about 12 centimetres of the longest leaves: this second and final operation is called "rechausser" (to reshoot). After this second operation, the celery is blanched in twelve or fifteen days; then we pull it out, trim its roots, put it in bunches of six to seven feet per bunch, wash it, and finally send it to the market. TURKISH CELERY. This variety differs from the previous one in that it is much shorter and stockier; its ribs are a little larger and its heart fuller. Consumers now prefer it to large celery, and market gardeners are pleased with it because it is less costly to blanch than the other variety. CELERI-RAVE. This plant is sown at the same time and in the same way as the previous one, but it is only planted once and at a greater distance, so it doesn't grow so tall. In a bed 2 meters 33 centimeters wide, we plant seven rows of it, placing the plants 4o centimeters apart in the rows; we water it with as much care as the previous ones, until autumn, and when the frosts come, its root must be as big as a fist or the belly of a bottle; The tubers or roots are taken to the cellar, buried in sand if possible, and sold by the dozen throughout the winter. Celery root is excellent cooked. CELERY FOR CUTTING. This variety is distinguished by its smaller size and hollow ribs; its cultivation is also different and simpler. Sow in late March or April, broadcasting on a well-ploughed bed, and when the seed is buried and harrowed, cover with a centimetre of compost; When the plant has risen, it is moistened to encourage germination, and then tended with water to ensure that it remains tender at all times. When the plant has reached a height of 15 to 20 centimetres, it is cut, arranged in small bunches and sent to the market: It grows back immediately, and if care is taken to keep it moist, it can be picked every fifteen or eighteen days until the frosts. JUNE. RAIPONCE. Plant of the Campanulaceae family and the Campanula genus. It is a small native plant, biennial, with simple, taprooted roots, and radical, oval, lanceolate leaves; the stem is straight, slender, 5 centimeters or more high, sparsely twiggy, with bluish, campanulate flowers, followed by dried fruits that open at the sides to spread the seeds. The edible parts of this plant are the root and the radical leaves, which are eaten in salads. CULTIVATION. - Riponce is sown in June and July, to produce large roots in winter; those who prefer leaves to roots don't sow it until September. It is not necessary to plow the land where you want to sow raiponce; simply pass the push rake over it, to stir the soil to a depth of 2 centimeters. As this seed is very fine and very loose, it would be difficult to sow it alone without exposing yourself to putting too much in places, and it must be sown very lightly, as its leaves spread out in a circle on the ground: The seed is mixed with ash or fine, dry earth, and sown together on the prepared soil; then the soil is harrowed with a three-pronged fork to hide the seed, the earth is compacted a little so that it touches the seed on all sides, then a light, short mulch is spread over the whole, and finally the plant is watered as needed. Rapunzel is eaten from late December to February, or even later; it is pulled up with its root and rosette of leaves, peeled, washed and sold in small batches. The cultivation of raipouce is very restricted in our marshes, firstly because it is not lucrative, and secondly because country folk will pull it up where it grows naturally and supply the capital markets with it at very low prices. STRAWBERRY. A plant of the Rosaceae family, of the Dryadae order and of the genus whose name it bears. There are several species and many varieties of strawberry: all are small, herbaceous perennials, some native to Europe and others to America, 15 to 25 centimeters high, with trifoliate leaves and white flowers, followed by oval or rounded, sweet, red or white fruits, which bear seeds on their surface. The edible part of these plants is their fruit. OBSERVATIONS. - Strawberries are in everyone's taste, so strawberry cultivation has spread considerably around Paris, and gardeners within the city walls, where land is so expensive, can no longer compete in this crop, which has become a specialty. Of the eighteen hundred market gardeners in Paris, not even half a dozen grow strawberries naturally, and even fewer grow them under forced cultivation, which is the only way to expect any profit in Paris. Today, there are several varieties of strawberry whose fruit is larger and more attractive than that of the Alpine strawberry, but none of them bears fruit for as long, and their fruit is nowhere near as tasty as the Alpine strawberry. Its marketability is therefore more certain, and it is the only strawberry that market gardeners, who are still very few in number, can grow using forced cultivation only, with the hope of obtaining some profit. ALPINE OR FOUR-SEASON STRAWBERRY. FORCED CULTIVATION. - To cultivate the Alpine strawberry in this way, it must be renewed every year, either by sowing or dripping: renewal by sowing being the most advantageous, we will not deal with the other. At the end of June, we select a number of the most beautiful Alpine strawberries in a state of perfect maturity, crush them in water, and the seeds are easily extracted; when they have dried out, we plough a small corner of land, not in the shade, but which we can shade with a straw mat or two; and, when the top of the soil is well-divided and levelled with a rake, we sow the seed, on which we spread only a few millimetres of very fine soil or potting compost, and give it a light drenching; Repeat this drenching two or three times a day, if you don't shade with straw mats, as the top of the soil must not dry out until the seeds have risen, if you want them all to rise quickly; in less than 15 days, everything will have risen; six weeks later, i.e. two months after sowing, the plant will be ready to be transplanted into a nursery, where it will flower and produce runners, but we'll remove some as they appear. At the end of November, plough as many beds as you like, plant the strawberry plants 28 centimetres apart and, as soon as the frosts start, cover the beds with boxes and frames, so that the strawberry plants can continue to vegetate a little. We usually start forcing strawberry plants in February, when we remove all the soil from the paths to a depth of 54 centimetres, and fill them to the top of the collars with good new horse manure; Protect against frost by all known means, ventilate as needed, and rearrange and replace the stoves when you realize that they no longer provide enough heat for the strawberry plants, which must be kept clean and have 19 of their old leaves removed to allow light to penetrate everywhere, especially when the fruit begins to appear; finally, in the first days of April at the latest, the strawberry harvest can begin and last until the strawberry plants in the open ground give. There are other ways of forcing strawberry plants, in pots or greenhouses: some prefer runners to plants grown from seed, planting them in another season, etc.; but the cultivation method we have just described is the simplest and the only one practicable by market gardeners. TENDER TURNIP OF THE VIRTUES. We mention only this variety, which is oblong, white and very early, and could be sown at the end of June by market gardeners, if they still grew turnips; but they had to give up growing them a long time ago, because turnips are not a plant that can be forced, because they go to seed and stop growing if sown before June. All extramural growers sow turnips and can sell them much more cheaply than intramural market gardeners, where land is much more expensive. CHAMPIGNON. 291 A plant of the cryptogam family and the agaric genus; it grows naturally in France, on the lawns of dry meadows and woods, and at the foot of piles of horse manure: it's a white or grayish mushroom, fleshy, globose at first, composed of a stem and a cap whose blades, pinkish at first, become brown, even blackish, as the cap opens, spreading to a diameter of 10 centimeters; but then it's no longer saleable, though still good: it's when the mushroom is the size of a pigeon's egg to a hen's egg that it's most valuable, and most esteemed by consumers. In the past, market gardeners in Paris used to cultivate this mushroom at a profit; but since mushroom growers have been cultivating it at much lower cost in the quarries of Paris and the surrounding area, market gardeners have been making much less of it; they would even have been obliged to give it up altogether, if the mushrooms they bring in weren't whiter and more beautiful, and didn't have a better appearance and a higher price than those from the quarries. Although we are far from wishing to play the scholar, we will nevertheless note here, in favor of our colleagues, that scholars regard the white of this mushroom as the stems and branches of the plant, and the mushroom itself as its fruit. Although mushrooms can be made at any time of year, we prefer to make them in autumn. To do this, the manure must be prepared as early as July, in the manner described below. To judge the quantity of manure to be prepared, we first need to decide how many stacks to make; we usually make no less than two and no more than twelve stacks at a time, whose length we cannot determine here, although the most convenient are about 10 to 12 meters long. Manure preparation. - We take horse manure that we've been accumulating in piles for a month or six weeks, and bring it to the marshes on an empty, level, firm site; We then remove any large pieces of litter that have not been impregnated with urine, hay and any pieces of wood that may be present, as white does not set on these bodies. Striking the manure with the back of the fork, we lay it down in front of us in a floor at least 66 centimetres thick, pressing it down with the back of the fork. When the floor, which is almost always a long square, is made, it is stomped well, watered abundantly and stomped a second time, then left in this state for eight or ten days; then the manure ferments, scalds, sweats, and its surface is covered with a kind of white mold. After these eight or ten days, the floor must be remade from top to bottom on the same ground and rebuilt as before, with the necessary precaution of placing the manure from the edges of the floor into its interior, and the floor is left in this state for a further eight or ten days; at the end of this time, the manure should have acquired all the quality suitable for making millstones: on inspection, it should be found to be supple, soft, unctuous or greasy, without the smell of manure, and bluish-white inside, neither too wet nor too dry; if the tumier were not in all these conditions, there would be reason to fear that the millstones formed from it would not be very fertile. Mounting, larding and goptering. - The millstones must be 66 centimetres wide at the base, 66 centimetres high, and formed into a humpback shape, placed parallel to each other, 48 or 50 centimetres apart. The prepared manure is brought to the site; a man accustomed to this work, or better still, the market gardener himself, takes the manure in small bunches, lays them down in front of him, spreading them out and pressing them firmly against each other, and forms a humpback of the width and height indicated above; the man always works backwards, and when 08 he reaches the end, the humpback is finished: it is then combed, and beaten on the sides and top with the back of a shovel to make it smooth. In this state, the manure warms up, but can no longer take on very great heat; after a few days, we probe the wheel with the hand, and, if the heat is suitably gentle, we lard it. This operation consists of making small openings in the manure, the width of your hand, 5 centimetres from the ground and in a single line around the millstone, 33 centimetres apart (1). As you make these openings, you insert into each of them a small wafer of mushroom spawn (a mise, in market gardener's terms), three fingers wide and 8 or 10 centimetres long, and fold the manure over it so that it is well enclosed. Once this operation has been completed, the pack is covered with 10 to 1a centimetre-thick dry litter: this covering is called a chemise. Ten or twelve days later, we visit the millstones to see if the white has set; to do this, we lift the bottom of the liner, and look at the places where the white has been placed. When you see white filaments extending into the manure of the millstone, you know that the spawn has set and that it is good: if there are cakes or bets whose spawn does not (1) Some market gardeners place a second row of spawn 18 centimeters above the first. O8 not extend into the wheel, it's because it wasn't good; they are then removed and new ones put in their place; finally, when all the white is well set, and its filaments extend into the wheel, it's time to gopter it. This operation consists of coating the whole wheel with 5 centimetres of very fine, very soft earth: first, we remove the liner from over the wheel, plough the paths to a depth of about To centimetres, mix in potting soil and make everything as fine as possible; we lightly baste the entire surface of the wheel, and, while it's still wet, we take a shovel; This shovel is used to scoop up the earth prepared in the pathway, and to throw it against the grinding wheel, where it is held in place by applying the back of the shovel very sharply against the earth to prevent it from falling off, which requires dexterity and liveliness; as you gopt, you solidify the earth on the grinding wheel by striking it lightly with the back of the shovel, and then you put the shirt back on. When the millstones have been in this state for another fifteen or twenty days, they are inspected to see if the white is showing through the earth at the bottom of the millstones, and if the mushroom seed is forming, if all goes well, a few days later there will be mushrooms to pick: each time one is detached, a little compost is put in the hole left by the mushroom, and the part of the liner that was lifted is immediately put back on. When millstones are producing well, mushrooms can be picked every two days, and well-grown millstones usually produce mushrooms for two or three months; we've even seen millstones that, after producing a good crop and resting for two months, started again and produced a second crop. We have just described autumn mushroom cultivation as it is practiced in years that are neither dry nor rainy; but, in dry years, it is sometimes necessary to water the liner to maintain a slight humidity in the soil of the millstone; in rainy years, on the contrary, it is sometimes necessary to remove the excessively wet liner and replace it with a dry one. GROWING MUSHROOMS IN A CELLAR. CULTURE. - The manure is prepared in the open air in the marsh, as we have just said, and when it has reached the right point, it is lowered into the cellar: there it is arranged along the walls, so as to form a half-mill or a single-slope millstone; it can also be set up on shelves above the first. In the middle and on the floor of the cellar, millstones are built with two slopes, like those built in the open air: they are larded and gopted like the others, but they are not covered with a shirt; darkness takes its place: the skylights and doors are carefully closed, and the millstones, being sheltered from atmospheric influences, produce mushrooms for longer than those built in the open air. In the quarries in and around Paris, mushrooms are grown in the latter way, but in much greater quantities, and a prodigious amount of manure is consumed; but market gardeners' mushrooms are always preferred to the halle. Mushroom cultivation in the open air is rarely affected by the disease we call mole, but it frequently shows up in stacks built in cellars, sometimes taking over all the mushrooms and forcing the mushroom grower to establish his cultivation in another cellar. A mushroom affected by this disease has a warty cap, its leaves thicken, weld together, change color and become a shapeless mass that has lost the good odor of a healthy mushroom, has contracted another unpleasant one and is no longer saleable. How to make mushroom spawn. - Many market-gardeners take their spawn from old millstones that have ceased to produce, and store it in slabs in an attic or dry place; we have stored it in this way for a dozen years, and at the end of that time it was still good for larding millstones. Many of us thought that white from old millstones, which had already produced mushrooms who knows how many times, must have lost its fertility, and looked for a way to obtain some which had not yet produced mushrooms and which, therefore, could not be said to be exhausted. It's not that you can make spawn from scratch, but so little old spawn is used in the process that all the resulting spawn is considered new. Here's how it's done: first prepare a little manure, as you would for making millstones; then open a pelite trench at the foot of a wall, facing north, at least 66 centimetres wide and deep, and throw the earth over the edge of the trench. If you have a bit of old whitewash, divide it into small slabs and place them in two rows at the bottom of the trench, spacing the slabs 33 centimetres apart; then bring in the manure prepared in advance, arrange it neatly in the trench with a pitchfork and pack it down well. When the manure is 25 to 30 centimetres thick all over, you tamp it down well, then put the soil back on top, and tamp it down again. After twenty or twenty-five days, the spawn deposited in the bottom of the trench has vegetated and spread throughout the manure, which has itself become a mass of spawn; then the soil that covers it is removed, and with a spade the O8 manure is cut into square pieces 33 centimeters square and 20 to 24 centimeters thick: these pieces are split in two to facilitate their drying, and taken to an attic, where they are stored for four or five years, to be used for larding millstones. The best time to make this white is July. CHAPTER XI. Insect and disease damage to cheeses. To speak of insects and vegetable diseases in scientific terms is impossible for any market gardener, and the language of entomologists and nosologists will long be unfamiliar in our marshes. We have, however, felt it necessary to discuss insects and diseases in a work of the nature of this one, even though we can only designate them by trivial names, understood, it is true, by all market gardeners in Paris, but highly unintelligible to men accustomed to scientific language. In order to shorten this unattractive chapter, we name each vegetable in turn, as article headings, and below, in two columns opposite each other, we summarize the insects and diseases to which these vegetables are prone. As for the means of preserving them, we give very little information, as we are no more skilled in this area in market gardening than in field gardening. ASPARAGUS. Insects. When asparagus stalks go to seed, they are often attacked by the criocer, a red insect that eats the bark of the stalks and is sometimes so numerous that they are all covered with them. White grubs and moths also torment claws in the ground. Diseases. In greasy, damp soils, asparagus claws are prone to melting or rotting. AUBERGINE. Eggplant is prone to aphids and kermes; both can be destroyed by brushing the plant with water. This plant has no particular disease. CARDON. The cardoon's greatest enemy is a black fly that attaches itself to its leaves in huge numbers. We don't know whether this fly pumps juices or intercepts transpiration, but we do know that it slows and stops vegetation, and that the plant withers. As long as the cardoon is young and small, the moth also sometimes cuts the root and kills it. CARROT. A very formidable enemy of carrot seedlings in dry soils is a small spider that cuts young plants as they emerge: it can be destroyed by frequently watering the young plant. We know of no disease affecting carrots, apart from damping-off, which can affect them to some extent in unsuitable soils. CELERY. Celery leaves and ribs are quite prone to rust in its various stages of cultivation. Diseases. CELERY. Chervil has no known insects. Chervil is not prone to disease; only, Insects. Diseases. in summer, it can suffer from jaundice if the weather is very hot, and in unsuitable soils, it can be partially destroyed by damping-off. CURLY ENDIVE. In addition to the insects that harm romaine and lettuces, curly endive's other enemy is the cutworm, also known as the earthworm or large gray caterpillar. Curly endive is exposed to the same diseases as romaine and wild lettuce. We know of no other enemy to wild chicory, sown on a bed or under a frame, than the cattail, which, by making its galleries in all directions, reverses the germinating seeds and kills them. When grown on a bed and under a frame, little wild chicory is subject to only one disease: damping-off. Its name indicates its effect: the plant attacked by it melts in place, and the disease spreads by contagion. We attribute this disease to three causes: 1° a defect in the soil; 2° too much humidity; 3° lack of sunlight. You can't sow, nor can you plant. 303 plant in a place where melting has just wreaked havoc: everything will perish. CAULIFLOWER. Cauliflowers, like other cabbages, have many enemies; when sown in spring, they are liable to be devoured by flea beetles, which we call flea beetles and ground lice, as soon as the cotyledons emerge. The damage can be stopped by watering three or four times a day, or by shading the seedling. When the cauliflowers are free of this danger, caterpillars of several species arrive: yellow caterpillars, produced by white butterflies; large gray and green caterpillars, produced by butterflies of different colors. The caterpillar of the white butterfly eats only the leaves, but it devours quickly; it needs to be watched and destroyed often, as do the egg-layers that the butterflies deposit on the leaves. Large green caterpillars Diseases of cauliflower and other cabbages are not numerous. When the plant is very young, it is prone to suckering, and when the cauliflower is close to its head, it can still be attacked. Canker and rot can also occur in the trunk, ribs and pith of the cabbage, leading to death. Insects. or greyflies attack the cauliflower head itself as soon as it begins to appear, and would devour it if the cauliflowers were not visited every four or five days to kill all these insects. Later, when the cauliflowers go to seed, it's the green aphids that besiege the shoots by the millions, sometimes rendering the seed crop useless. If we now turn to the roots of the cauliflower, we find first of all the worm, which causes exostoses at the base of the cabbage, lodges in its interior, hollows it out and makes it suffer; the red-headed horsefly, the guillotine or small white worm, which eat its roots and core. APPLE KALE. Cabbage apples and diseases. What has just been said for cauliflower can have the same enemies as cauliflower. applies to all other cabbages. CUCUMBER. Same as melons. Same as melons. 305 Insects. Diseases. This plant is prone to melting, especially if sown thickly. We remind you that plants often melt because they are sown too thickly. SPINACH. The green aphid attacks spinach, but quite rarely. The big green caterpillar also eats it sometimes; its greatest enemy is the moth, when it starts to make its galleries in a spinach bed that is beginning to emerge; it tumbles and fans the racipes of the young plant and kills a large part of it. As spinach does not like very hot weather, spinach sown in summer is prone to jaundice; this disease can be stopped or reduced by plenty of watering. In very wet autumns, spinach can catch the sucker; in greasy, damp soils, it is prone to melting. ESTRAGON. Tarragon's only known disease is a kind of canker that appears as a yellowish spot at the foot of the plant. 20 Insects. Diseases. the base of the plant, causing the stems to die: this disease appears in inclined springs. HARICOT. In forced and natural cultivation, beans are frequently subject to gray mold. Loaches and slugs eat its leaves in wet weather. No other disease is known to affect beans grown under glass other than rust caused by gray mold. LETTUCE. The insects that damage romaine, some through the leaves, others through the roots, are the same ones that damage lettuces, and in the same way. Lettuces are subject to the same diseases as romaine; rust in particular is highly detrimental, manifesting itself first as coffee-colored, then as blackish spots: This disease leads to the loss of the plant. CHEESE. Lamb's lettuce has few enemies other than the moth, which makes its numerous galleries in an emerging seedling; in all its subterranean journeys, it causes damage to the plant. Moth and sometimes miller are the diseases of lamb's lettuce, when it is sown in damp soil, or when the season is very wet; it lifts and cuts young plants, especially after watering; it is at night in particular that it causes damage. Diseases. 307 also affected by rust, but very rarely. MELON. The greatest enemy of melons, in terms of insects, is the gray moth, a small spider barely perceptible to the eye, which settles on the underside of the leaves, forms a small web, bites the epidermis, sucks the parenchyma, alters or destroys the sonctions of the leaves and harms vegetation so much that melons attacked by it languish, and their fruit is never perfect, if, however, they can reach maturity. We counteract the multiplication of the grey melon by detaching the affected leaves; but the animal is so small that it can only be recognized by its damage, making it difficult to purge completely, and its multiplication is so rapid that in five or six days a square We call canker or ulcer an all-too-frequent disease that most often appears in the arms of the melon, sometimes on the branches, sometimes on the fruit itself. When this canker appears on a branch or fruit, either the branch or the fruit is removed with the disease, and all is said; but when it is located at the foot of the plant, as is most often the case, it is very dangerous. It first manifests itself as a small, livid spot on the surface of the bark; this spot is the beginning of a rot that spreads rapidly, taking over and rotting the entire bark around the base. If only we could see this disease when it starts, when it has only a or 3 mil Insects. of melons, especially under frames, is poisoned by it. This insect doesn't like water, and watering would destroy it; but how to reach it with water, since it's always under the leaves? It has been suggested to destroy it by fumigating with tobacco, but this method, already successfully used, is not yet accepted in market gardening. Melons still have to fear the large flea beetle, which bites the underside of the leaves; but this insect is much less dangerous; watering destroys it or keeps it away. Diseases. limeters in diameter, it could undoubtedly be cured by scraping off what is rotten and cauterizing the wound with ashes or powdered plaster; but by the time it is noticed, it is almost always too late; cauterization is no more than a palliative, which more or less prolongs the life of the plant, without being able to restore to the fruit the quality that the disease has caused it to lose. ONION. We know of no other enemy to the onion than the guillotine or small white worm, the same one that eats the root of cabbage: it gets into the onion's tray and prevents it from growing. In greasy, cool soil, in wet years, onions are subject to damping-off, also known as "nuile". This disease manifests itself as a yellowish spot where the onion forms; it grows, the leaves turn yellow and the whole plant rots. 309 Insects. Sorrel has two very damaging insects: one is the large beetle, which eats the leaves down to the rib and drops to the ground as soon as it is approached; the other is a caterpillar, which also devours the leaves, and only disappears in cold weather. Diseases. Rust manifests itself as reddish spots on sorrel leaves; these gradually enlarge, multiply and eventually lose the leaf. PARSLEY. Parsley has no insects. Parsley is subject only to the diseases of chervil, but heavy frosts cause it to perish from the crown. No insects. PIMPRENELLE. Melt sometimes attacks this 1 salad garnish in certain areas. LEEK. The enemies of pear and pear cactus are caterpillars, which eat the leaves, and the small red-headed horsefly, which cuts the stalk in the ground. Unseasonal weather causes rust on pear leaves, and the whole plant is subject to melting in POURPIER weather. Purslane, sown in heavy, loamy soil, is subject to melting, which also happens elsewhere when it is sown too thickly. PUMPKIN. Pumpkins, giraumonts, turbans, etc., could be exposed to the same diseases as melons; but their vigorous vegetation protects them from their attacks or renders them incapable of harming them.
