Green aphid. - When this aphid gets into the heart of the romaine, it is lost; because it multiplies so quickly, there is no way to get rid of it without sacrificing it. Black aphid. - This one is as harmful as the previous one, but is destroyed by heavy thunderstorms. White aphid. - It attaches itself to the plant's root and causes it to languish: it can sometimes be destroyed by watering. Red-headed chafer - Larva of the small chafer, which eats and cuts the root of the romaine plant, killing it. When you see a romaine wilting, you dig around at the foot, find the gadfly and kill it: this gadfly is also called the red-headed worm. The miller - This disease manifests itself as white spots on the underside of the leaves; these spots multiply very quickly and cause the plant to languish. Red ćollet. - This is a consequence of the previous one, when it appears on romaine plants which, after having grown vigorously, are stopped by a sudden cold spell. Melting. - This manifests itself as a black spot on the plant's collar; it is fatal and there is no cure. Rust. - Its name indicates its color; it attaches itself to the leaves, particularly on the underside, and makes them hooked, emInsects. Cricket. - There are several ways of destroying it, either by giving it a pile of manure to hide in, where it is crushed, or by filling its hole with water and pouring a few drops of oil over the water. When the oil touches the cricket, it emerges from the hole and dies immediately. Illnesses. This is not incurable; sometimes good weather makes it disappear when bad weather has produced it. Fly speck. - This disease is particularly common on romaine; it manifests itself as gray or russet spots on the inner leaves, which soon turn rotten and kill the plant. Speckles appear on romaine plants that are watered several times when they have reached three-quarters of their size, and when the sun is shining brightly during watering. SCAROLE. Escarole has the same insects as those mentioned for lettuce. This plant has the same texture and juices as curly endive, and is subject to the same diseases. TOMATO. Tomatoes have no insects. Sometimes, but rarely, canker manifests itself at the foot of tomatoes in very wet weather. Having said what we know about the insects and diseases that damage or destroy vegetables, we feel it our duty to end this chapter with an account of the opinion, handed down by tradition among Paris market gardeners, concerning three atmospheric phenomena. Sensible people advised us not to talk about them in this book, because they thought there were errors or prejudices in the opinion in question: in spite of our deference to their advice, we were unable to comply; for, since we are writing the history of market gardening in Paris in 1844, and setting out its improvements, integrity dictates that we should also make known those beliefs likely to be regarded as errors or prejudices; subsequently, we can destroy them or explain the reason for them, if they have any foundation: here are the three phenomena. Rainbows. - Many market gardeners believe that when a rainbow appears, its influence can kill mushrooms that are in the seed stage in earthen millstones, albeit covered by their shirts. We don't all believe in this effect of the rainbow: many of us are inclined to attribute the loss of the mushroom seed, in this circumstance, to a sudden drop in temperature; but we haven't verified it, and leave the question as we found it. Thunder. - The loss of mushroom kernels by thunder, and even of mushrooms as large as hazelnuts, is far more proven than that caused by rainbows; there is not a single one of us who has not had the unfortunate experience of this in his millstones on earth. Those who make their millstones in cellars, in the quarries of Paris or the surrounding area, have never complained about it; its influence probably doesn't extend that far. Be that as it may, it's a fact that a violent thunderclap kills the fungus grain of shirt-clad millstones on the ground. Is it noise, is it electricity, is it a sudden cooling of the atmosphere, is it lightning that produces this unfortunate effect? No market gardener can answer these questions; but all can assure that the fact takes place. Trombe de vent. - This is the name given to the whirlwinds of wind and dust that rip up or break down trees and uncover the houses they pass through. There are hardly any market gardeners who haven't seen at least one of these pass through their marshes, and who don't know that such a whirlwind can take away manure, bells and sashes, and do appalling damage: In an old farm where a whirlpool was passing through, we saw the master, the girls and the boys running, shouting and waving anything they could get their hands on, such as spades, forks and rakes, in an attempt to break the whirling column of air; but all this noise and movement produced no effect, and the whirlpool continued on its way. Another method, whose effectiveness we ourselves have once tested, consists in throwing water against the whirlpool; one day we saved the last quarter of a line of frames that a whirlpool was taking away, by throwing water at it from the head of a watering can. One day, some physicist will be able to explain what we've just said about rainbows, thunder and waterspouts. CHAPTER XUI. Harvesting and preserving vegetable seeds. We felt it necessary, in a book on vegetable growing, to include a short chapter in which some information could be found on how we go about obtaining and harvesting the best seeds, and preserving them for as long as possible. We will have little to say that is not known to all gardeners, and especially to those who have been concerned with the organization of seeds, their tissues and the more or less alterable substances of which they are composed. What we will say here, once and for all, is that when the seeds are dry, they should be placed, species by species, in paper or canvas bags, and placed in a dry place, sheltered from the outside air, humidity and heat. ASPERGE. When, around June 15th, we stop picking asparagus, it rises and produces round, succulent, pea-sized, very red fruits, which ripen in autumn and contain, or should each contain, six black seeds; we crush these fruits in water to extract the seeds and dry them: they keep well for two or three years. AUBERGINE. When eggplants are harvested for sale, some are left to ripen completely; the seeds are then removed, and are lenticular or kidney-shaped, brick-colored and 2 millimeters in diameter: they are good for two years. CARROT. To obtain good carrot seeds, sow them at the end of July; during winter, cover them with litter to protect them from frost; when the thaw comes, uproot them and replant them 4o centimeters apart; water, if necessary, until mid-June, and in July the carrot seed ripens: This seed, as in parsley and other umbellifers, is a small fruit, divided into two parts, each containing a seed under a tough, oval-shaped envelope, 3 millimeters long, with four lines of long lamellar teeth on the outside, making them look bristly: These lamellae are usually broken by rubbing the seeds in the hands before sowing them, but this is not enough: when the seed is sown, it must be plumbed with the feet, to bring it into contact with the earth and speed up germination. Carrot seed is good for five or six years. CARDON. To obtain cardoon seed, in autumn, leave a few plants in place; as frost approaches, cut off the large leaves, mound them and cover them with litter, like artichokes; in spring, uncover them, grow their stalk, flower and ripen their seeds in October: these seeds are gray, almost straight and cylindrical, 8 to 9 millimeters long, with a few small longitudinal ridges: they are good for three or four years. CELERY. In November, select the most beautiful celery plants of each species, replant them in the ground at a distance of 53 centimeters, cover them over winter and uncover them in spring; they come up and ripen their seeds in August: these seeds are one or two times smaller than parsley seeds and of the same conformation; they are good for six years. CHERFEUIL. Seeds are obtained from chervil sown in September, which has overwintered and risen in the spring; they are linear, acute, 12 mm long and blackish: their germinative property is preserved for four or five years. 319 Cabbages, in general, are biennial plants that only produce seeds in their second year, and it is from cauliflowers sown in September that we collect seeds ten or eleven months later. So, in April, May and June, when the cauliflower harvest is at its peak, we mark a certain number of plants with the best apples, and we don't cut them off; we even leave a leaf or two on the apple, so that it doesn't harden, until we see several shoots coming out of the seed: then we remove these dry leaves and water to give the cabbage vigor. These shoots grow, branch out and flower; it's then that you must not spare the wetting; it's also the moment when, sometimes, the green aphid establishes itself, in immense quantities, on the young shoots of the shoots and would compromise the harvest of the seeds, if we didn't hasten to destroy it by all known means, among which we indicate this one, as the most expeditious and safest: wet the plant with a watering can and sprinkle powdered quicklime on the aphids; if the aphids return, start again. Soon the siliques grow and swell; when, in the course of August, you think they're ripe, you cut off I the little twigs that carry them, dry them on a sheet in the sun, where almost all the siliques open naturally and drop their seeds, which are round, blackish, as big as No. 9 shot; you winnow them, and, when they're quite dry, put them in bags: they're good for eight or nine years. CHOU-POMME. Under the name of apple cabbage, we include all cabbages that form an apple with their central leaves, from York sprouts to large cabbages. What we're about to say also applies to savoy cabbage. To collect seeds from all these cabbages, it is not essential to let the apple fall off, as is done in many places; when the apple is cut for sale or for cooking, enough of the core remains to produce shoots, flowers, siliques and seeds in large enough quantities for horticultural use, and these seeds are just as good as those that would have come from the apple. Most of these seeds are a little larger than those of cauliflower, equally round and of the same blackish color; they are harvested at various times of the summer and in the same way as we said for cauliflower: the seeds of apple and Milan cabbages are also good for eight or nine years. CURLY ENDIVE. To obtain seed for curly endive and its varieties, sow in a bed at the beginning of February and plant out in April. When the time comes to bind it for blanching, select a dozen of the best plants and do not bind them. The seeds are small, oblong, angular and 2 millimeters long, not including the small toothed crown at the end. The seeds of curly endive and wild chicory, and those of escarole, are very tightly packed in their calyxes, only coming out with great difficulty, and can remain on their stems for several years without falling off; they must therefore be beaten on a sheet with a flail to loosen them: they are good for five or six years. CUCUMBER. Cucumbers and gherkins are left to ripen completely, even to rot, before the seeds are collected; they are as long, but narrower, than those of melons: they are treated in the same way and keep just as long. 21 CRESS ALÉNOIS. The seed is chosen for sowing in April; it rises at the end of May and ripens in June; its seed is brick-colored, oval, 2 millimeters long: it is good for two years. SPINACH. When autumn-sown spinach has spent the winter, it rises quickly in spring and soon ripens its seeds, which are rounded or armed with a few sharp points: it is good for three or four years. LITTLE BLACK LETTUCE. In February, this lettuce is harvested, so we choose the best ones, lift them with a good root ball, replant them on a warm layer and place a bell on each one. In March, the cloches are removed, the lettuces grow taller, flower and set seed. By July, most of the seeds are ripe, so the plants are gently plucked, placed upright against a wall in the sun for a day or two, then shaken and beaten on a sheet in a van; left to dry in the sun for a few more days, they are beaten a second time, the seeds are winnowed out and, when well cleaned, put into bags (1). All lettuces have ellipsoid seeds, 3 millimeters long, black or whitish, with longitudinal striations; they can hardly be distinguished from each other except by their black or whitish color; the little black one (2) is black: it retains its germinative capacity for three or four years; the following ones retain it for the same length of time. GOTTE LETTUCE. This one is treated like the previous one, but (1) It's by ellipsis that we say petite noire; to complete the meaning, we should say petite laitue à graine noire. (2) This is the general way of harvesting lettuce seed; but those of us who insist on harvesting only perfect seeds leave the seed-bearing lettuces in place, and pick the seeds as they ripen, which can be recognized when their white aigrette rises out of the calyx and detaches the seeds; and, as these aigrettes only appear successively, we are obliged to return to the same seed-bearing lettuce eight or ten times to strip it of its good seeds. This way of harvesting the seed in detail, by pinching only the tips of the branches that carry it, is called pinching. It gives its seed a little later: this seed is whitish and can be collected like that of the little black one, as well as the three following ones. LETTUCEJROUGE. This lettuce heads in May. Its black seed is harvested in July. GEORGES LETTUCE. It heads and rises at the same time as red lettuce; its seed is black and it is harvested at the same time. GREY LETTUCE. This lettuce can be grown throughout the summer, but the best apples for seed are left to set in June; they are grayish-white and are harvested in September. ORDINARY LAMB'S LETTUCE. Having spent the winter, lamb's lettuce goes to seed in the first spring; but as its flowering lasts long enough, and its seed (or rather its berries) fall as it ripens, it is left to fall; The soil falls to the bottom, and the seed (or small fruit) floats to the surface through its two sterile, air-filled compartments. It is then washed, dried on a cloth, winnowed and bagged. The seed of ordinary lamb's lettuce is different in that it is not crowned: it is good for seven or eight years. REGENCE CITRUS. The seed of this variety can be recognized by its smaller size and the small toothed crown at the top. It is also harvested in spring and in the same way. MELON. Everyone knows that, when you eat a good melon, you can hope that its seed will reproduce other equally good ones; but you can never be sure. The way to avoid being deceived is not to prefer the seeds of the largest melons, but rather those that are the best of their kind; for melons of an extraordinary size for their species are subject to variation the following year: we therefore choose for grainc the melon that is the best of its kind; we let it ripen well, but not rot; we extract the seed, which we wash and dry well before putting it in a bag. All melon seeds are oblong, compressed, more or less large, leathery-skinned, yellowish-white and smooth; cantaloupe seeds are 11 millimeters long and 6 millimeters wide, while market melon seeds are only 1 centimeter long and 5 millimeters wide. We keep melon seeds for four or five years, but there are examples that show they can be good for twenty-five years or more. ONION. Having spent the winter in an attic, onions are planted in the open ground, in spring, 45 centimetres apart; soon they come up and their uprights are tied to stakes, to prevent the wind from breaking them. Around August and September, the numerous globular capsules, as large as peas, ripen and begin to open into three valves; the heads are then cut off, dried on a sheet in the sun, and rubbed in the hands to bring out the seeds, which are black, angular, irregular and less than 3 millimeters long: they are good for three years. ORDINARY sorrel: what we call the seed in sorrel is a triangular, brown, shiny, unilocular capsule, indehiscent and containing the true seed. Sorrel rises in spring, and its seed is easy to collect: it is good for three aus. PANAIS. The parsnip seed is treated like the carrot and grows at the same time; it belongs to the same family, but its seed is very different from that of the carrot; here it is very flat, oval, surrounded by a broad membrane no less than 5 millimeters high: this seed germinates for two or three years. PERSIL. After spending the winter, parsley rises in spring and ripens its fruit in June; these fruits are oval, longitudinally striated, 2 millimeters long, didymous or dividing into two bean-shaped parts, each containing a seed. Harvesting parsley berries or seeds is straightforward: they retain their germinative capacity for four or five years. PARSLEY. Burnet is not often sown in gardens, because it is a perennial plant that is usually propagated by bursting. When sown in spring, it sets seed the following year. The seed is brown, oval, wrinkled, ridged and has four wings; its length is 3 to 4 millimeters: it is good for three years. LEEK. The leek overwinters well in the open ground, so you can leave as much as you like, and it comes up in the spring; it bears fruit in exactly the same way as the onion, and its capsules are treated in the same way: its seed is black like the onion's, but a little smaller, more pointed at one end and less angular; it germinates equally well for three years. POIRÉE, CARDE-POIRÉE. These plants are stored over winter; the following spring, they come up and ripen their seeds in the summer. The seeds must be dried in the sun and beaten to detach them from the plant; these seeds are fruits as large as peas, very rough on the outside, black and hard as jet on the inside, and contain a single kernel: their germinative capacity is preserved for three or four years. PUMPKIN. The seeds of pumpkins and giraumonts vary according to species, not in shape, but in size; they are much smaller in the Jerusalem artichoke than in the large pumpkin; but they all resemble each other in the bead that surrounds them, whereas the seeds of melons and cucumbers lack this bead. In large pumpkins, the seeds are 2 centimetres q millimetres long, while in turbans, they are only 1 centimetre 8 millimetres. All these seeds are collected when the fruit is eaten, can be washed or not, and keep well for four or five years. RAIPONCE. The raiponce, sown in July, rises the following spring, and the seed ripens in July. This seed is as fine as dust, and is good for three years. BLACK RADISH. Black radishes, sown in June, are usually chosen as seed-bearing plants: at the end of December, the most beautiful ones are selected, planted 60 centimetres apart, covered during heavy frosts, and their seeds ripen in June; seeds that are harvested like those of pink radishes, which are the same color and size, and good for three years. RAVE, RADIS. When seeds are made for the trade, they are sown in spring in beds or fields of rabe or radish, and left to set seed; but market gardeners grow very little rabe, and among the radishes, pink is the most popular with consumers, so it's almost the only one they grow: When they need pink radish seed, they choose a certain number of the best from among the pink radishes they have sown in the spring, pull them out and replant them in the open ground, 40 or 45 centimetres apart; they are watered immediately, of course, and three or four more times as they come up, and the seed usually ripens in June. We then pull out the stalks and place them upright against a wall, facing south, to dry; but the siliqua of turnips and radishes does not open of its own accord like that of cabbages; it must be beaten and broken with a flail to bring out the seeds, which are once as large as those of cabbages, less round and of a C color tending towards brick: after having winnowed them, we lock them up and they are good for three years. ROMAN. These plants have seeds of the same shape and length as those of lettuces, also ridged, but a little narrower: all are grayish-white and retain their germinative capacity for three or four years. GREEN ROMAINE. At the end of April or beginning of May, when romaine planted in the ground is well capped, we let the most beautiful heads grow upwards, water them to give them vigor, and in the course of the summer, when they have deflowered and the calyxes are well swollen, we pull them up and place them upright against a wall, in the sun, as we said for lettuces, and collect the seed in the same way. Romaine seeds keep well for three or four years. BLOND ROMAINE. This is planted a little later in the ground, and the seed is harvested about a month later. GRAY ROMAIN. Grey romaine is sown in the open ground throughout the summer, so there are several times when the seed can be harvested. SCAROLE. As we don't grow escarole in spring, we get our seeds from the escarole we sow in August. In November, we mark the most beautiful plants and protect them from frost during the winter by covering and uncovering them as required. During the light frosts of spring, we cover them with cloches that we remove when the weather is fine; the escarole then rises, its seed ripens in September and we harvest it as we just said for curly endive: it is equally good for five or six years. 333 Calendar of market garden cultivation, or summary of work to be done and produce to be harvested in each month of the year. Having given, on page 92, the reasons which determined us to begin our presentation of market gardening with the month of August, these same reasons lead us to begin this calendar with the same month. AUGUST. In this month, the soil must continue to be covered with all the vegetables of the season, and to keep them moist and clean. Remember, too, that this month is the beginning of the horticultural year, and that it's time to start accumulating horse manure, which will need to be consumed prodigiously from November until April next year. As the melons, planted on the trench beds, are sold, the trenches are emptied of the sumier that formed the beds, filled with the soil that had been taken from them, ploughed and planted with hard and semi-hard cauliflower, escarole, coarse and semi-fine chicory and curly savoy cabbage, to be sold during the winter. After the 15th of the month, as the beds become emptier, we sow white onions, round-seeded and prickly spinach, to be sold from autumn to spring; radishes and lamb's lettuce, to be sold in October; half-long carrots, to be delivered for consumption the following spring. We also sow Dutch beans, which we cover with frames as the first frosts approach. Cardoon, celery, cauliflower, pear-cardoon, leek, sorrel and tarragon are all given heavy wetting, but escarole, chicory, cucumbers and gherkins are watered only moderately, unless the weather is very dry. Visit seedbeds often to purge them of insects, and collect seeds as they ripen. If you haven't started growing mushrooms in July, you can do so in this month and the next; however, you should use this latitude very sparingly, as it's always a good idea to get there first, and then eight days late can mean losing a season: finally, all vegetables should be weeded, hoeed and weeded in this month, as in all those of the year when vegetation is active. This month's crops are chicory, grey lettuce, blond romaine, summer cauliflower, leeks, carrots, a little spinach, purslane, escarole, chervil, parsley, melons, cucumbers, radishes, gherkins, sorrel, tomatoes, eggplants, pumpkins of all kinds. SEPTEMBER. The earth should be just as full of vegetables in this month as in the last. Autumn cauliflowers, escarole and chicory take pride of place, while romaine, melons and cucumbers gradually disappear. No more lettuces in our marshes, but then we sow early cabbages, such as York, pain-de-sucre and cœur-de-bœuf, to be transplanted later in nurseries; from the 8th to the 12th, we sow small and large salomon cauliflowers, half-hard and hard, to be transplanted either on ados or in trays, and then planted on beds or in the open ground when the season arrives. We sow little black or crepe lettuce to be planted under a bell or cold frame; we continue to sow lamb's lettuce and spinach, and, if the weather is dry, we need to water these plants to make them stand up. We continue to pile up manure to make layers in November, and to warm up white and green asparagus from the end of October; we also prepare some to make mushroom wheels. Towards the end of the month, we start tying and stuffing cardoons to blanch them; we replant celery in trenches or in old layers and pile it up; we sow pink radishes again, we sow chervil which will give in spring, we make a second sowing of small black lettuce which will be transplanted under cloche and planted on layers in November; At the same time, we also sow green romaine, to be transplanted under cloche, because it's romaine that we'll later plant between four lettuces per cloche on bed. Autumn cauliflowers are harvested as their heads reach perfection; a second sowing of raiponce is made to follow the one already made, escarole and chicory continue to be bound, and, if the weather is dry, cauliflowers, celery and anything else that seems to need it is wet. At the end of this month and in the following one, it's time to think about putting up the trunks and frames, and making straw mats; there's no time to take care of this during the day, and even the rain doesn't bring the market gardener or his people in; it's in the morning by candlelight and in the evening at night that all these things are prepared and put in order, so that when the time comes to use them, they're all ready to hand. In this month, we harvest chicory from Meaux and Rouen, escarole, cauliflower, carrots, parsnips, the last lettuces, the last melons, all sorts of supplies, sorrel, gherkins, pimentos, eggplant, tomatoes and pumpkins of all kinds. OCTOBER. In the first days of this month, you can also sow some of the seeds sown in the previous month: for example, you can still sow raiponium and lamb's lettuce; you can sow small black lettuce and early green romaine for bedding or coastal planting; and finally, you can sow chervil, which will be ready in spring: From one fortnight to the next, cardoons are stuffed and relined; celery is completely milled to bleach it and keep it available for sale; York, sugarloaf and oxheart cabbages, sown in the first days of September, are transplanted into the nursery. Cauliflowers sown at the same time are transplanted into boxes, so that they can be covered with frames at the first frost; if the asparagus stalks are dry, they are cut flush with the ground and the claws are lightly tilled. We continue to blanch cardoons and celery, and bind escarole and chicory; we begin to pick mâches sown in August, radishes and spinach. Towards the end of the month, we sow gotte, Georges, red and gray lettuces under cloche, to be transplanted onto teens and under cloche; we also sow blond and gray romaine in the same way, to be transplanted onto teens and under cloche or under chassis. Autumn cauliflowers are currently in full growth, and there are abundant heads to be cut every day and sent to the market; the white onion sown in August is being planted permanently. There are usually light frosts at the end of the month, so we put the frames on the cauliflowers we've transplanted into boxes and give them some air during the day. You can start heating green asparagus, even white asparagus; but it's in the following month that the big harvest of these early asparagus takes place. Chicory, escarole, cauliflower, premien cardoons, celery, carrots, parsnips, lamb's lettuce, raipons, eggplant, tomatoes, peppers, mushrooms, parsley, chervil, turnips, radishes, spinach and various pumpkins are harvested. NOVEMBER. This is the month when the early harvest begins in earnest, and a considerable supply of manure is needed to get the job done. From the very first days, white asparagus is heated, and sales continue to be heated; romaine, black, gotte, Georges, grey and red lettuces, sown in the previous month, continue to be transplanted, on ados and under cloche; some may even be sown again at the beginning of this month, if you are afraid of not having enough, as there is a huge consumption of forced lettuces and romaines in Paris. The first black lettuces are planted in beds, transplanted onto ados; York, pain-de-sucre and cœur-de-bœnf cabbages are planted in rows and even in furrows, and when frost threatens, a handful of litter is placed on each plant; We continue to plant white onions indefinitely; if it freezes at 3 degrees, we cut the remainder of the autumn cauliflowers, and cover the unbound escarole and chicory with litter. If the young romaine plant seems to be growing too fast, transplant it onto another bed; make supports around the beds and beds to prevent frost from penetrating; also replace cauliflower transplanted onto a frame for the same reason you transplanted romaine. As the frost increases, we spread straw mats over the beds and bells, pull out the unbound escarole and chicory and cover them with large quantities of dry manure, or take them to the cellar, where they are placed root up to blanch; we cover the celery trenches with litter, and put the cardoons in a greenhouse or cellar to protect them from the frost: In November, chicory, escarole, lamb's lettuce, raiponce, cardoons, celeriac and others, red cabbage and others, carrots, leeks, parsnips, mushrooms, chervil, spinach and various pumpkins are harvested. DECEMBER. Winter is usually more severe in this month than in the previous one, and the greengrocer must redouble his vigilance to preserve his plants: when the frost increases, we put short, dry manure between the bells of ados, we surround the layers already made with good accots, and we put new manure in the paths; we continue to heat the white and green asparagus; we continue to make layers to plant black lettuce under frame, to sow early carrots, turnips, radishes. We put manure on the soil where we have to make new layers, so that it doesn't freeze: whenever the sun is shining and the weather is mild, we give air to the frames, even to the bells: if severe frosts are forecast, short manure is put back between the bells on the bines, full-ground plants such as pear, carder-pear, sorrel, parsley and lamb's lettuce are covered with litter and uncovered as soon as it thaws; but, as sorrel and parsley are an everyday need, old plants are warmed up on beds and under frames: when snow has fallen on the straw mats that cover the frames and bells, and the frost continues without the sun coming out, you can leave this snow, which is a good shelter, for some time, but you mustn't let it melt on the layers or on the ados, which it would cool and make too damp. Those who did not sow snow peas or early peas under cold frames in the previous month can sow them in this one: we can prepare mushroom wheels that will yield in March; we can sow melons and cucumbers. We sell the last chicory and escarole, the last loose cauliflower; we continue to sell lamb's lettuce, celeriac, celery and celeriac, cardoon, mushrooms, parsley, chervil, leeks, red cabbage and others, spinach, sorrel, the first white and green asparagus, the first black lettuce. 08 JANUARY. This month's work is a continuation of the previous month's, and requires no less care to protect young plants from frost, and to give them as much sun and air as possible during the day, if only for an hour. So we continue to make new layers to plant black lettuce or crepe under frame, to heat sorrel, parsley, and sometimes other supplies, to sow turnips, radishes. We heat white and green asparagus; we make layers with bells, and we plant under each bell four lettuces and a romaine in the middle; we sow caroltes hátivest on layer and under frame, of the small wild chiconée also under frame. It goes without saying that we cover these new layers with good aggots, and that we line the paths with good meuf manure. Several types of cauliflower are planted under frames. At the end of the month, if it doesn't freeze, we plow the best coastal areas; and duty plants the first green romaine, which we preud on the teens. In November, we begin to pick black lettuce planted on beds and under chasers. Open ground yields little in this month, but if you have a few cofters and frame panels to shelter a bit of sorrel, parsley, chervil and periwinkle, these plants will still yield some produce: In this month, we harvest black lettuce, lamb's lettuce, celeriac, leek; celery and celeriac, red cabbage, Brussels sprouts and others, parsley, chervil, spinach, mushrooms, white and green asparagus, cauliflower kept in the cellar by the hub indicated on page 13a. = FEBRUARY. From the first days of February, a mother layer is made to sow cantaloupes of various varieties and cucumbers, which are transplanted eight or ten days later onto another layer known as the nursery layer, in potting soil or in small pots, according to the calculation made of the time to elapse until they can be planted permanently. By this time, greengrocers already have empty beds that have yielded black or crepe lettuce: these beds are turned over, mixed with new manure, rebuilt, and replanted with another season of small black lettuce, a first season of cauliflower, romaine; carrots, radishes, etc. are sown in them.: we make new layers to sow fine chicory, flax beans to transplant on other layers; we plant the first semi-hard cauliflowers in the open ground in a coastal where we have already planted green romaine, and which we will shelter if necessary with a handful of straw; carrots and radishes are sown in the open ground and only on the coast, at the same time as romaine and cauliflowers; leeks are sown in the sun to be planted in the open ground in spring; eggplants and tomatoes are sown in the sun and under a frame to be planted in the open ground after the frosts. If you haven't been able to plant all your white onions in autumn, as well as York, pain-de-sucre and cœur-de-bœuf cabbages, you finish planting them; you continue to heat white and green asparagus. During this time, you must make sure that frost does not penetrate under any cloche or frame, take advantage of the sun to give air to what needs it, get rid of excess humidity, clean and remove any damaged leaves from lettuces, romaine and other plants. After the 15th or 18th, the first melons and cucumbers are planted on beds that have been turned over and refurbished, and fitted with boxes and frames. Towards the end of the month, we plough the land where we'll be making trenches for cantaloupe melons in March; we sow carrots, spinach, parsley and chervil in the open ground; we plant the beans and beanslageolets we sowed and transplanted at the beginning of the month on beds; we cover them with trays, and sow others to succeed them. For sale this month: black lettuce, new radishes, green romaine, sorrel, lamb's lettuce, raipon, parsley, chervil, sorrel, celery and celeriac, leeks, parsnips, first green beans, mushrooms, white and green asparagus, preserved cauliflower, new lettuce for cutting. MARCH. In the first few days of this month, we start to open the trenches in which we will successively make layers on which we plant cantaloupe melons in large quantities and which we cover with frames as we plant them; we make layers to continue planting lettuces and romaines under cloche and under frame; we sow on layer and under frame other melons for a second or third season; green, blond and grey romaine, grey, red and Georges lettuces are planted in the open ground, and taken from the ados where they have spent the winter; and, as in this season there is usually no fear of heavy frosts, many more seeds are sown in the open ground in our marshes than elsewhere, and in strong, cold soils, they are only sown in April and May: so, in March, we sow short, half-long and long carrots, onions, leeks, fine and half-fine chicories, turnips and radishes, oscille, parsnips, Alençon cress; gotte, red and Georges lettuces; we do a second! of spinach to succeed the one sown in February. in February, and although we sowed fine and semi-fine chicory in the open ground, we also sow some on beds and under chassis, which will have come before that sown in the open ground; we make new beds in trenches, for beans; we sow eggplants and tomatoes on beds; we sow asparagus seed in the open ground, and we can start planting last year's seedling; finally we plant in the open ground many cauliflowers raised under chassis. In this month, there is usually a drying haze, which requires moderate and judicious watering of beds and soil. In this month, we have black lettuce, new radishes, green romaine, sorrel, parsley, chervil, the last lamb's lettuce and raipons, new carrots, green beans, leeks, spinach, small wild chicory, champiguon, tarragon, the last white and green asparagus, the last preserved cauliflower. 16 347 There are no longer any heavy frosts to fear; but we must always be on guard against late frosts, which are often fatal to over-confident early fruit growers. We continue to plant melons in trenches under panels, and we sow others to be planted on beds and under cloche; we prune and line melons under frames; we turn over old lettuce beds, as they are emptied, and we replant them with cloche melons. It's time to sow all the cucurbits that are to be planted in the open ground, such as the various pumpkins, giraumonts, cucumbers and gherkins. Tomatoes and eggplants are planted on beds; all market garden vegetables are sown and planted in the open ground in spring, summer and autumn, and it would be too long and tedious to list them here. The frames of the beds for carrots, turnips, radishes and chicory are removed and placed on top of the last ones, melons, which are planted in trenches. As the cold decreases, dryness usually increases: care must be taken to ensure that each vegetable does not lack the moisture it needs; finally, by the end of the month, no bare patch should be left in a swamp. In this month, we sell the last black lettuces, the first gotte and Georges lettuces, the first melons, petit prescott fond blanc and cucumbers; we continue to sell green romaine, carrots, turnips, radishes, sorrel, parsley, certeuil, tarragon, salad burnet, green beans, carde-poirée, mushrooms. MAY. A market garden must, in this month, represent a carpet of varied greenery, and one of the market gardener's main tasks is to sow in such a way that he always has plants ready to replace those that have reached maturity and are daily delivered for consumption, so that his marsh is always full until the end of autumn. We plant the last cantaloupe melons in trenches; we turn over the last empty lettuce and carrot beds, to plant other melons; we plant eggplants and tomatoes, if we weren't able to do so at the end of the previous month; if we're not too worried about competition, we sow a bed or two of green beans to replace those under chassis; we sow spinach and chervil, to replace those that have gone to seed; We sow all types of lettuce, except the little black one, which is no longer in season; we also sow the three types of romaine, but the green one will end with the month; we will only sow the blond and gray ones, which the escarole and chicory will make disappear in their turn in September; we sow the cardoons on a piece of bed to be replanted, or we sow them immediately in place. Celery is best sown on a piece of bedding, because it comes on faster and can be transplanted more quickly than that sown in the ground; however, small celery for cutting can be sown in the ground. Cauliflowers, whether bedded or planted in the ground, require a lot of watering and insect control. Pumpkins, giraumonts, cucumbers and gherkins sown on beds last month are planted permanently. Last year's pear, chard and parsley go to seed, and are replaced by new seedlings if this has not already been done. We sow pink radishes every ten days, black radishes for winter, carrots and parsnips to be delivered for consumption as they grow. Pruning melons takes up a good part of the market gardener's time, in this month and the next. This month sees the sale of red and grey lettuces, blond and grey romaine, cauliflower, melon, cucumber, chicory, York sprouts, sugar loaf, O8 beef hearts, green beans, radishes, turnips, parsley, chervil, spinach, sorrel, tarragon, burnet, watercress, carrot, chard, leek, white onion, spring onion. JUNE. In this month, as in the two preceding ones, the vegetable gardener must always remember how long it takes for this or that seed to sprout, how long it takes for the seedling to be ready for planting, and how long it takes for the vegetable to be ready for consumption, so that he always sows on time, and is not exposed to losing a season, or to seeing his land unoccupied. A few melons can still be planted in the first days of this month, if the plant is in good condition; hard and half-hard cauliflowers are sown to give their apples in autumn; those sown in May are planted; pear chard is also sown; romaine and the season's lettuces are sown. lettuces of the season, a few at a time, but every ten or twelve days; plant the cardoons sown on a piece of bed the previous month; you can even sow them in place if you haven't already done so; sow the Brussels sprouts, the Savoy cabbage fr. Brussels sprouts, curly Savoy cabbage and others are sown for the winter; we start transplanting celery, celery turcet and celery raye. Melons (I) are still taking up part of the market gardener's time this month. Supplies such as golden purslane, chervil and lady's mantle rise quickly in this season, and must be sown very often. Purslane and lady's mantle don't mind the sun, but chervil needs shade; the first escarole is sown this month. Much of the time is taken up with watering, without which no harvest can be expected. This is the month in which the frames and bells are omitted from the layers; the frames are stacked in a shed sheltered from the rain; the bells are stacked and set out in a corner of the marsh sheltered from shocks (see page 78). This month's produce includes cauliflower, York, painrde-sucre and cœur-de-bœuf cabbages, melons, cucumbers, green beans, ton ates, eggplants, cress alénois, cerfenil, parsley, salad burnet, sorrel, golden purslane, white onion, spring onion, chives, turnips and radishes, first celery for cutting, bonne-dame. JULY. No less foresight is needed in this month? than in the previous ones, since we have more or less the same sowing and planting to do, except for green romaine, which has given way to escarole. In the first few days of the month, celery continues to be transplanted; hard and semi-hard cauliflowers continue to be planted for the autumn; kale, Brussels sprouts, red cabbage and other types of cabbage are planted for consumption over the winter; chicory, escarole, turnips, radishes, chervil, maidenhair and anything else that can ripen before winter continue to be sown. As the melon trenches are emptied, the manure is removed from the trenches, and the trenches are filled with the soil from the trenches. The trenches are then ploughed and planted with autumn cauliflower, semi-fine chicory, Rouennaise chicory, Meaux chicory, escarole and other seasonal vegetables. Watering is usually even more frequent and more essential than in the previous month, and you can't expect to get beautiful cauliflowers or celery if they aren't abundantly watered. In this last month of the horticultural year, the marshes offer an abundance of melons, cucumbers, gherkins, fine and semi-fine chicories, blond and gray romaine, gray and red lettuces, sweetbread and beef cabbage, summer cauliflower, carrots, tomatoes, eggplant, parsnips, leeks, cut celery, pear, radish, parsley, sorrel, belle-dame, and all the season's supplies.
